Average-Case Reductions for k k -XOR and Tensor PCA
Guy Bresler, Alina Harbuzova

Abstract
We study two canonical planted average-case problems – noisy k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} and Tensor PCA – and relate their computational properties via poly-time average-case reductions. For a { ± 1 } \{\pm 1\} signal vector x x , both models are noisy observations of the rank- 1 1 tensor x ⊗ k x^{\otimes k} : in k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} we observe m ≈ n k / 2 m\approx n^{k/2} entries each independently flipped with probability 1 / 2 − δ 1/2-\delta ; in Tensor PCA we observe all ≈ n k \approx n^{k} entries with additive Gaussian noise, and the mean is δ ​ x ⊗ k \delta x^{\otimes k} , but typically for a much smaller signal level δ \delta compared to that of k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} . We study a family of problems that interpolates between k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} and Tensor PCA, allowing intermediate densities n k / 2 ≤ m ≤ n k n^{k/2}\leq m\leq n^{k} and signal levels δ \delta . We give several reductions, all achieving tight parameter tradeoffs: they preserve proximity to the conjectured computational threshold m ​ δ 2 ≍ n k / 2 m\delta^{2}\asymp n^{k/2} , yielding new hardness results for all conjectured-hard instances. We introduce two densifying reductions that increase the number of observed entries while controlling the decrease in signal, and, in particular, reduce any k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} instance at the computational threshold (i.e., m = n k ⁡ ( 1 + ε ) / 2 m=n^{k(1+\varepsilon)/2} entries and δ ≈ n − k ε / 4 \delta\approx n^{-k\varepsilon/4} ) to Tensor PCA at the computational threshold ( m = n k m=n^{k} and δ ≈ n − k / 4 \delta\approx n^{-k/4} ). Additionally, we give new order-reducing maps (e.g., 5 → 4 5\to 4 k ​ - ​ 𝖷𝖮𝖱 k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR} and 7 → 4 7\to 4 Tensor PCA) at fixed entry density. Our reductions are built on an equation combination primitive – multiplying pairs of entries – analyzed in both the discrete and Gaussian settings. Using a computational equivalence between these formulations, we freely pass between them, using whichever yields stronger reductions at the given parameter. For the Gaussian setting correctness of our reduction rests on a new high-dimensional CLT for Gramians of sparse Gaussian matrices. In the discrete setting, we design a dependence-avoiding equation combination procedure. Our framework extends beyond the binary case, yielding analogous reductions for k k -sparse 𝖫𝖶𝖤 \mathsf{LWE} over 𝔽 q \mathbb{F}_{q} . Taken together, these results establish a hardness partial order of discrete and Gaussian problem variants in the space of tensor order and entry density, advancing a unified study of planted tensor models and relationships between them. This unified perspective and our results lead us to formulate numerous open problems.



1
Introduction

This paper relates two central planted average-case problems –
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA – via new average-case reductions that trade off the number of observations against entrywise noise.

The two canonical problems.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
(also called
k\mathsf{\text{-}LIN}
), there is a secret
signal vector

x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
. We observe
m
samples
\left(\alpha,\mathsf{Y}\right)
, where for each sample
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is a uniformly random
1
1

1

Our
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
model assumes that the
m
index sets
\alpha
are sampled independently
with replacement
; other sampling procedures for
\alpha
are explored in
§​​ A.2
and
§​​ A.5
.

k
-subset of
\left[n\right]
and

\mathsf{Y}=\begin{cases}x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}&\text{ w. prob. }1/2+\delta/2\,,\\
-x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(1)

Via the natural
\mathbb{F}_{2}\leftrightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
map, each sample is a noisy linear equation
y=\langle a,s\rangle+e
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
where
a
has
k
-sparse support,
s_{i}\in\{0,1\}
with
x_{i}=(-1)^{s_{i}}
, and
e\sim\mathsf{Bern}(1/2-\delta/2)
.
Two fundamental
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
tasks are
detection
(distinguish these samples from pure noise) and
recovery
(estimate the secret vector
x
). The most extensively studied regime (which we will henceforth call the
canonical regime
) has
\delta=\Theta(1)
(e.g.,
1/3
), in which case poly-time algorithms are known for
m=\widetilde{\Omega}(n^{k/2})
.
As discussed in
§​​ 1.2
, both detection and recovery are believed to be
computationally hard
(i.e., there exists no polynomial-time algorithm) whenever
m\ll n^{k/2}
(or, more generally,
m\delta^{2}\ll n^{k/2}
) and such hardness assumptions have found applications, e.g., in cryptography
[
43
,
6
,
50
,
1
,
33
,
66
,
27
,
32
,
74
]
.
Beyond cryptography,
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
is a
canonical problem in average-case complexity that is of interest in its own right
[
36
,
6
,
37
,
23
,
8
,
73
,
54
,
41
,
40
]
.
Hardness of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(equivalently,
k
-sparse LPN) has also been instrumental in deriving hardness results for statistical tasks: agnostically learning halfspaces

[
29
]
, learning DNFs
[
34
,
14
]
, and noisy tensor completion
[
13
]
.

In the canonical statistical model for Tensor PCA
[
71
]
, denoted
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
, there is (again) a secret
signal vector

x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, and we observe an order-
k
tensor

\mathsf{Y}=\delta x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}\,,

(2)

where
\mathsf{G}
is a symmetric tensor of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Entry
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}}
of this tensor has the form
\delta x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}+G_{\left\{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k}\right\}}
.
The
detection
and
recovery
problems for Tensor PCA have become central to the fields of high-dimensional statistics
[
60
,
3
,
26
,
4
,
49
,
61
,
31
]
and statistical physics
[
7
,
70
,
78
,
63
,
15
,
35
]
and tensor PCA has also been intensely studied as a prototypical average-case planted problem
[
46
,
17
,
45
,
58
,
10
,
56
]
.
As discussed in
§​​ 1.2
, for
\delta\gg n^{-k/4}
there are polynomial-time algorithms and below this level, i.e.,
\delta\ll n^{-k/4}
(equiv.
m\delta^{2}\ll n^{k/2}
, where
m=n^{k}
), both tasks are believed to be computationally hard.

The models (
1
) and (
2
) are in some respects very different, and each has largely been studied in isolation.
Nevertheless, observe that the signal
x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\cdots x_{i_{k}}
in each entry has the same form.
How closely related are these problems? As one possible goal, can one be algorithmically reduced to the other, thereby transferring algorithms and hardness conjectures between the two?

A new connection.

Canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
has relatively few low-noise equations, while Tensor PCA has many more but highly noisy equations.
In
§​​ 1.1
we identify a
continuum of problems
interpolating between
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA, and give
poly-time average-case reductions
along it: this continuum includes
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
in Eq. (
1
) for
m
in the
\left[n^{k/2},n^{k}\right]
range.
As one consequence, in Thm.
1.1
we reduce
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
with
m\approx n^{k/2}
and
\delta\approx 1
to Tensor PCA
(roughly,
m=n^{k}
and
\delta\approx n^{-k/4}
); in essence, the conjectured
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
computational threshold implies the believed threshold for Tensor PCA.
In general, all our reductions map one conjectured-hard instance to another and have tight parameter tradeoffs: they preserve proximity to the computational threshold
m\delta^{2}\asymp n^{k/2}
, yielding hardness results for all conjectured-hard instances.
2
2

2

In fact, for regimes where poly-time algorithms are known, most of our reductions also transfer success of the algorithms (with up to a
\mathrm{poly}\log n
loss in parameters).

Beyond hardness implications, our reductions show a fundamental connection between problems.

Theorem 1.1

(Corollary of Thm.
1.4
)
.

Fix constants
k^{\prime}\geq 2,\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, and
\eta>0
. For some sufficiently large
k=k(k^{\prime},\varepsilon)
, if there is
no
polynomial-time algorithm solving detection (resp. recovery) for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta^{\mathsf{xor}})
with

\delta^{\mathsf{xor}}=n^{-k\varepsilon/4-\eta/2}\text{ and }m=n^{k(1+\varepsilon)/2}\,,

then there is
no
polynomial-time algorithm solving detection (resp. recovery) for
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta^{\mathsf{tpca}})
and signal level

\delta^{\mathsf{tpca}}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta}\big)\,.

Our reductions are the first to trade off the signal strength
\delta
against the number of equations
m
.
Prior work giving reductions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
has instead considered search to decision reductions
[
9
,
25
]
, maps from dense to sparse coefficient vectors (i.e.,
k=n\to O(1)
)
[
13
]
, etc.

As noted above, beyond this example, we give a broad family of reductions along the
(m,\delta)
curve (
§​​ 1.3
).
We also obtain reductions that
change the tensor order
k
:

Theorem 1.2

(Corollary of Thm.
1.3
)
.

Let
P,Q
be either of the following pairs:

1.

P
is
5\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{7/2}
and
\delta=n^{-\eta/2}
(for any fixed
\eta>0
), and
Q
is
4\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
with
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{2}\big)
and
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{-\eta}\big)
;

2.

P
is
7\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
with
\delta=n^{-7/4-\eta/2}
(for any fixed
\eta>0
), and
Q
is
4\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta^{\prime})
with
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{-1-\eta}\big)
.

If there is
no
polynomial-time algorithm solving detection (resp. recovery) for
P
, then there is
no
polynomial-time algorithm solving detection (resp. recovery) for
Q
.

Technique.

Our reductions are built around an
equation combination
primitive, which combines two input equations into a new
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
equation on the symmetric-difference support, implemented by multiplying two values in Eq. (
1
) (equiv. adding two equations over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
), see
§​​ 2
for the overview of main reduction ideas. Crucially, since we work in the average case, our reductions must map to the correct (approximately in total variation) output distribution. The same framework extends beyond binary equations, yielding analogous reductions for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
over larger fields
\mathbb{F}_{q}
(
§​​ 8
).

1.1
A family of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
problems

We are primarily motivated by the canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA problems, but a key insight is that it is useful to view them as endpoints of a
continuum
of planted models, which provides a natural domain for our reductions. Apart from having Gaussian versus discrete noise distributions, the key differences between canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(
1
) and
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}
(
2
) are (1) the number of observed entries (noisy equations)
m
and (2) the entrywise expectation
\delta
measuring signal strength. Canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
has
\left(m,\delta\right)\approx(n^{k/2},1)
;
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}
has
\left(m,\delta\right)\approx(n^{k},n^{-k/4})
. The
continuum
of both discrete and Gaussian problems that captures both settings is defined below.

Definition 1.1

(Family of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
Models)
.

Let
k,n,m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\delta\in\left[0,1\right]
be parameters. Draw the signal vector
x\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
. Let
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
be the set of
k
-sets of distinct indices in
\left[n\right]
and for a set
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
write

x_{\alpha}=x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{2}}\dots x_{i_{k}}\,.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
, we generate a collection of pairs
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
as follows. For each
j\in\left[m\right]
, sample
\alpha_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
(with replacement) and set

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=x_{\alpha_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\quad\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
)

where
\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
3
3

3

We define
\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
as the
\pm 1
random variable such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta\right)\right]=\delta
.
is
entrywise noise
.

We define a Gaussian analog
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
where we replace the multiplicative Rademacher noise
\mathsf{W}_{\alpha_{j}}
in (

⁢
k
-
XOR

) with additive Gaussian
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
: we observe
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
for
\left\{\alpha_{j}\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
sampled as above and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta x_{\alpha_{j}}+\mathsf{G}_{j}\quad\in\mathbb{R}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)

where
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Remark 1.1

(Polynomial Scale)
.

We treat
k
as a constant, consider sequences of problems indexed by
n\to\infty
, and work at polynomial scales:
\log_{n}m
, and
\log_{n}\delta
are viewed as constants.
As is made precise in
§​​ C
(Prop.
C.3
), two model sequences with parameters
\delta_{n},p_{n}
and
\delta^{\prime}_{n},p^{\prime}_{n}
such that
\lim_{n}\log_{n}\delta_{n}=\lim_{n}\log_{n}\delta_{n}^{\prime}
and
\lim_{n}\log_{n}m_{n}=\lim_{n}\log_{n}m_{n}^{\prime}

are equivalent for our purposes.

The following proposition (see
§​​ 5.1.1
) shows that discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models are, in fact,
computationally equivalent
up to
\mathrm{poly}\log(n)
factors in the parameters and hence
interchangeable for our purposes
.

Proposition 1.1

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)
.

For any
k,n,m,\delta
, models
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
(Eq. (

⁢
k
-
XOR

)) and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
(Eq. (

⁢
k
-
Gauss

)) are
computationally equivalent
. In particular, there is a poly-time reduction algorithm that preserves signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta^{\prime})
for some
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta)
and vice versa.
4
4

4

\widetilde{\Theta}
hides
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors.

As a consequence, poly-time detection/recovery algorithms are transferred between the problems.

While the discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
in Def.
1.1
with
m=n^{k/2},\delta=\Theta(1)
precisely captures the canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, we require several cosmetic reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
and the standard form in Eq.
2
in (Cor.
5.1
,
§​​ 5.1.4
):

Proposition 1.2

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
and Canonical Tensor PCA)
.

For any
k,n,
and
\delta\ll n^{-k/4}
,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
is computationally equivalent to the Tensor PCA model
\mathsf{Y}=\delta x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}
of Eq. (
2
) with only distinct-index entries observed.
A
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
model variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
(defined in
§​​ 5.1
) that allows index repetitions in observed equations is computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
as in Eq. (
2
).

Moreover, all our results hold verbatim for
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
in place of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
.

Definition 1.2

(Density Parameter
t
)
.

The number of observed entries in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
is
m
; we focus on the
\Theta(n^{k/2})\leq m\leq\Theta(n^{k})
regime, with the two endpoints corresponding to canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA. Accordingly, we parametrize

m=n^{k(t+1)/2}\quad\text{for }t\in[0,1]\,.

Canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
has
m=n^{k/2}
, and hence
t=0
, and Tensor PCA has
m=n^{k}
, and hence
t=1
.
5
5

5

Models differing by
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors in parameters
m,\delta
are equivalent for our purposes, see
§​​ C
.

1.2
Complexity Profiles

Tasks.

We formalize the following detection and recovery tasks in
§​​ C
:

1.

Detection:
distinguish an instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
) from a null (pure noise) instance (see Remark
1.2
below) that does not depend on the signal vector
x
;

2.

Recovery:
given a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
) instance, estimate the secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, either exactly or approximately.

Remark 1.2

(Choice of Null Distribution)
.

For
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
we define the null distribution by replacing the structured signal
x_{\alpha_{j}}
in equation
j
with a random sign
w_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\big(\left\{\pm 1\right\}\big)
.
A common alternative is to set the SNR parameter
\delta=0
. These coincide exactly for all discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
; for Tensor PCA
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta\ll n^{-k/4})
, the two versions are statistically indistinguishable (i.e., their total variation is
o(1)
, see Prop.
C.1
).

Our results address both the detection and recovery tasks. The separate
refutation
task (certifying an upper bound on the satisfiable fraction under the null) is not our focus; our reductions do not directly apply to it and we leave it as an interesting direction for future research. We next summarize known computational results for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
; an extended overview is in
§​​ 4
.

Computational Threshold.

We defer an enumeration of known algorithms to
§​​ 4.2
.
For canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
with
\delta=\Theta(1)
, state-of-the-art polynomial-time algorithms succeed when
m\gg n^{k/2}
. For Tensor PCA (
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
), the best polynomial-time algorithms succeed when
\delta\gg n^{-k/4}
.
For intermediate densities
t\in\left(0,1\right)
(recall
m=n^{k(t+1)/2}
), known spectral algorithms for detection
[
78
]
extend naturally and succeed whenever
m\delta^{2}\gg n^{k/2}
, and our reductions yield recovery algorithms for all
t\in\left(0,1\right)
at the same threshold for sufficiently large order
k=k(t)
(
§​​ 3
). We refer to the boundary of the computationally feasible regime,
m\delta^{2}\gg n^{k/2}
, as the
computational threshold
; below it, no poly-time algorithms are known:

\qquad\qquad m\delta^{2}\asymp n^{k/2}\quad\text{or equivalently},\quad\delta\asymp n^{-kt/4}\,,

(Comp. Thr.)

using the parametrization
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
(see Def.
1.2
).

We encode distance to the computational threshold via the
SNR deficiency
parameter
\eta
:

\qquad\qquad\qquad\eta\coloneq\log_{n}\frac{n^{-kt/2}}{\delta^{2}}=\log_{n}\frac{n^{k/2}}{m\delta^{2}}\,,

(
SNR deficiency
)

which we take to be constant. Efficiently solvable instances have
\eta<0
and conversely there are no known efficient algorithms for
\eta>0
. We will often parameterize in terms of
t
and
\eta
.

Definition 1.3

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
Family, Reparametrized)
.

For
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(resp.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
) to denote the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
(resp.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
) (Def.
1.1
) with

m=n^{k(t+1)/2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}\,.

Information Theoretic Lower Bounds.

For canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
with
\delta=\Theta(1)
, it is folklore that detection is information-theoretically impossible when
m\ll n
. For Tensor PCA (
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m=n^{k},\delta)
), the IT threshold is at
\delta\ll n^{-(k-1)/2}

[
71
,
62
,
69
]
. For intermediate regimes
t\in\left(0,1\right)
, the computation of
[
69
]
naturally extends and yields the
information-theoretic threshold

m\delta^{2}\ll n\quad\text{or equivalently},\quad\delta\ll n^{-kt/4-(k-2)/4}\,.

(IT Thr.)

Computational-Statistical Gap.

The discrepancy in the signal strength required to solve the problem
efficiently
(
Comp. Thr.
) and
information-theoretically
(
IT Thr.
) leaves a wide regime

n\ll m\delta^{2}\ll n^{k/2}\quad{(\text{equiv. }n^{-kt/4-(k-2)/4}\ll\delta\ll n^{-kt/4})}

(3)

with no known poly-time algorithms and is known as
computational-statistical gap
. For the two endpoints at
t\in\{0,1\}
that, as discussed earlier, correspond to the extremely well-studied
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}
, a line of work described in
§​​ 4.3

provides evidence for the computational-statistical gap in (
3
).

We believe that the analysis based on restricted classes of algorithms extends to intermediate
t\in(0,1)
.
We summarize the believed hardness in the following conjectures:

Believed Hardness 1.1

(Computational Hardness of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
at Density
t
:
\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)
)
.

Fix parameters
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1]
.

\displaystyle\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right):\text{ There exists no polynomial-time algorithm solving the {detection task}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right):\text{ There exists no polynomial-time algorithm solving the {recovery task}}

for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
with any
\eta>0
(see Def.
1.3
).

Note that by Prop.
1.1
, the Gaussian versions
\mathsf{CONJ}_{\mathsf{tpca}}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)
and
\mathsf{CONJ}_{\mathsf{tpca}}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
are equivalent to their discrete analogs:

\mathsf{CONJ}_{\mathsf{tpca}}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{CONJ}_{\mathsf{tpca}}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)\Leftrightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)\,.

1.3
Main Results: Hardness Partial Order

We study the computational complexities of the models
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(Def.
1.1
,
1.3
) in the regime with no known efficient algorithms (
\eta>0
). We do so by building
average-case reductions in total variation
between instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
with different
k,n,t,\eta
parameters and consider the algorithmic and hardness implications of these reductions.

1.3.1
Average-Case Reductions and Their Implications for Hardness

An
average-case reduction in total variation
(defined formally in
§​​ C
) is a (poly-time) algorithm (or Markov kernel) sending instances of one average-case problem to another, while preserving the target distribution up to
o(1)
total variation error.

A key consequence of these reductions (Lem.
C.3
) is that computational complexities are transferred: if an
O(n^{c})
-time average-case reduction from one model to another (e.g. from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
) is proved, then any
O\big((n^{\prime})^{t}\big)
-time algorithm for the latter model (
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
) yields an
O\big((n^{\prime})^{t}+n^{c}\big)
-time algorithm for the first model (
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
).
6
6

6

Since we sometimes map to approximate target values
t^{\prime}+o(1)
and
\eta^{\prime}+o(1)
(Def.
C.6
), the algorithm for the target instance is assumed to succeed in a small ball around
(t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})
, see Lem.
C.3
. This does not affect the hardness conjecture implications.

As a consequence, our reductions relate the hardness conjectures
\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
problems and establish a
hardness partial order
on the parameter pairs
(k,t)
:

Definition 1.4

(Hardness Partial Order
\Rightarrow
)
.

We write
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)
to denote that the hardness conjecture
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)
implies hardness conjecture
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)
. Similarly
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)
conveys the analogous implication.
We write
\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)
if both detection and recovery implications hold.

Relating detection to recovery, e.g., showing
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)
, is an important question that we do not address. (The implication
\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)
in the opposite direction is easy to show.)
Our reductions keep the task the same and change
(k,t)
.

Proposition
C.2
.

(Implications of Average-Case Reductions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
Hardness Conjectures).
Fix
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. If for every
\eta^{\prime}>0
there exists
\eta>0
and a poly-time average-case reduction (Def.
C.6
) from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
with
n^{\prime}=\mathrm{poly}(n)
, then

\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)\,.

Remark 1.3
.

Some of our reductions nontrivially alter the signal vector
x
. We formalize the weak/strong/exact recovery tasks (Def.
C.7
,
C.8
) in
§​​ C.4
and show that all of them transfer.

decrease
the tensor order
k
at fixed density

(Thm.
1.3
), and (2)
densify

t
and trading off against the tensor order

1.3.3
Our Results:
k
-sparse LWE

In
§​​ 8
we extend our reductions to the
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
model (
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
), which was first formalized in
[
53
]
. While
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
can be viewed as noisy linear equations over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
,
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
consists of noisy linear equations over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
with
k
-sparse supports (for constant
k
). Let
\chi
be a probability distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
In
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi)}
(Def.
8.1
) one observes
m
i.i.d. samples

\mathsf{Y}=a_{1}x_{i_{1}}+a_{2}x_{i_{2}}+\dots+a_{k}x_{i_{k}}+e\,,

(5)

where
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is the secret,
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is a uniformly random index set,
e\sim\chi\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is i.i.d. noise, and the coefficients
a_{s}\sim_{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0\right)
.

Theorem 1.6

(Reductions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
; Informal)
.

Let
\left\{\chi_{\theta}\right\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
be a
combination-stable
noise family
9
9

9

Informally, the distribution of a difference of random variables remains in the family, see Def.
8.2
.
. Then, for a wide range of parameters, there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}

for
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
and
(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=f(k,m,\theta)
for explicit parameter maps
f
stated in
§​​ 8
. The secret in the output instance is the restriction
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

In particular, the following noise families are combination-stable (Def.
8.2
):

•

Uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{\delta}
if
e=\begin{cases}0,&\text{w. prob. }\delta\\
\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right),&\text{w. prob. }1-\delta\,.\end{cases}

•

Discrete Gaussian Noise:

\chi_{s}(e)\propto\sum_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi(e+tq)^{2}/s^{2}}\,.

•

Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{l}
,
where
\chi_{l}
is a given measure on
\left\{-l,l\right\}
for a parameter
l\leq q
.

Our reductions prove a new connection between different parameter values and, combined with a simple collision-based algorithm for detection (
§​​ 8.2
), yield new algorithms that outperform the collision-based algorithm.
However, the complexity profile of
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
remains largely underexplored. See
§​​ 3
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
open problems and
§​​ 8
for our results.

2
Main Reduction Ideas

We obtain our hardness implications (Theorems
1.3
,
1.4
,
1.5
) via
average-case reductions
: efficient algorithms
that transform an instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to an instance of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
while preserving the planted structure and the distributions.
10
10

10

Up to
o(1)
error in total variation.
With our parametrization, this means

mapping

\displaystyle k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)\quad\text{with }m=n^{k(t+1)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}

to

\displaystyle k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\quad\text{with }m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big((n^{\prime})^{k^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+1)/2}\big),\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\big((n^{\prime})^{-k^{\prime}t^{\prime}/4-\eta^{\prime}/2}\big)\,.

See
§​​ 1.3.1
for a high-level overview and
§​​ C
for formal definitions and complexity implications.

2.1
Equation Combination Identity, Naïve Reduction, and Challenges

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
with a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, there are
m=n^{k(t+1)/2}
samples
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
with independent index sets
\alpha_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\big[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\big]
and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=x_{\alpha_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\,,\qquad\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)\,,

where we denote
x_{\alpha}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}
for an index set
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
.

Now observe that for any two distinct samples
\left(\alpha_{s},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\right)
and
\left(\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
with
s,t\in\left[m\right]
, the product

\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}=x_{\alpha_{s}}x_{\alpha_{t}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{s}\mathsf{W}_{t}=x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\cdot{\left(\mathsf{W}_{s}\mathsf{W}_{t}\right)}\sim x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta^{2})\,,

(6)

where
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
denotes the symmetric difference and recall that
\mathsf{Rad}(\mu)
is the distribution of a
\pm 1
random variable with mean
\mu
. Let
k^{\prime}\coloneq|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|
; then
\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
is distributed as a
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
sample with signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
. We call the operation of multiplying two
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entries to obtain another
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry
equation combination
(
\mathsf{EqComb}
).
11
11

11

In logic and SAT solvers similar combination procedures, termed
resolution
, have a long history
[
72
,
22
]
.

Eq. (
6
) suggests building a new
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instance by taking products
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
over all available pairs
(s,t)
with overlap size
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
. There are two qualitatively different regimes, depending on how many pairs
(s,t)
map to the same output index set
\gamma=\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
. It turns out that if
2kt\leq k^{\prime}
, then there are typically
O(1)
equations created for each index set
\gamma
: a natural reduction is to simply return this collection of equations.

1.

Sparse case – few pairs
(s,t)
:
for all
s,t\in\left[m\right]
satisfying
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
, include entry
\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
into the output instance
\mathcal{Z}
:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right):\,s,t\in\left[m\right]\text{ and }|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}\right\}\,.

(Sparse
\mathsf{EqComb}
)

However, if
2kt>k^{\prime}
, the individual equations that are produced are too noisy: each one has bias only
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}\approx n^{-kt/2}\ll n^{-k^{\prime}/4}
(for
\eta\approx 0
). On the other hand, in this regime we expect
\approx n^{k-k^{\prime}/2}\cdot(m/n^{k})^{2}=n^{kt-k^{\prime}/2}
created equations
12
12

12

Since there are
\approx n^{k-k^{\prime}/2}
candidate pairs for each
\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
and probability to observe one is
\approx(m/n^{k})^{2}
.
for each index set
\gamma
, so we can
aggregate
these to boost the signal strength.

2.

Dense case – many pairs
(s,t)
:
for each
\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
collect
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
with
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma
into

\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{obs}}=\left\{\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}:s,t\in[m]\text{ and }\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma\right\}

(7)

and aggregate them via a majority vote, outputting a collection of equations

\mathcal{Z}=\Big\{\big(\gamma,\underbrace{\mathsf{MAJ}(\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{obs}})}_{\coloneq\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}}\big):\,\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\text{ and }|\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}|\neq 0\Big\}\,.

(Dense
\mathsf{EqComb}
)

The aggregation in (
Dense

EqComb

) boosts the signal level in
\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}
equations to
\delta^{\prime}\approx\delta^{2}\cdot\sqrt{|\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{obs}}|}
, which turns out to be precisely the signal level needed to trace the computational threshold.

In both cases, the
marginal distribution
of individual equations in
\mathcal{Z}
as well as their total number matches
13
13

13

Up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors in parameters
m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}
that of a
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n)
instance with the same secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
and

\eta^{\prime}=2\eta\quad\text{and}\quad t^{\prime}=\min\Big\{1,\frac{2kt}{k^{\prime}}\Big\}\,.

This change in parameters
(t,\eta)
traces the computational threshold (
Comp. Thr.
), mapping to an arbitrarily small
\eta^{\prime}>0
from an appropriate input deficiency
\eta=\eta^{\prime}/2
.

However, several obstacles prevent (
Sparse

EqComb

) and (
Dense

EqComb

) from being valid reductions: reusing the same input equations introduces
noise dependence
across output equations, and (in both regimes) the resulting equation index sets do not match the target support distribution. Moreover, for any fixed input density
t
, the naïve combination map only reaches a finite set of attainable target densities
t^{\prime}
, which is far weaker than the continuum of implications in Thm.
1.4
.

We make equation combination into a valid reduction using two techniques:
discrete equation combination
(
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
) and
Gaussian equation combination
(
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
). In
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
(
§​​ 2.2
), we avoid entry reuse by design, which ensures noise and index set independence across output samples.
In
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
(
§​​ 2.4
), we work in the
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
model (computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
) and replace the majority vote in (
Dense

EqComb

) by normalized average of products
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
. Although this introduces noise dependence, the entire aggregation step admits a clean matrix/tensor representation (a Gram-type object built from a sparse Gaussian matrix). This viewpoint lets us control the dependence via a new high-dimensional CLT, yielding an output instance that is close (in total variation) to one with i.i.d. entries.

A crucial ingredient is the intermediate model
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
(see

§​​ 2.3
). It is needed for the order-reduction subroutine used in the integer-approximation step that removes the discretization in attainable
t^{\prime}

(
§​​ 2.2.3
), and at density
t=1
it provides a connection to standard Tensor PCA.

2.2
Discrete Equation Combination: Avoiding Dependence by Design

In
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
(full details in
§​​ 7
) we instantiate both (
Sparse

EqComb

) and (
Dense

EqComb

) starting from discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
samples. Here we sketch some of the challenges and their solution.

2.2.1
Distributional Challenges in Discrete Equation Combination

Two issues arise immediately: dependence created by reusing noisy input entries, and a mismatch between the output support distribution and the i.i.d. uniform support of the target model.

Challenge 1: Noise dependence from entry reuse.

A single noisy observation
\mathsf{Y}_{s}
can participate in many products
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
(across different
\gamma=\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
), which introduces entry noise dependence in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Challenge 2: Index-set clustering in the sparse regime and support restriction.

\displaystyle\big\{\overbrace{i_{1},\dots,i_{k^{\prime}/2},\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}i_{k^{\prime}/2+1}\dots,i_{k}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\rho}}}^{\alpha_{s}}\big\}\triangle\big\{\overbrace{\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}i_{k^{\prime}/2+1}\dots,i_{k}}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\rho}},i_{k+1},\dots,i_{k+k^{\prime}/2}}^{\alpha_{t}}\big\}=\big\{\overbrace{i_{1},\dots,i_{k^{\prime}/2},i_{k+1},\dots,i_{k+k^{\prime}/2}}^{\gamma}\big\}

For a pair
\alpha_{s},\alpha_{t}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
with
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
, let
\rho=\alpha_{s}\cap\alpha_{t}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
.
For fixed
\rho
, denote

\mathcal{I}_{\rho}=\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\rho\subseteq\alpha_{j}\right\}\,.

Then, for most
s,t\in\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
, we include
\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
into the output
\mathcal{Z}
.
In (
Sparse

EqComb

) this means the locations of the samples in
\mathcal{Z}
cluster within each
\rho
-block, unlike the i.i.d. uniform index sets of the target model. Moreover, each produced index set
\gamma=\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
is constrained to be disjoint from its originating
\rho
.

Idea: Avoiding entry reuse by design.

For
\rho\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}=\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\rho\subseteq\alpha_{j}\right\}
, all pairs
s,t\in\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
with
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
yield candidate samples
\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
.
If
k^{\prime}\leq k(1-t)
, then

\mathbb{E}\big|\mathcal{I}_{\rho}\big|\approx\left(\text{num. of }\alpha:\,\rho\subseteq\alpha\right)\cdot\Pr\left[\alpha\text{ is observed}\right]\asymp n^{k^{\prime}/2}\cdot\frac{m}{n^{k}}=n^{k^{\prime}/2}\cdot n^{k(t-1)/2}=O\left(1\right)\,,

(8)

so for any fixed
\rho
w.h.p. there are at most
O(1)
candidate products
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
(
s,t,\in\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
) to consider.

In the regime of (
8
), our
dependence-avoiding

\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
reduction (
§​​ 7
) simply selects
one
disjoint pair of elements
s,t\in\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
at random and discards the rest.
14
14

14

A remaining issue is that the same observation
\mathsf{Y}_{s}
can belong to
\mathcal{I}_{\rho}
for multiple
\rho
, which would reintroduce dependence; see
§​​ 7
.
This removes noise dependence (Challenge 1) by construction, since no observation is reused. Additionally, this eliminates the index-set dependence from the
\rho
-block clustering (Challenge 2). What remains is the structural support restriction
\gamma\cap\rho=\emptyset
: full index-set independence is obtained by splitting
[n]
into dedicated “cancellation indices” (used only to form the
\rho
sets) and the remaining indices, see
§​​ 7
. Achieving a continuum of target
t^{\prime}
is a more challenging problem, discussed next.

2.2.2
Discrete Equation Combination: Overcoming the Discretization in
t^{\prime}

The algorithm in
§​​ 2.2.1
gives an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}(n)\quad\text{with}\quad t^{\prime}=\min\left\{1,\frac{2kt}{k^{\prime}}\right\}\,,

valid for all even
k^{\prime}\leq k(1-t)
(Lem.
7.1
). The discrete nature of the map
t\to\frac{2kt}{k^{\prime}}
prevents hardness implications for other target densities
t^{\prime}
, which we need for Theorem
1.4
.

Challenge: Discrete nature of the parameter map.
The reduction above is constrained to output
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
with density
t^{\prime}
for

t^{\prime}\in\left\{1,\frac{2kt}{k_{\min}},\frac{2kt}{k_{\min}+2},\dots,\frac{2kt}{k_{\max}}\right\}\subset\Big(\frac{2t}{1-t},1\Big]\,,

(9)

for even
k_{\min},k_{\max}
satisfying
2kt<k_{\min}\leq k_{\max}<k(1-t)
. The complexity implications of the hardness assumption
\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)
are thus limited to the finite set of
\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)
above.

Idea 1: Naïve reduction to adjust
t^{\prime}
.
A trivial post-processing reduction (Lem.
5.3
) can change parameter
t^{\prime}
by either (1) inserting pure noise
\mathsf{Rad}(0)
entries into the instance or (2) removing some of the observed entries at random. This achieves the transformation

k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{1},2\eta}(n)\to k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{2},\eta^{\prime}}(n),\quad\text{where}\quad\eta^{\prime}=2\eta+{k^{\prime}|t_{1}-t_{2}|/2}\,.

(10)

Naïvely composing this with the equation combination step gives deficiency
\eta^{\prime}=2\eta+k^{\prime}\cdot\Delta t/2
, where
\Delta t
is the necessary density adjustment. The discrete set of attainable
t^{\prime}=\min\left\{1,\frac{2kt}{k^{\prime}}\right\}
has spacing at least
\frac{4t}{k-2}=\frac{2kt}{k-2}-\frac{2kt}{k}
, yielding
\eta^{\prime}-2\eta\geq 2kt\cdot\frac{t}{k-2}>2t^{2}
. Thus, for a fixed input density
t>0
, this fails to cover the entire regime of interest
\eta^{\prime}>0
.

Crucially, this lower bound
\eta^{\prime}>2t^{2}
is independent of
k
. Hence, with this analysis, even the stronger
\mathsf{CONJ}_{t}
(i.e.,
\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)
for all
k\geq 3
) does
not
imply
\mathsf{CONJ}_{t^{\prime}}
for all
t^{\prime}\in\big(\frac{2t}{1-t},1\big]
. In contrast, Thm.
1.4
fills these gaps and shows

\mathsf{CONJ}_{t}\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}_{t^{\prime}}\,,\quad\text{for all }t^{\prime}\in\Big(\frac{2t}{1-t},1\Big]\,.

Idea 2: Choosing an appropriate input order
k
.
In Thm.
1.4
, for any fixed
t\in(0,1/3)
,
k^{\prime}
, and
t^{\prime}\in\big(\frac{2t}{1-t},1\big]
, we construct a reduction

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},3\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{\Theta(1)}\,,

(with the discrete eq. combination of Lem.
7.1
as subroutine) for a carefully chosen
k=k\left(k^{\prime},t,t^{\prime},\eta\right)
.
15
15

15

One can achieve output deficiency
\eta^{\prime}=C\eta
for any
C>2
.

Concretely, we show in Claim
10.1
a Diophantine approximation (i.e., rational approximation with constraints) statement, proved via the theory of continued fractions: for any (arbitrarily small)
c>0
, and for any sufficiently small
\xi=\xi(c)
, there exists
a
k
and

r\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying

\frac{2kt}{rk^{\prime}}\in\left[t^{\prime}\pm\xi\right]\quad\text{and}\quad k\leq c\xi^{-1}\,.

With this choice of
k
, the equation combination reduction to instance of order
K^{\prime}\coloneq rk^{\prime}
gets
16
16

16

K^{\prime}\leq k(1-t)
follows from the
t^{\prime}>\frac{2t}{1-t}
condition and sufficiently large (constant) choice of
k
.
to within
\xi
of
t^{\prime}
and the post-processing in (
10
) allows us to move to
t^{\prime}
with a cost in deficiency of

(rk^{\prime})\cdot\xi/2\leq k\cdot\xi/2\leq c/2\leq\eta

for an appropriate choice of
c\leq 2\eta
. As can be seen from the last display, a weaker result bounding
k\leq c\xi^{-1.01}
would not be sufficient; conversely, a stronger bound
k\leq\xi^{-0.99}
is impossible.
Note that mapping to a multiple
K^{\prime}=rk^{\prime}
of the desired target
k^{\prime}
is necessary; directly mapping to
k^{\prime}

results in the discrete set of possible target densities
2kt/k^{\prime}
in (
9
).

The required final step is to reduce the order by a factor of
r
(Lem.
5.4
), yielding an instance of order
k^{\prime}
with the desired parameters, establishing hardness implications for the entire
\eta^{\prime}>0
range. This step, however, is not immediate – we describe the ideas and challenges in the following
§​​ 2.2.3
and develop the necessary machinery in
§​​ 2.3
.

2.2.3
Decreasing Order by a Factor

As discussed in
§​​ 2.2.2
, the final ingredient of discrete equation combination is an order-reduction step: for an integer factor
r
, we want to map a
K^{\prime}
-order instance
K^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}(n)
with
K^{\prime}=rk^{\prime}
to an instance of the target order
k^{\prime}
at the same density
\tilde{t}
. We first describe a natural change-of-variables idea and then explain the technical machinery needed to make it distributionally correct.

Idea: Change-of-variables.
This idea is related to coefficient-sparsifying reductions for noisy linear equations
[
13
]
and the
k^{\prime}=2
case already appears as a subroutine in the recovery algorithm of
[
40
]
, which did not require mapping to the precise target distribution as we do.

Given an input
K^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}(n)
for
K^{\prime}=rk^{\prime}
, the idea is to group the
K^{\prime}
indices of every equation into
k^{\prime}
blocks of size
r
. For each
r
-tuple
\beta=\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\right)\in\left[n\right]^{r}
, define a new variable
y_{\beta}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{r}}
, so
y\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{r}}
. Then an input sample
\left(\alpha,\mathsf{Y}\right)
with a set
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{K^{\prime}}\right\}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{K}
(in any fixed ordering) satisfies

\mathsf{Y}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{K^{\prime}}}\cdot\mathsf{W}=\Big(\prod_{j=1}^{k^{\prime}}y_{\left(i_{(j-1)r+1},\dots,i_{jr}\right)}\Big)\cdot\mathsf{W}\,,

i.e., is a noisy observation of
y^{\otimes k^{\prime}}
entry. To restore the uniform prior of the planted signal, we sample an independent random sign vector
w\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{r}}\right]
and change the entry signal to
y\odot w
by returning a collection of
\big(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}\big)
for

\tilde{\alpha}=\left\{\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\right),\dots,\left(i_{r(k^{\prime}-1)+1,\dots,rk^{\prime}}\right)\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}=\mathsf{Y}\cdot w_{\tilde{\alpha}}\,.

Challenge: Wrong index-set distribution.
Marginally, each output pair
\big(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\mathsf{Y}}\big)
has the correct label/noise structure for
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}/r}(n^{r})
. The problem is the distribution of the supports
\tilde{\alpha}
: the input index sets
\alpha
do not allow index repetitions, so
r
-tuples in
\tilde{\alpha}
are forced to be disjoint. This mismatch is inherent: allowing overlaps between the
r
-tuples would correspond to an input equation with repeated indices, which the regular
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
model forbids.

Solution: Work in a model that allows repetitions.

We therefore pass to the
\mathsf{FULL}
variant of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, denoted
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
, which samples index
multisets
with repetitions allowed, independently from the natural measure (
§​​ 5.1
,
§​​ A.3
). In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
, the change-of-variables mapping becomes distributionally correct, yielding the order-reduction Lemma
5.4
:

\text{from}\quad K^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}/r}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n^{r})\quad\text{for }K^{\prime}=rk^{\prime}\,.

To use this inside
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
, we must therefore construct an instance of
K^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
(which includes samples with repeated indices) starting from the regular
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
. This is achieved by a separate “composition” step: in
§​​ 5.1.3
, Prop.
5.3
shows how to assemble a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
instance of order
K^{\prime}
from a collection of independent low-order instances:

\Big\{(K^{\prime}-2s)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{s},\tilde{\eta}}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq s\leq\frac{K^{\prime}-1}{2}}\quad\to\quad K^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\,,\qquad\text{where }t_{s}=\min\left\{\frac{K^{\prime}\tilde{t}}{K^{\prime}-2s},1\right\}\,.

Finally, this collection of low-order instances is naturally available in our setting. Recall that discrete combination maps an input
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to order
k^{\star}
with density

t^{\star}=\min\left\{1,\frac{2kt}{k^{\star}}\right\}

for all even
k^{\star}\leq k(1-t)
. Setting
k^{\star}=K^{\prime}-2s
we match the
t_{s}
above. Using initial “instance splitting”(
§​​ 5.2.1
), we run equation combination independently for each
s
to obtain the required family
\Big\{(K^{\prime}-2s)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{s},\tilde{\eta}}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq s\leq\frac{K^{\prime}-1}{2}}
. Prop.
5.3
then assembles these into a single
K\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
instance, after which Lemma
5.4
decreases the order by the factor
r
.

We overview the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
model in the next section, both to explain Prop.
5.3
and to formalize its connection to standard Tensor PCA.

2.3
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
: Repeated Indices and Reductions to Tensor PCA

We introduced the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
model in
§​​ 2.2.3
as an intermediate instance needed for the order-reduction subroutine of
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
. Another reason to introduce
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
is that, at density
t=1
, it is computationally equivalent to the standard Tensor PCA model in Eq. (
2
). This equivalence is what enables our reductions to Tensor PCA starting from a regular
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instance (Thm.
1.4
,
1.5
).

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
and connection to Tensor PCA.

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
(see
§​​ 5.1
,
§​​ A.3
) is a variant of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
that allows index repetitions – it consists of
m
samples
(\alpha,\mathsf{Y})
for independent index
k
-multisets
\alpha\in\left\{n\right\}^{k}
sampled from a natural measure in Def.
A.1
and
\mathsf{Y}\sim x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
. We show in Corollary
5.1
the computational equivalence of

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=1,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{and}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta}(n)\,,

(11)

where recall that
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta}(n)
observes
\delta x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}
with
\mathsf{G}
a symmetric tensor of i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries. Computational equivalence means there exist average-case reductions both ways preserving signal parameter
\delta
up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors: they use our Gaussianization/Discretization subroutines (
§​​ 5.1.1
) and reductions changing sampling of
\alpha
to with or without replacement (
§​​ 5.1.4
).

As we will see next, all our reductions that output
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
can be modified to output
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=1,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
; composing with (
11
) then yields reductions to
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta}(n)
.

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
as a collection of low order instances.
In Prop.
5.3
we show that
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
is computationally equivalent to a collection of independent low-order instances:

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\Big\{(k-2s)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{s},\eta}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq s\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}\,,\qquad\text{where }t_{s}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2s},1\right\}\,.

(12)

Indeed, consider those samples
(\alpha,\mathsf{Y})
of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
for which the index multiset
\alpha
can be expressed as

\alpha=\beta\cup\left\{i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{s},i_{s}\right\}\text{ for }\beta\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-2s}\text{ and }i_{1},\dots,i_{s}\in\left[n\right]\,,

i.e.
\beta
collects the indices of
odd
multiplicity in
\alpha
. Then,

\mathsf{Y}=x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}x_{\beta}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)

is distributed as a lower order
k-2s\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry. Such classification (with additional aggregation step) decomposes the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
instance into the desired collection and the reverse reduction (with a cloning step,
§​​ B.1.1
) composes the instances into one
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
.

Reductions to Tensor PCA.
Similarly to discrete combination in
§​​ 2.2.3
, our reductions from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=1,\eta^{\prime}}(n)
(Thm.
1.5
) work for all even
k^{\prime}\leq\tau k
, where
\tau=\tau(t)
is explicit. To leverage Eq. (
11
) and (
12
), we first apply our splitting reduction (
§​​ 5.2.1
) to obtain
k^{\prime}/2
independent copies of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
. We then run the densifying reduction on these copies to obtain the collection of lower-order instances at orders
k^{\prime},k^{\prime}-2,\dots,2
and densities
t_{s}=\min\left\{\frac{k^{\prime}}{k^{\prime}-2s},1\right\}=1
, assemble them into a single
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=1,\eta^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
instance using (
12
), and finally map to
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta^{\prime}}(n)
via (
11
).

Finally, passing through
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
is what lets us reach odd target orders
k^{\prime}
: the order-reduction step by a factor of
2
applies in the
\mathsf{FULL}
model (as in
§​​ 2.2.3
).

2.4
Gaussian Equation Combination: Gram Matrix

In Gaussian equation combination (
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
) (see
§​​ 9
) we permit entry reuse in (
Dense

EqComb

), which lets us map to
t^{\prime}=1
for a wider parameter range than
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
(
§​​ 2.2
).

Challenge: Strong noise dependence.
When the same noisy
\mathsf{Y}_{s}
is reused across many output
\gamma
, this introduces noise dependence in the output. In the regime of
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
each
\mathsf{Y}_{s}
is expected to contribute to
\gg 1
output entries and this reuse is crucial: it preserves the
signal strength
in the output instance – without it, the reduction maps to a much noisier
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instance, yielding weak (or vacuous) complexity implications.

Idea: Gaussian noise and tensor/matrix viewpoint.

We carry out this reduction for the Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
model and transfer the results to discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
via the equivalence in Prop.
1.1
. The key is to switch to a
tensor/matrix viewpoint
where the dependence created by reuse can be written explicitly and then controlled via a new high-dimensional CLT.

Recall that in
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
(equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
,
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) with a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, there are
m
samples
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
with
\alpha_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta\cdot x_{\alpha_{j}}+\mathsf{G}_{j}\,,

where
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. The product of two
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
entries
\mathsf{Y}_{s},\mathsf{Y}_{t}
for distinct
s,t\in\left[m\right]
with
k^{\prime}=|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|
has a mean matching the
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry with signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
and index set
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right]=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}}x_{\alpha_{t}}=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\,.

However, unlike in the discrete model (
§​​ 2.2
), the product
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
is not (even approximately) a Gaussian-noise observation of
x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}
:

\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}+{\delta x_{\alpha_{s}}\mathsf{G}_{t}+\delta x_{\alpha_{t}}\mathsf{G}_{s}}+{\mathsf{G}_{s}\mathsf{G}_{t}}\,.

(13)

The key is the aggregation in (
Dense

EqComb

): in the Gaussian case we take a renormalized (variance-1) average of the products
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
, which marginally yields the correct Gaussian law by CLT.

For this, we adopt a
tensor view
of the input instance
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, arranging the values
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
into corresponding tensor entries and ignoring duplicates. Let
\mathbf{Y}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
be a symmetric (up to index permutations) tensor, where entries with repeated indices are set to
0
. Symmetry allows us to index the remaining distinct-index entries by
k
-subsets of
\left[n\right]
; we set for each
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
:

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right\}=\emptyset\\
\mathsf{Y}_{j^{\star}},&\text{ otherwise, where }j^{\star}=\min\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right\}\,.\end{cases}

Consider the matrix flattening

Y=\mathsf{mat}_{k-k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{n}{k^{\prime}/2}}\,,

which is a matrix whose rows are indexed by elements of
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
and columns by
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
:
Y_{\rho,\gamma}=\mathbf{Y}_{\rho\cup\gamma}
for disjoint
\rho\times\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
(and
0
otherwise).
Each entry
\big(Y^{\top}Y\big)_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}
for disjoint
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
can then be expressed as

\big(Y^{\top}Y\big)_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}}=\sum_{\rho\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}}\mathbf{Y}_{\gamma_{1}\cup\rho}\mathbf{Y}_{\gamma_{2}\cup\rho}\ \ \approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s,t\in\left[m\right]:\\
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{2}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\,,

(14)

where the last approximation ignores the potential index set duplicates in the input. Then the tensorization (i.e., the inverse procedure to
\mathsf{mat}_{k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\left(\cdot\right)
)

\mathbf{Z}=\mathsf{tens}{\big(Y^{\top}Y\big)}\quad\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k^{\prime}}\,.

has

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Z}\right]\propto x^{\widetilde{\otimes}k^{\prime}}\,,
17
17

17

x^{\widetilde{\otimes}k^{\prime}}
denotes entries of
x^{\otimes k^{\prime}}
with distinct indices.
and a natural Gaussian analog of (
Dense

EqComb

) is to output renormalized
\mathbf{Z}
. We first use Gaussian Cloning, Poissonization, and index subsampling (see
§​​ 9
) to reduce
Y^{\top}Y
to a product of two independent non-centered, Bernoulli-masked Gaussian matrices.

The remaining challenge is the Wishart-like noise dependence and cross-terms (as is evident in Eq. (
13
)), which we address with our main technical Lemma
9.2
. Lemma
9.2
extends prior results of
[
12
]
for Wishart matrices (
X^{\top}X
where
X
is a matrix of i.i.d. Gaussians) to our setting of non-centered, masked Gaussian matrices. This shows that our reduction based on (
14
) outputs
\mathbf{Z}
close in total variation distance to a tensor with planted signal
\delta^{\prime}x^{\widetilde{\otimes}k^{\prime}}
and additive i.i.d. Gaussian noise as required by the Tensor PCA model. Additional postprocessing (
§​​ A.5
) can then map
\mathbf{Z}
back to the equation-based view
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
.

In the special case when the input density is
t=1
, through our tensor/matrix viewpoint we identify a connection to Spiked Covariance and Spiked Wigner Models and use recent reductions between them
[
20
]
to obtain a wider range of output
k^{\prime}
.

2.5
Reductions that Decrease Tensor Order
k

We now overview the main ideas behind the order-reducing reductions of Thm.
1.3
.
For the two endpoints
t\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, the discrete
§​​ 2.2
(for
t=0
) and Gaussian
§​​ 2.4
(for
t=1
) equation combination techniques give a reduction from

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n^{\prime})

for any
even

k^{\prime}<\begin{cases}k,&t=0\\
2k/3,&t=1\end{cases}
. While the result for
t=0
is a special case of
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
described in
§​​ 2.2
(proved in
§​​ 7
), for
t=1
, we additionally improve upon the general
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
results described in
§​​ 2.4
(proved in
§​​ 9
) to get a wider range of attainable
k^{\prime}
(see
§​​ 9.3
). We achieve this by generalizing a recent reduction from Spiked Covariance to Spiked Wigner
[
20
]
and apply it to the tensor/matrix
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
viewpoint described in
§​​ 2.4
.

To reach
odd

k^{\prime}
, in the case of even
k
or
t=1
we stack this with the
k^{\prime}\to k^{\prime}/2
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
instance (see
§​​ 2.2.3
,
§​​ 2.3
), obtaining the
k^{\prime}<\begin{cases}k/2,&t=0\\
k/3,&t=1\end{cases}
range claimed in Thm.
1.3
.

For
k
odd,
t=0
, we obtain a wider range by doing a two-step reduction
18
18

18

With additional cloning subroutines that ensure instance independence,
§​​ B.1
.
: first map
k
to
\tilde{k}
as above and then use the two instances of orders
k
and
\tilde{k}
to get one of order
k^{\prime}
. For this, we generalize the equation combination reduction to combine two
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
inputs, which may be of different order
k
, but share the same secret
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
(Lem.
7.2
):

k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta_{1}}(n)\text{ ``+" }k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta_{2}}(n)\rightarrow k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}(n)\,,

where
k^{\prime}=k_{1}+k_{2}-2a
and
a=|\alpha\cap\beta|
is the number of canceled indices in the equation combination identity.

3
Discussion and Open Problems

As noted in
§​​ 1.3
, our reductions establish

\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)\preceq\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)

(Hardness Implication)

for many pairs with
t\leq t^{\prime}
and
k\geq k^{\prime}
. We conjecture (Conj.
1.1
) that the current parameter restrictions reflect limitations of our techniques, rather than a fundamental barrier.

Open Problem 1

(Conj.
1.1
)
.

Establish (
Hardness Implication
) for all
0\leq t\leq t^{\prime}\leq 1
and
k\geq k^{\prime}
.

A particularly important special case is
t=0
to any
t^{\prime}>0
. Our densifying reductions map any fixed
t>0
to larger
t^{\prime}
(including
t^{\prime}=1
, i.e., Tensor PCA), but do not currently succeed for
t=0
(canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
) to denser regimes.

Open Problem 2

(Reductions from Canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
)
.

Establish (
Hardness Implication
) for some
k,k^{\prime}\geq 3
and
t=0,t^{\prime}\in(0,1]
.

All our reductions increase density
t
: they map instances with fewer entries to denser ones (with appropriately smaller entry-wise SNR). This leaves open the natural opposite direction – e.g., whether hardness of canonical Tensor PCA implies hardness of canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
.

Open Problem 3

(Sparsifying Reduction)
.

Establish (
Hardness Implication
) for some
k,k^{\prime}\geq 3
and
t>t^{\prime}\in\left[0,1\right]
.

Such a result from
t=1
to
t=0
would have an additional consequence: it would imply hardness of canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
from the secret-leakage planted-clique conjecture. This is because there exists an average-case reduction from secret-leakage planted clique to Tensor PCA
[
10
]
.

Hardness against subexponential-time algorithms.

Prior work gives
subexponential-time
algorithms and matching lower bounds in the regime where polynomial-time algorithms are believed not to exist, i.e.,
\eta>0

[
21
,
73
,
55
,
78
,
58
,
18
]
. In particular, it is conjectured that there is no
n^{O\left(l\right)}
-time algorithm solving
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
whenever

m\delta^{2}\approx n^{k/2-\eta}\ll n\left(n/l\right)^{k/2-1}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\eta>\left(k/2-1\right)\log_{n}l\,.

Poly-time average-case reductions transfer sub-exponential runtime algorithms. However, most our reductions are based on equation combination, multiplying pairs of tensor entries; this
doubles the deficiency
, so these maps do not trace the conjectured subexponential runtime curve.

Open Problem 4

(Correct Subexponential Trade-off)
.

Devise a reduction from

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\,,
for
t^{\prime}\neq t

that preserves the conjectured best-possible subexponential runtime, i.e., such that

\frac{\eta}{k/2-1}=\frac{\eta^{\prime}}{k^{\prime}/2-1}\,.

Sparse LWE.

As mentioned in
§​​ 1.3.3
, our
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
average-case reductions extend to
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
model (for constant
k
), first formalized in
[
53
]
(
§​​ 8
). In
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi)}
(Def.
8.1
) one observes
m
i.i.d. samples

\mathsf{Y}=a_{1}x_{i_{1}}+a_{2}x_{i_{2}}+\dots+a_{k}x_{i_{k}}+e\,,

(15)

where
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is the secret,
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is a uniformly random index set,
e\sim\chi\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is i.i.d. noise, and the coefficients
a_{s}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0\right)
. We prove an average-case reduction mapping

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}

for a wide range of parameters and explicit maps
f
, such that
(k^{\prime},m^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})=f(k,m,\theta)
(see Lemma
8.1
and informal Thm.
1.6
). Our results apply as long as the noise family
\left\{\chi_{\theta}\right\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
is
combination-stable
(Def.
8.2
), which includes the following common
\mathsf{LWE}
noise models:

•

Uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{\delta}
if
e=\begin{cases}0,&\text{w. prob. }\delta\\
\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right),&\text{w. prob. }1-\delta\,.\end{cases}

•

Discrete Gaussian Noise:

\chi_{s}(e)\propto\sum_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi(e+tq)^{2}/s^{2}}\,.

•

Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{l}
,
where
\chi_{l}
is a given measure on
\left\{-l,l\right\}
for a parameter
l\leq q
.

For these noise models we present a simple detection algorithm for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
based on equation collision (
§​​ 8.2
) that, combined with our reductions, yields new detection algorithms for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
.

First, exhaustive exploration of equation combination for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
is outside the scope of this paper and we believe the reductions in Thm.
1.6
can be extended to a wider parameter range. Second,
[
53
]
relate hardness of
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
in dimension
n
to that of (dense)
\mathsf{LWE}
in dimension
k
, and, more recently,
[
13
]
give a polynomial-time reduction from dense
\mathsf{LWE}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
that yields
n^{\Omega(k/\mathrm{poly}\log(k,n))}
-runtime lower bounds for solving
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
under standard assumptions for dense
\mathsf{LWE}
, but the overall complexity profile of
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
model remains underexplored.

Open Problem 5

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
)
.

1.

For fixed
k
and noise
\chi
, characterize the minimal sample complexity
m=m(n)
for poly-time detection and recovery in
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi)}
, and determine whether there is an computational-statistical gap;

2.

Develop a broader set of average-case reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
instances to characterize the complexity profile of
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
.

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
against low-noise.

In the
low-noise
regime of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
,

m\ll n^{k/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta=1-n^{-\nu},\nu>0\,.

Recent work
[
28
]
gives efficient detection and recovery algorithms in this regime whenever

m\geq\Omega\big(n^{1+(1-\Delta)\cdot\frac{k-1}{2}}\big)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\geq 1-n^{-\frac{1+\Delta}{2}}\quad\text{for }\Delta\in\left(0,1\right)\,.

(16)

Our discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
reductions in
§​​ 7
extend naturally to the low-noise setting and give an average-case reduction from

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,,\qquad 2|t|k<k^{\prime}\leq k(1-t)\text{ is even},

with
m=n^{k(t+1)/2}
and
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{k^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+1)/2})
,
t^{\prime}=\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t
, and
\delta^{\prime}=1-2(1-o(1))n^{-\nu}
(see Lem.
7.1
). This particular reduction does
not
trace the algorithmic trade-off (
16
) and does not yield new algorithms in this low-noise regime.

Open Problem 6
.

Develop average-case reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instances with different parameters
\left(k,m,\delta\right)
that (1) work in the low-noise regime above, and (2) trace (or improve) the
m\leftrightarrow\delta
tradeoff (
16
).

Algorithmic implications of our average-case reductions.

Our reductions to Tensor PCA (
§​​ 11
) work for any
t\in(0,1]
and map

The reductions are stated for
\eta\geq 0

k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,\qquad k^{\prime}<\alpha k\text{ is even},

where

Moreover, these reductions can be adjusted

For our purposes even the matrix case

above at

\delta^{\prime}\geq\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)\cdot n^{-k/4}\,.

Putting these together gives:

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
t\in(0,1],k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and let

m=n^{k(t-1)/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\geq\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)\cdot n^{-kt/4}\,,

where
\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)
hides the
\mathrm{poly}\log(n)
factors. For

satisfying

k>\begin{cases}1/t,&t\in\left(0,1/3\right)\\
2/\left(1-t\right),&t\in[1/3,1]\,,\end{cases}

there exists a
\mathrm{poly}\left(n\right)
-time algorithm for weak recovery up to a global sign for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
(and, by the discrete-Gaussian equivalence,
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\tilde{\Theta}\left(\delta\right))
).

The conditions on
k
are the admissibility conditions for mapping
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
to the full 2-Tensor PCA for
\delta^{\prime}=\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)\cdot n^{-k/4}
via
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
(see
§​​ 11
).
This leaves open several small-

\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)
factors. While known techniques (e.g.,
[
78
]
) likely extend naturally to the intermediate densities
t\in\left(0,1\right)
, removing the
\tilde{\Theta}\left(1\right)
factors for
t\in[0,1)
might be more challenging – such tight bounds have been obtained for Tensor PCA only recently in
[

]
.

Open Problem 7

(Recovery at Intermediate Sparsities
t\in\left(0,1\right)
)
.

For all
