at the computational threshold (
Comp. Thr.
)

m=n^{k(t+1)/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta\geq\Theta\left(1\right)\cdot n^{-kt/4}\,.

4
Review of Known Algorithms and Lower Bounds for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA

Here we overview existing algorithmic and statistical results for the two models.
From our computational equivalence results (Prop.
1.1
), the existing algorithms and statistical and computational lower bounds transfer between discrete and Gaussian versions of the problems up to
\mathrm{poly}\log
factors.

4.1
Information-Theoretic Lower Bounds

The first information-theoretic lower bounds for Tensor PCA, i.e. proof that the model is indistinguishable from pure noise below the threshold (
IT Thr.
), were obtained in
[
71
,
62
]
and were generalized to our case of the signal Rademacher prior
x\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\right)
in
[
69
]
. For sharp analysis of information-theoretically optimal estimation precision, see
[
52
]
. We remark that the second-moment method of
[
69
]
adapts to the case of Bernoulli mask, i.e., to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
with intermediate densities
t\in[0,1]
.

4.2
Algorithms

State-of-the art algorithms for the canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
succeed above the computational threshold (
Comp. Thr.
) for detection/refutation
[
23
,
8
,
73
,
41
,
44
,
5
]
and recovery
[
21
,
39
,
40
]
, with several works (starting with
[
73
]
) obtaining subexponential algorithms in the conjectured hard regime.

For Tensor PCA, known algorithms include tensor unfolding
[
71
]
, gradient descent (or Langevin dynamics) and AMP (all suboptimal relative to other efficient algorithms)
[
16
,
60
]
, spectral and sum-of-square methods
[
46
,
45
,
47
,
18
,
65
]
, and Kikuchi hierarchy
[
78
,
59
]
.
The detection algorithm of
[
78
]
naturally extends to the case of
t\in(0,1)
. For recovery in this intermediate regime, we obtain the first known recovery algorithms directly from our reductions to Tensor PCA that succeed for sufficiently large (but constant) tensor order
k
.

4.3
Computational Lower Bounds

A growing line of work gives evidence towards computational hardness in the conjectured hard regimes by proving that broad restricted classes of algorithms fail to solve the problem. For the canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
,
[
55
,
75
,
42
]
show sum-of-squares (SOS)/Lasserre and
[
38
]
statistical query lower bounds; for Tensor PCA, there exist lower bounds for SOS algorithms
[
46
,
45
,
67
]
, statistical query model
[
30
]
, memory-bounded algorithms
[
31
]
, and low-degree polynomials
[
58
,
56
]
; any method relying on spectral information is ruled out by
[
62
]
.

Additionally, reduction-based hardness from the variants of planted clique conjecture (secret leakage and hypergraph PC) was obtained for Tensor PCA in
[
10
,
61
]
.

5
Model Variants and Simple Reductions

Here we describe preliminary reductions and equivalences used as subroutines in our main results. These include (1) equivalences between variants of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
models (e.g., discrete/Gaussian models, different equation sampling procedures, etc), summarized in
§​​ 5.1
, and (2) some simple parameter-changing reductions (
§​​ 5.2
).

5.1
Variants of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
Model

A
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instance (Def.
1.1
) consists of
m
independent samples
\left(\alpha,\mathsf{Y}\right)
, where
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
and
\mathsf{Y}
is a noisy observation of
x_{\alpha}
.
Prop.
1.1
is a representative example of the basic equivalences we use as subroutines (here: discrete vs. Gaussian observation models).
In this subsection we record several additional variants of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and relate them by simple reductions.

We parametrize a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
-like model by four choices. By default (i.e., model with no superscript) we use the first option in choices (2)-(4); superscripts indicate deviations from these defaults:

(1) Noise model

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}:\ \mathsf{Y}\sim x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}:\ \mathsf{Y}\sim\delta x_{\alpha}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)

(2) Number of samples

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:\ m\text{ samples}

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{Pois}}:\ m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)\text{ samples}

(3) Index space for
\alpha

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:\ \alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
(
k
-sets of distinct indices)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{FULL}}:\ \alpha\in[n]^{k}
(
k
-multisets, index repetitions allowed, Def.
A.1
)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{asymm}}:\ \alpha\in[n]^{\underline{k}}
(ordered
k
-tuples of distinct indices)

(4) Sampling of
\alpha

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:
with replacement

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{wor}}:
without replacement

Figure 6:
Choices in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variants: the defaults are checked and correspond to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
.

With this notation, the standard
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
(Def.
1.1
) uses the default options in (1)-(4), while
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
differs only in (1) (Gaussian noise) and keeps the defaults in (2)–(4).

We use the
(t,\eta)
reparametrization for these models analogously to standard
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(see Def.
1.3
).

5.1.1
Equivalence of Models with Different Noise Models: Choice (1)

Switching between the discrete and Gaussian noise models (choice (1)) results in computationally equivalent models, up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
losses. We apply standard scalar reductions between Gaussian and Bernoulli variables (as developed in
[
64
,
11
]
)
that preserve signal level
\delta
up to
\log n
factors to our models, entrywise, and obtain in
§​​ A.1
:

Proposition 5.1

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (2)-(4) in Fig.
6
. The corresponding discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
model and its Gaussian analog
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{Gaussianize}/\mathsf{Discretize}
, Alg.
10
,
11
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

5.1.2
Equivalence of Models with Fixed and Poisson Number of Samples: Choice (2)

Options for the number of samples (choice (2)) are also interchangeable for our purposes:
for any
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
-like model in Fig.
6
, modifying the sample count rule in choice (2) yields a computationally equivalent model, up to polylogarithmic losses in parameters.

We prove the following equivalence, that allows us to switch between models with
m
and
\mathsf{Pois}(m^{\prime}),m^{\prime}=\Theta(m)
samples, in
§​​ A.2
.

Proposition 5.2

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be fixed model choices (1,3,4) in Fig.
6
. Then the fixed-sample
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and its Poissonized variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ResamplePois}/\mathsf{ResampleM}
, Alg.
12
,
13
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

5.1.3
Models that Allow Index Repetitions and Symmetrization: Choice (3)

We show in
§​​ A.3
that, with a natural choice of measure on the index
k
-multisets (Def.
A.1
), the
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
models are equivalent to a collection of independent lower-order instances
k\mathsf{\text{-}model},(k-2)\mathsf{\text{-}model},(k-4)\mathsf{\text{-}model},\dots
.

Proposition 5.3

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}
and a Collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z},t\in\left[0,1\right],\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any choices (1,2,4)
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
in Fig.
6
. Then,

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}_{t,\eta}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{and}\quad\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t_{r},\eta}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}\qquad\text{with }t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}

are computationally equivalent: there exist poly-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{Decompose}/\mathsf{Compose}
, Alg.
14
,
15
) both ways.
22
22

22

Here the second model is a collection of independent
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
instances.
These reductions preserve the input signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
exactly.

Additionally, when sampling is
with replacement
(choice (4)), one can switch between the standard index space
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
(first option in choice (3)) and the space of ordered
k
-tuples (third option in choice (3)), i.e., the symmetric and asymmetric
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
models become equivalent (
§​​ A.4
).
23
23

23

We note that the models are equivalent for recovery for any option in choice (4) and the limitation is a reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{asymm}}
for recovery.

Proposition 5.4

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{asymm}}
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be any fixed choices (1,2) in Fig.
6
and choice (4) set to sampling with replacement. Then,
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and the corresponding “asymmetric” variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{asymm}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{DeSymm}/\mathsf{Symm}
, Alg.
16
,
17
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

5.1.4
Sampling
\alpha
with or without Replacement: Choice (4)

All reductions in this paper are stated for sampling with replacement (the default in choice (4)). With small technical modifications in the reduction algorithms, the same results also hold verbatim for the without-replacement variants, provided both the source and target models use the same sampling rule.

The above claim is not immediate: while known techniques such as cloning (
§​​ B.1.1
) imply that
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variants with entry sampling
with
or
without
replacement are computationally equivalent for recovery, their equivalence for detection in our setting is not addressed, to the best of our knowledge.
24
24

24

The obstacle to obtaining the detection equivalence is the reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
: for recovery we can use cloning to obtain entry duplicates, but for detection the null models are mapped incorrectly. This is because our null models (Def.
C.1
) contain a uniformly random signal
w_{\alpha}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right]
in place of structured
x_{\alpha}
and cloning replicates
w_{\alpha}
.

We show in
§​​ A.5
:

Proposition 5.5

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
for Recovery)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
6
. The two models
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent for recovery: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ToWORSampling}/\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
, Alg.
18
,
19
) for recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

Additionally, we show the complete computational equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{wor}}
(for both detection and recovery) for
t=1
:

Proposition 5.6

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
for
t=1
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
6
and let
\eta>0
. The two models
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ToWORSampling}/\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
, Alg.
18
,
19
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

Case
t=1
in Prop.
5.6
is of independent interest, since it captures Tensor PCA model. Recall that in the standard Tensor PCA, denoted as
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta}(n)
(equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
with
\delta=n^{-k/4-\eta/2}
), there is a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
and one observes a noisy tensor

\delta\cdot x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}\,,\qquad\delta=n^{-k/4-\eta/2}\,,

(17)

where
\mathsf{G}
is a symmetric order-
k
tensor of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. As a special case, Prop.
5.6
relates this model to the other variants we consider.
25
25

25

To obtain the exact number of samples in
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}
, the potentially missing
(1-\mathrm{poly}\log(n)^{-1})
fraction of entries can be filled with pure noise via the
\mathsf{Densify}
reduction (Alg.
21
), see
§​​ 5.3

Corollary 5.1

(of Prop.
5.6
)
.

The
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}_{\eta}(n)
(equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
with
\delta=n^{-k/4-\eta/2}
) model with
\eta>0
, which observes the noisy symmetric tensor in Eq. (
17
), is computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
for the choices
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
(and, in particular,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
).

Moreover, the model that observes only distinct-index entries of Eq. (
17
) is computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
for the choices
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
(and, in particular,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
).

5.2
Simple Reductions

5.2.1
Cloning and Splitting Reductions

Here we consider the reductions (cloning and splitting) that map one input instance
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
to two output instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
sharing the same signal
x
(
§​​ B.1
). Cloning keeps the same index sets as the input but ensures the noise independence across the two outputs. Splitting makes the two outputs fully independent instances (independent indices and independent noise), while preserving the same signal
x
.

Cloning.

Cloning uses the standard Gaussian-cloning identity (e.g.
[
11
]
): given
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
, generate an independent
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and set
Y^{(1)}=\frac{Y-Z}{\sqrt{2}}
,
Y^{(2)}=\frac{Y+Z}{\sqrt{2}}
. Then
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}
are independent and each is distributed as
\mathcal{N}(\mu/\sqrt{2},1)
. Applying this primitive entrywise,
26
26

26

For
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
we first reduce to the Gaussian analog via
§​​ 5.1.1
, apply the cloning primitive entrywise, then (if needed) map back.
we obtain:

Lemma 5.1

(Instance Cloning)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
6
. There exists a poly-time algorithm (
\mathsf{kXORClone}/\mathsf{kGaussClone}
, Alg.
20
) that, given an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
, returns two instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta))
that have independent entrywise noise, the same index sets, and the same signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as the input
\mathcal{Y}
.

Remark 5.1

(Reduction up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
Factors)
.

In the
t,\eta
parametrization, Lemma
5.1
yields an average-case reduction from
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
(two same-index copies with independent noise) by Prop.
C.3
.

Remark 5.2

(Creating Several Clones)
.

To obtain
A>2
clones
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{Y}^{(A)}
, we can repeat the cloning procedure recursively
\lceil\log A\rceil
times, incurring a
\Theta(\sqrt{A})
loss in
\delta
. In
t,\eta
parametrization this becomes an average-case reduction from
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{Y}^{(A)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta^{\prime}}(n)
for
\eta^{\prime}=\eta+\log_{n}A
admitting the same guarantees: entrywise noise independence across the copies, the same index sets and signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Splitting.

Cloning achieves noise independence but reuses the input index sets. Splitting instead produces two fully independent instances by allocating each observed sample to one of the two outputs.

We state splitting for sampling with replacement. For sampling without replacement this paper’s techniques suffice for an average-case reduction for recovery;
27
27

27

With
\mathrm{poly}\log n
losses in parameters
with minor modifications this suffices as a subroutine in our main reductions.

Lemma 5.2

(Instance Splitting)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
6
and choice (4) set to sampling with replacement. There exists a poly-time algorithm (
\mathsf{kSplit}
) that, given an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
, returns two independent instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,\Theta(m),\delta)
that share the same signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as the input
\mathcal{Y}
.

Remarks
5.1
,
5.2
similarly apply.

5.3
Reductions that Change the Number of Samples
m

In
§​​ B.2
we prove simple reductions that modify the number of samples in
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
from
m
to
m^{\prime}
by either (1) injecting pure noise observations, or (2) removing some of the samples.

Lemma 5.3

(Adjusting the Number of Samples
m
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
6
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be parameters. There exists a poly-time average-case reduction (
\mathsf{Densify}/\mathsf{Sparsify}
, Alg.
21
,
22
) for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,,\qquad\text{with }\delta^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\delta\,,&m^{\prime}\leq m\\
\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta\cdot m/m^{\prime})\,,&m^{\prime}>m\,.\end{cases}

Moreover, the reduction preserves the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

In the
t,\eta
parametrization this yields:

Corollary 5.2
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
6
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
, and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters. There exists a poly-time average-case reduction (
\mathsf{Densify}/\mathsf{Sparsify}
, Alg.
21
,
22
) for both detection and recovery

\displaystyle\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n)\,,\quad\text{where }\eta^{\prime}=\eta+{k|t-t^{\prime}|/2}\,.

Moreover, the reduction preserves the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

5.4
Reduction Decreasing Order
k
to
k/a

In
§​​ B.3
we prove a reduction that maps
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
to
(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
for some
a|k
in the case
\mathsf{FULL}\in\star
. The reduction uses a change of variables, modifying the signal
x
to
x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{a}}\right]
. We emphasize that the assumption of the full
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variant (
\mathsf{FULL}\in\star
), where index repetitions are allowed, is essential for a correct distributional mapping: otherwise, the resulting index sets do not match the desired distribution.

Lemma 5.4

(Reducing Order
k
to
k/a
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1,2,4) in Fig.
6
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters.
For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},a|k
, there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (
\mathsf{ReduceK}
, Alg.
23
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{a}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
23
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right]
.

The reduction in Lemma
5.4
, in combination with reductions in Prop.
5.3
that decompose an instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}
into standard (distinct index) instances of orders
k,k-2,k-4,\dots
, yields the following order-reducing reductions. For convenience, we also refer to the reduction in Cor.
5.3
as
\mathsf{ReduceK}
(Alg.
23
).

Corollary 5.3

(of Lemma
5.4
)
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1,2,4) in Fig.
6
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters.
For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},a|k
and
k^{\prime}\in\left\{k/a,k/a-2,k/a-4,\dots\right\}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{a}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
23
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right]
.

6
Preliminaries

6.1
Notation

Distributions.
Denote
\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta\right)
to be a
\pm 1
random variable such that
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta\right)\right]=\delta
.

For
M\in\mathbb{R}^{a\times b}
,
\mathcal{N}(M,I_{a\times b})
is a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{a\times b}
with i.i.d. Gaussian entries of mean
M
and entrywise variance
1
.

A two-component Gaussian mixture is written as

p\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\sigma_{1}^{2})+(1-p)\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\sigma_{2}^{2})\,,\quad p\in\left(0,1\right)\,.

Sets and indices.
For a set
S
,
\binom{S}{k}
denotes all
k
-tuples of elements in
S
without repetitions. We write
\left[n\right]=\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
. We write
\left\{n\right\}^{k}
to denote a set of all
k
-multisets of
n
and
\left[n\right]^{\underline{k}}
to denote a set of all distinct-element
k
-tuples of
n
.

We denote
\mathcal{S}_{n}
to be a set of all permutations
\left[n\right]\to\left[n\right]
.

For a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an index tuple
\alpha=\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
, denote
x_{\alpha}=x_{\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right)}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}
.

Denote
x^{\widetilde{\otimes}a}
to be the vector composed of
x_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{a}
. This is just the vectorized rank-1 tensor of order
a
where we keep only entries with distinct indices.

For a vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and indices
a<b\in[n]
, denote
x_{\left[a:b\right]}\in\mathbb{R}^{b-a+1}
to be
x_{\left[a:b\right]}=\left(x_{a},\dots,x_{b}\right)^{\top}
.

For
x,y
vectors, matrices, or tensors of the same dimension, denote
x\odot y
to be their entrywise product.

Symmetric tensors.
We say a tensor
\mathbf{Y}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
is symmetric if it is symmetric up to index permutations, i.e. for any permutation
\sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
,
\mathbf{Y}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}=\mathbf{Y}_{\sigma(i_{1}),\dots,\sigma(i_{k})}
.

Invariance under index permutations allows us to index symmetric tensors by multisets
\alpha\in\left\{n\right\}^{k}
: we write
\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}
for
\mathbf{Y}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}
, where
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
is any fixed ordering of
\alpha
.

For a symmetric tensor
\mathbf{Y}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
, denote the flattening
Y=\mathsf{mat}_{a\times k-a}\left(\mathbf{Y}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{{\binom{n}{a}}\times\binom{n}{k-a}}
. It is indexed by a pair of sets
(\alpha,\beta)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{a}\times\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-a}
and satisfies

Y_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha\cup\beta}\,.

In a special case
a=k
,
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{a}}
is a vector.
For a matrix
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{{\binom{n}{a}}\times\binom{n}{k-a}}
, denote the reverse operation to be
\mathbf{Y}=\mathsf{tens}\left(Y\right)\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
(where missing entries are set to
0
).

Polylog factors.
We will sometimes use
\widetilde{O},\widetilde{\Theta},\widetilde{\Omega}
to hide
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors, e.g.,
\widetilde{O}(1)
may be as large as
\mathrm{poly}\log n
.

6.2
Concentration Inequalities

Proposition 6.1

(Concentration of Binomial RV)
.

Let
X\sim\mathsf{Bin}(n,p)
, where
p>0
and
np\geq n^{t}
for some
t>0
. By Bernstein’s inequality,

\Pr\left[|X-pn|\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{np(1-p)+t/3}\right)\,,

and as a consequence, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

X\in\left[pn\pm C\sqrt{pn}\log n\right]=:\left[pn\pm\phi(p,n)\right]\,,

where the constant
C=C(c)
is an explicit function of
c
.

Proposition 6.2

(Concentration of Poisson RV)
.

For
X\sim\mathsf{Pois}(\lambda)
, the Chernoff bound argument yields for any
t>0
,

\Pr\left[|X-\lambda|\geq t\right]\leq 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2(t+\lambda)}}\,.

In particular, if
\lambda=n^{t}
for some
t>0
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

X\in\left[\lambda\pm C\sqrt{\lambda}\log n\right]\,,

where the constant
C=C(c)
is an explicit function of
c
.

6.3
Properties of Total Variation

The analysis of our reductions will make use of the following well-known facts and inequalities concerning total variation distance (see, e.g.,
[
68
]
). Recall that the total variation distance between distributions
P
and
Q
on a measure space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})
is given by
d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P,Q)=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}}|P(A)-Q(A)|
.

Fact 6.1

(TV Properties)
.

The distance
d_{\mathsf{TV}}
satisfies the following properties:

1.

(Triangle Inequality) For any distributions
P,Q,S
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})
, it holds that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P,Q)\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}(P,S)+d_{\mathsf{TV}}(S,Q)\,.

2.

(Conditioning on an Event) For any distribution
P
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})
and event
A\in\mathcal{B}
, it holds that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(P(\,\cdot\,|A),P\big)=1-P(A)\,.

3.

(Conditioning on a Random Variable, Convexity) For any two pairs of random variables
(X,Y)
and
(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
each taking values in a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})
, it holds that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(Y),\mathcal{L}(Y^{\prime})\big)+\mathbb{E}_{y\sim Y}\big[d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(X|Y=y),\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime}|Y^{\prime}=y)\big)\big]\,,

where we define
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(X|Y=y),\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime}|Y^{\prime}=y)\big)=1
for all
y\not\in\textnormal{supp}(Y^{\prime})
.

4.

(Data Processing Inequality) For any two random variables
X,X^{\prime}
on a measurable space
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B})
and any Markov Kernel
K:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
, it holds that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(K(X)),\mathcal{L}(K(X^{\prime}))\big)\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(X),\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime})\big)\,.

Lemma 6.1

(KL-Divergence Between Gaussians)
.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be positive definite matrices. Then

\mathsf{KL}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},\Sigma_{1})\|\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},\Sigma_{2}))=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\log\frac{\det\Sigma_{2}}{\det\Sigma_{1}}-n+\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{1})+(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})^{\top}\Sigma_{2}^{-1}(\mu_{2}-\mu_{1})\Big]\,.

In particular, by Pinsker’s inequality,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},I_{n}),\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},I_{n}))\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{KL}(\mathcal{N}(\mu_{1},I_{n})\|\mathcal{N}(\mu_{2},I_{n}))}=\frac{1}{2}\|\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}\|_{2}\,.

(18)

Combining the convexity of total variation (Item 3 in Fact
6.1
) and the bound (
18
) above, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 6.2

(TV Between Gaussians with Shifted Means)
.

Let
X,A,B\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be random variables with
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
independent of
A
and
B
. Then

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(X+A+B),\mathcal{L}(X+B)\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\|A\|_{2}\,.

7
Discrete Equation Combination

7.1
Reduction Idea and Lemma Statement

We prove a reduction for the discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
models in Lemma
7.1
and analogous result for Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
follows by the discrete-Gaussian equivalence in Prop.
5.1
.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(equivalently
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) with a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, we observe a collection
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
for
\alpha_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=x_{\alpha_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\,,

where
\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
and we recall that
x_{\alpha}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}
for an index set
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
. As described in detail in
§​​ 2.1
, the product of two
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entries
\mathsf{Y}_{s},\mathsf{Y}_{t}
for
s,t\in\left[m\right]
with
k^{\prime}=|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|
is distributed as a
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry with signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
corresponding to an index set
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
:

\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}=x_{\alpha_{s}}x_{\alpha_{t}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{s}\mathsf{W}_{t}=x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\cdot{\left(\mathsf{W}_{s}\mathsf{W}_{t}\right)}\sim x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta^{2}\right)\,.

(19)

We call the operation of multiplying two
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entries to obtain another
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry
equation combination
. The observation in Eq. (
19
) suggests two potential naïve reductions depending on the number of available pairs
(s,t)
with
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
:

1.

Sparse case – few pairs
(s,t)
:
for all
s,t\in\left[m\right]
satisfying
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
, include a pair
\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)
into the output instance
\mathcal{Z}
:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right):\,s,t\in\left[m\right]\text{ and }|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}\right\}\,.

(Sparse
\mathsf{EqComb}
)

2.

Dense case – many pairs
(s,t)
:
if for every index set
\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
we expect many
s,t\in\left[m\right]
satisfying
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma
, we can collect all of these observations into

\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}=\left\{\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}:s,t\in[m]\text{ and }\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma\right\}

(20)

and aggregate them via a majority vote, outputting a collection of equations

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathsf{MAJ}\big(\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}\big)\right):\,\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\text{ and }|\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}|\neq 0\right\}\,.

(Dense
\mathsf{EqComb}
)

As discussed in
§​​ 2.1
, in both cases the output
\mathcal{Z}
does not match the
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
distribution because of the dependencies in the index sets of observed entries and in the noise distribution. In Lemma
7.1
, we build a combination-based reduction for both sparse and dense cases that avoids index set and entry dependence by design.
Essentially, Algorithm
1
ensures that each input entry
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
participates in at most one product pair, so the products in Eqs. (
Sparse

EqComb

) and (
Dense

EqComb

) do not reuse noise across different
\gamma
(see
§​​ 2.2
for more intuition). The resulting reduction is the main building block of our densifying reductions in Thm.
10.1
(
§​​ 10
).

Lemma 7.1

(Discrete Equation Combination Reduction)
.

Fix
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. For any even integer
k^{\prime}
, s.t.

k^{\prime}\leq k\left(1-t\right)\,,

there exists an average-case reduction
28
28

28

As defined in Def.
C.6
.

for both detection and recovery (Alg.
1
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,

where
t^{\prime}=\min\left\{\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t,1\right\}
. Moreover, Alg.
1
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}
for
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
.
29
29

29

The algorithm can set
x^{\prime}
to have any
n^{\prime}
-coordinate subset of
x
; the algorithm succeeds for any
n^{\prime}=n\left(1-(\mathrm{poly}\log n)^{-1}\right)
.

Remark 7.1

(Sparse and Dense
\mathsf{EqComb}
)
.

In Lemma
7.1
, the case
t^{\prime}=\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t\leq 1

corresponds to the (
Sparse

EqComb

) and
\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t>1
with
t^{\prime}=1
to the (
Dense

EqComb

).

Remark 7.2

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
Sampling Procedure for Alg.
1
and Gaussian Input)
.

While Algorithm
1
, as stated, is a reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})
, we sometimes use it as a subroutine for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
models with the standard sampling procedure, i.e., as a reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}(n^{\prime})
. It is implicitly assumed that first a Poissonization (Alg.
12
) reduction is applied, then Alg.
1
, and then the reduction back to standard sampling model (Alg.
13
).

Similarly if Alg.
1
is applied as a Gaussian model reduction for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
: this implicitly assumes the insertion of the appropriate Gaussianization/Discretization (
§​​ A.1.1
,
§​​ A.1.2
) or/and Resampling (
§​​ A.2
) reductions.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

Output:

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,
\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}
.

1

//
Prune the input equations; only consider index sets
\alpha_{j}
that contain exactly
k-k^{\prime}/2
coordinates in
\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]
, denote them as the "cancellation set"
\mathcal{C}(j)

\mathsf{S}=\left\{j:\,j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]\text{ and }|\alpha_{j}\cap\left[\kappa n\right]|=k^{\prime}/2\right\}

2

For each
j\in\mathsf{S}
,
\mathcal{C}(j)\leftarrow\alpha_{j}\cap\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]

3

//
Aggregate entries with the same cancellation set
\beta

For each
\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
, let

\mathcal{N}(\beta)=\left\{j\in\left[\mathsf{S}\right]:\,\mathcal{C}(j)=\beta\right\}

4

//
Form disjoint combination pairs and record them

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

5

forall

\beta\text{ s.t. }\big|\mathcal{N}(\beta)\big|\geq 2

do

6

s,t\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}{2}\right]

7

if

|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}

then

8

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\left\{\left(\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t},\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right)\right\}

9

if

2kt\leq k^{\prime}

then

10

//
Sparse case -- few observations in
\mathcal{Z}

11

\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z})
,
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=\min\big\{\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}},|\mathcal{Z}|\big\}

//
See pf. of Lem.
7.1
for defn of
B_{Z}

12

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\leftarrow\text{ random }m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\text{-subset of }\mathcal{Z}

13

Return
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}

14

else

15

//
Dense case -- many observations in
\mathcal{Z}

16

\forall\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
,
\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}\leftarrow\big\{\mathsf{Z}:\,\left(\gamma,\mathsf{Z}\right)\in\mathcal{Z}\big\}

17

N\leftarrow B_{obs}

//
See proof of Lemma
7.1
for defn of
B_{obs}

18

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\leftarrow\emptyset

//
Initialize the answer to be empty

19

for

\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}

do

20

21

\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}\leftarrow\text{ random }\min\left\{N,|\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}|\right\}\text{-subset of }\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}

22

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\leftarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\cup\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathsf{MAJ}\big(\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}\big)\right)\right\}

23

Return
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\big)

//
Alg.
19

24

Algorithm 1

\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}\left(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n\right)

We prove Lemma
7.1
in §
7.2
. In §
7.3
we generalize Lemma
7.1
to accept as an input two instances
k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
of different order, which will be used as a subroutine to our reductions that decrease the tensor order
k
(§
2.5
and §
12
). In §
8
, we extend Lemma
7.1
to the
k
-sparse LWE model, which considers linear equations over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
as opposed to
\mathbb{F}_{2}
as in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
.

7.2
Proof of Lemma
7.1

We prove Lemma
7.1
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
, i.e, we show that Alg.
1
is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime}=\kappa n)\,,

where
t^{\prime}=\min\left\{\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t,1\right\}
and the signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
is mapped to a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}
.
The analogous result for the standard
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
immediately follows by the computational equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
: we show in Prop.
5.2
that there exist average-case reductions for both detection and recovery from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
and vice versa.

We present the proof for the planted case and the same argument applies verbatim to the null case
\delta=0
(see Def.
C.1
for the null model definition).

Let
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]},m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
be a (planted) instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
. In particular,
\alpha_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=x_{\alpha_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\,,\text{ where }\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)\,.

We will show that Alg.
1
outputs
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n)
, satisfying

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}})\approx_{d_{\mathsf{TV}}=o(1)}k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,,

(21)

where
n^{\prime}=\kappa n
,
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left((n^{\prime})^{k^{\prime}(1+t^{\prime})/2}\right)
,
and
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left((n^{\prime})^{-k^{\prime}t^{\prime}/4-\eta}\right)
, where
\widetilde{\Theta}
hides constants depending on
k,k^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors. Since it is sufficient to map parameters up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors by Prop.
C.3
, Eq. (
21
) shows that Alg.
1
is an average-case reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})
.

Step 1: Distribution of elements in
\mathcal{Z}
.

We show that
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\left(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[|\mathcal{Z}|\right]}
, which is the set constructed in Lines 4-8 of Alg.
1
, contains elements
\left(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right)\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}
for which

\gamma_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Z}_{j}\sim x_{\gamma_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta^{2}\right)

(22)

are independent across
j\in\left[|\mathcal{Z}|\right]
.

For every
\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
and every recorded observation
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\in\mathcal{Z}
with
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}=\gamma
in Lines 4-8

\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}=x_{\alpha_{s}}x_{\alpha_{t}}\mathsf{W}_{s}\mathsf{W}_{t}\sim x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta^{2}\right)\,.

(23)

By construction, every entry
\mathsf{Y}_{j},j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
appears in exactly one
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
for some
\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
, and since every
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
yields at most one recorded observation into
\mathcal{Z}
, every entry
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
appears in at most one equation (
23
). Therefore, the entry-wise noise terms
\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta^{2}\right)
in Eq. (
23
) are independent, so the distribution of values
\mathsf{Z}_{j}
in the elements
\left(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
matches the one in Eq. (
22
).

Moreover, for any
\beta
with
|\mathcal{N}\left(\beta\right)|\geq 2
, if the chosen
s,t\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}{2}\right]
satisfy
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
, the resulting index set
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
is uniformly random on
\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
, chosen independently for different
\beta
. Hence, the distribution of index sets
\gamma_{j}
in the elements
\left(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right)\in\mathcal{Z}
matches the one in Eq. (
22
).

Step 2: Bound on
|\mathcal{Z}|
.

We next show a high probability bound on
|\mathcal{Z}|
, which is the number of observations recorded in Lines 4-8 of Alg.
1
. We show that, with high probability,

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq C_{Z}\cdot n^{kt+k^{\prime}/2}\eqcolon 2B_{Z}\,,

where
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
hides constants depending on
k,a
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors and denote the corresponding event

S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}=\left\{|\mathcal{Z}|\geq 2B_{Z}\right\}\,,\qquad\Pr\left[S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}^{c}\right]=o(1)\,.

From Poisson splitting, for each
\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
, given that
\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n
,

|\mathcal{N}\left(\beta\right)|\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(m\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)})\right)\quad\text{and}\quad m\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)})=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k(t-1)/2+k^{\prime}/2}\right)=\widetilde{O}(1)\,,

where the last equality uses
k^{\prime}\leq k(1-t)
. Consequently,

\Pr\left[|\mathcal{N}\left(\beta\right)|\geq 2\right]=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{k(t-1)+k^{\prime}}\big)\,.

Given
|\mathcal{N}\left(\beta\right)|\geq 2
and
s,t\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\mathcal{N}(\beta)}{2}\right]
, with at least constant probability the remaining indices are disjoint and satisfy
|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|=k^{\prime}
. Then,

|\mathcal{Z}|\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(\binom{n(1-\kappa)}{k-k^{\prime}/2},\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k(t-1)+k^{\prime}}\right)\right)\,,

and by Binomial concentration in Prop.
6.1
, with high probability,

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq\widetilde{\Theta}(1)\cdot n^{k-k^{\prime}/2}\cdot n^{k(t-1)+k^{\prime}}=C_{Z}\cdot n^{kt+k^{\prime}/2}\eqcolon 2B_{Z}\,,

for
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

To summarize Steps 1 and 2,
\mathcal{Z}
contains
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq 2B_{Z}
elements
\left(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right),j\in\left[|\mathcal{Z}|\right]
for
\gamma_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\right]
and independent
\mathsf{Z}_{j}\sim x_{\gamma_{j}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta^{2})
.

Step 3: Distribution of the output set
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Here we conclude the proof by showing

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)=o_{n}(1)\,,

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is the output of the Alg.
1
. This is handled differently in the sparse and dense regimes.

Case 1:
2kt\leq k^{\prime}
(sparse).
In Alg.
1
, the output
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is set to be a random
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}
subset of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
, where
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=\min\{\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}},|\mathcal{Z}|\}
for a generated
\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z})
. By the Poisson concentration in Prop.
6.2
, with high probability, given
S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}
,

\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\leq 2B_{M}\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\,,

in which case
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z})
. Denote this event
S_{\mathrm{Pois}}=\left\{\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\leq|\mathcal{Z}|\right\}
,
\Pr\left[S_{Pois}^{c}\right]=o(1)
. Conditioned on
S_{Pois}
and
S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
contains
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z})

k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entries with uniformly random index sets and signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
, and therefore, for
n^{\prime}=\kappa n
and
m^{\prime}=B_{Z}
,

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{Pois}\cap S_{|\mathcal{Z}|})=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,.

Then, conditioning on a high probability event in total variation (Fact
6.1
),

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)=d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{Pois}\cap S_{|\mathcal{Z}|})\right)=1-\Pr\left[S_{Pois}\cap S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\right]=o_{n}(1)\,.

It remains to express
m^{\prime}=B_{Z}
and
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
in terms of
t^{\prime}=\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t
and
n^{\prime}=\kappa n
:

m^{\prime}=C_{Z}/2\cdot n^{kt+k^{\prime}/2}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)\cdot\left(n^{\prime}\right)^{k^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+1)/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=n^{-kt/2-\eta}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)\cdot\left(n^{\prime}\right)^{-k^{\prime}t^{\prime}/4-\eta}

for
t^{\prime}=\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t
. This concludes the proof of Eq. (
21
).

Case 2:
2kt>k^{\prime}
(dense).
In this case
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq 2B_{Z}\gg n^{k^{\prime}}
, i.e., the number of recorder observations in
\mathcal{Z}
significantly exceeds the number of distinct index sets in
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
. By binomial concentration (Prop.
6.1
), for all
\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
, simultaneously with high probability,

\big|\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}\big|\geq\widetilde{\Theta}(1)\cdot B_{Z}n^{-k^{\prime}}=C_{obs}\cdot n^{kt-k^{\prime}/2}\eqcolon B_{obs}

(24)

for
C_{obs}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
hiding constants depending on
k,a
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors. Denote the corresponding event
S_{obs}=\left\{\forall\gamma,\big|\mathsf{Z}_{\gamma}^{obs}\big|\geq B_{obs}\right\}
,
\Pr[S_{obs}^{c}]=o(1)
.
Given
S_{obs}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathsf{MAJ}\big(\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}\big)\right)\right\}_{\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}}\,,

where each majority is taken over exactly
B_{obs}
observations. In this case,

\mathbb{E}\,\mathsf{MAJ}\big(\mathsf{Z}^{obs}_{\gamma}\big)=x_{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\Theta}\big(\delta^{2}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{2}\left(kt-k^{\prime}/2\right)}\big)=x_{\gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\Theta}\big(\left(\kappa n\right)^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta}\big)\eqcolon x_{\gamma}\cdot\delta^{\prime}\,,

which shows that (see Fig.
6
for the definition of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{wor}}
) with
M=\binom{\kappa n}{k^{\prime}}
,

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\cap S_{obs})=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{wor}}(\kappa n,M,\delta^{\prime})\,.

Then, conditioning on a high probability event in total variation (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{wor}}(\kappa n,M,\delta^{\prime})\right)=d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\cap S_{obs})\right)=1-\Pr\left[S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\cap S_{obs}\right]=o_{n}(1)\,,

and therefore, Alg.
1
is an average-case reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(\kappa n)
.
Finally, by Prop.
5.6
,
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
(Alg.
19
) is an average-case reduction from
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(\kappa n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(\kappa n)
, concluding the proof.

7.3
Equation Combination for Two Input Tensors of Different Orders

As described in
§​​ 2.5
, the equation combination identity of Eq. (
19
) can be extended to accept as an input two instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\sim k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{1},\eta_{1}}(n)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{2},\eta_{2}}(n)
that share a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
. Our use case for such a reduction is the order-reducing reduction (
§​​ 12
), for which it suffices to consider
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
. Lemma
7.2
is a generalization of Lemma
7.1
for this case.
30
30

30

Similar result can be established for general
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,1)
using analogous techniques.

Lemma 7.2

(Extended Discrete Equation Combination Reduction)
.

Given independent instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\sim k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=0,\eta_{1}}(n)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t=0,\eta_{2}}(n)
that share a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, Alg.
2
outputs

\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{DiscrEqCombTwo}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)},k^{\prime}\right)
, where

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{Z},k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\,,

where
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{k^{\prime}/2})
and
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-\eta_{1}/2-\eta_{2}/2})
, if
(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2
is an integer and
k^{\prime}
satisfies

k^{\prime}\in\left[|k_{1}-k_{2}|,\min\left\{k_{1},k_{2}\right\}\right]\,.

Moreover, Alg.
2
maps two instances with the same secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}
for
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
.

Remark
7.2
similarly applies.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}^{(a)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{(a)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(a)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(a)}\right]}
for
a\in\left\{1,2\right\}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(a)}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j}^{(a)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(a)}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{a}}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

Output:

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,
\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}
.

1

//
Prune the input equations; only consider index sets
\alpha_{j}^{(a)}
that contain exactly
(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2
coordinates in
\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]
, denote them as the "cancellation set"
\mathcal{C}(j)

\mathsf{S}^{(a)}=\left\{j:\,j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(a)}\right]\text{ and }|\alpha_{j}^{(a)}\cap\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]|=(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2\right\}
for
a\in\left\{1,2\right\}

2

For
a\in\left\{1,2\right\}
and each
j\in\mathsf{S}^{(a)}
,
\mathcal{C}^{(a)}(j)\leftarrow\alpha_{j}^{(a)}\cap\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]

3

//
Aggregate entries with the same cancellation set
\beta

For each
\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2}
, let

\mathcal{N}^{(a)}(\beta)=\left\{j\in\left[\mathsf{S}^{(a)}\right]:\,\mathcal{C}^{(a)}(j)=\beta\right\}\text{ for }a\in\left\{1,2\right\}

4

//
Form disjoint combination pairs and record them

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

5

forall

\beta\text{ s.t. }\big|\mathcal{N}^{(1)}(\beta)\big|\geq 1
and
\big|\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(\beta)\big|\geq 1

do

6

s\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\mathcal{N}^{(1)}(\beta)\right]
,
t\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(\beta)\right]

7

if

|\alpha_{s}^{(1)}\triangle\alpha_{t}^{(2)}|=k^{\prime}

then

8

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\left\{\left(\alpha_{s}^{(1)}\triangle\alpha_{t}^{(2)},\mathsf{Y}_{s}^{(1)}\mathsf{Y}_{t}^{(2)}\right)\right\}

9

\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\leftarrow\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z})
,
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}=\min\big\{\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}},|\mathcal{Z}|\big\}

//
See pf. of Lem.
7.2
for defn of
B_{Z}

10

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\leftarrow\text{ random }m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\text{-subset of }\mathcal{Z}

11

Return
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}

12

Algorithm 2

\mathsf{DiscrEqCombTwo}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)},k^{\prime},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n\right)

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

The reduction (Alg.
2
) is a generalization of the discrete equation combination reduction (Alg.
1
) and its analysis for both planted and null distributions closely follows that of the Alg.
1
in Lemma
7.1
. The only differences are:

1.

If the two input instances had noise levels
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
, the output will have i.i.d.
\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\right)
entry-wise noise – this yields the
\eta^{\prime}=\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}
deficiency in the output instance.

2.

The cancellation vector is now
(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2
sparse, which is guaranteed to be
\leq\min\left\{k_{1},k_{2}\right\}
by the condition
k^{\prime}\geq|k_{1}-k_{2}|
.

3.

In Step 2, we similarly prove
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq 2B_{Z}
with high probability, but now for
B_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}/2}\right)
. In particular, we have

\big|\mathcal{N}(\beta)^{(1)}\big|\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k_{1}/2}\cdot n^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2}\right)=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}/2-k_{2}/2}\right)\right)\,,

and an analogous condition for
\mathcal{N}(\beta)^{(2)}
.
We have
k^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{k_{1},k_{2}\right\}
, in which case
|\mathcal{Z}|\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(\binom{n(1-\kappa)}{(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2},\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}-k_{1}/2-k_{2}/2}\right)\right)
. By binomial concentration, with high probability,

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq C_{Z}\cdot n^{k^{\prime}/2}=2B_{Z}\,.

for
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
. Note that
B_{Z}
is the desired number of output equations up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors.

4.

We only consider the sparse case for the regime of interest
t=0
.

∎

8
Discrete Equation Combination for
k
-Sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, via the natural
\mathbb{F}_{2}\leftrightarrow\left\{\pm 1\right\}
map, the entries are evidently noisy linear equations over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
. In this alternative view, in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
we have a signal
x\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{n}
and observe
m
equations over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
of the form

x_{i_{1}}+x_{i_{2}}+\dots+x_{i_{k}}+e=\mathsf{Y}\quad\left(\mathsf{mod}\ 2\right)\,,

(25)

where
e
is i.i.d. noise and
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is a uniformly random index set from
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
. Our
\mathsf{EqComb}
step adds (or, equivalently, subtracts) two equations whose index sets overlap, thereby canceling the variables that appear twice.

Noisy linear equations over larger rings
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
are central in both cryptography and average-case complexity. The
k
-sparse LWE problem (for constant
k
), i.e., the analog of (
25
) over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, was first formalized in
[
53
]
: one observes
m
samples of the form

a_{1}x_{i_{1}}+a_{2}x_{i_{2}}+\dots+a_{k}x_{i_{k}}+e=\mathsf{Y}\,,

(26)

where
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
is the secret,
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is a uniformly random index set,
e\sim\chi\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
is i.i.d. noise, and the coefficients
a_{s}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0\right)
.

We extend our discrete equation combination reduction (Lem.
7.1
) to
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
in
§​​ 8.1
. We present a general combination-based reduction for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
in Lemma
8.1
that succeeds whenever the entrywise noise distribution
\chi
is
combination-stable
(Def.
8.2
). A noise distribution
\chi
is cobination-stable if it is closed under subtraction, i.e., subtracting two equations of the form (
26
) yields an equation of
k^{\prime}
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
for
k^{\prime}=2k-2a
for
a=(\text{\# of canceled indices})
, possibly with a change in the noise parameter.

In
§​​ 8.3
,
§​​ 8.4
, and
§​​ 8.5
, we apply the general combination-based reduction for three common
\mathsf{LWE}
noise models:

•

Uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{\delta}
if

e=\begin{cases}0,&\text{w. prob. }\delta\\
\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right),&\text{w. prob. }1-\delta\,.\end{cases}

•

Discrete Gaussian Noise:

\chi_{s}(e)\propto\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi(e+iq)^{2}/s^{2}}\,.

•

Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:

e\sim\chi_{l}
,
where
\chi_{l}
is a given measure on
\left[-l,l\right]
for a parameter
l\leq q
.

Finally, for the three noise models above we present a simple detection algorithm for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
based on equation collision in
§​​ 8.2
; combined with our reduction this yields new detection algorithms for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
.
We do not attempt an exhaustive exploration of equation combination for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
. For instance, one can relax the equation combination requirement that coefficients cancel exactly on the support overlap; we leave this and other directions for future work. More generally, the computational complexity of
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
remains open: so far, no plausible conjectures have been proposed regarding existence of polynomial-time algorithms and how this depends on number of samples.

We now formally define the
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
(
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
) model as a
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
analog of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
. Here we will call a vector
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}

k
-sparse
if it has exactly
k
nonzero coordinates, i.e.,
|\mathsf{supp}(c)|=k
. For simplicity we assume
q
is prime, which allows better parameters tradeoffs in the case of the uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
noise in Lemmas
8.2
and
8.3
. For general odd
q
, the uniform noise detection thresholds incur a factor of
\sqrt{q-1}
31
31

31

The condition in Lemma
8.2
becomes
m\delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{(k-1)/2}\right)
with
n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\lesssim n\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k}
.
, while the equation combination reduction for uniform noise no longer improves over a general reduction; all other results (Lemmas
8.1
,
8.2
,
8.3
,
8.4
, and Cor.
8.3
) hold without modification.

Definition 8.1

(
k
-Sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
(
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
))
.

Let
k,n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
q
prime, and
\chi
be a distribution on
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. In the (planted)
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi)}
, there is a signal vector
x\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\right)
and we observe a collection of equations
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, where

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\langle c_{j},x\rangle+e_{j}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
)

where
e_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\chi
is
entrywise noise
and the coefficient vector
c_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
has uniformly random
k
-sparse support

\alpha_{j}\eqcolon\mathsf{supp}(c_{j})=\left\{i\in\left[n\right]:\,\left(c_{j}\right)_{i}\neq 0\right\}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]

and satisfies
\left(c_{j}\right)_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Unif}\left[(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0)\right],&i\in\alpha_{j}\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

In the null distribution of
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
(
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{NULL}}{(n,m)}
), for all
j\in\left[m\right]
,
c_{j}
is generated as above, and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right)\,.

Remark 8.1
.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
we think of
n
as the main dimensionality parameter,
k
and
\log_{n}m
as constants, and
q\leq n^{O(1)}
.

Remark 8.2

(Equation Sampling Procedures)
.

Similarly to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
(see
§​​ 5.1
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variants overview), the model above with exactly
m
equations is computationally equivalent to the one with
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
equations, which we denote
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n,m,\chi)}
. In particular, there exist average-case reductions in both directions that preserve
m
up to constant factors (see Alg.
12
,
13
). There also exists a trivial reduction from the model above to the one that samples
k
-sparse coefficient vectors
c_{j}
independently
without replacement
, but we are not aware of a reverse reduction that would succeed in all parameter regimes (see discussion in
§​​ 5.1.4
).

Similarly to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, our reduction results for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
(Def.
8.1
) hold without modification for the sampling-without-replacement model (where both the input and the output instance have the same sampling procedure).
32
32

32

The algorithms require small technical modifications to match the distribution in the different sampling model.
Our computational threshold heuristic, however, only exists for the sampling-with-replacement model.

8.1
General Equation Combination Reduction for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}

Definition 8.2

(Combination-Stable Noise Models for
\mathsf{LWE}
)
.

Let
\chi_{\theta}
be a noise distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
parametrized by
\theta\in\Theta
. We say
\chi_{\theta}
is
(f,g)
-combination-stable
for deterministic functions
f:\Theta\to\Theta
and
g:\Theta\times\mathbb{Z}^{+}\to\Theta
if

1.

For
e_{1},e_{2}\sim\chi_{\theta}
independent,

e=e_{1}-e_{2}\mod q\sim\chi_{\theta^{\prime}}

for
\theta^{\prime}=f(\theta)
.

2.

Given a secret
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
N
samples
s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
:

s_{i}=s+e_{i}\,,\quad e_{i}\sim\chi_{\theta}\,,

there is a poly-time algorithm
\mathcal{A}_{N}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}
that for every
N
, outputs
\hat{s}=\mathcal{A}_{N}(s_{1},\dots,s_{N})
such that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\hat{s},s+\chi_{\theta^{\prime}}\right)=n^{-C}\,,

for an arbitrarily large constant
C
and
\theta^{\prime}=g(\theta,N)
.

Remark 8.3
.

Note that the second condition in Def.
8.2
can be trivially satisfied with
\mathcal{A}_{N}\left(s_{1},\dots,s_{N}\right)=s_{1}
and
g\left(\theta,N\right)=\theta
. We aim for
g
that “decrease noise” with larger
N
.

Lemma 8.1

(General Discrete Equation Combination for
\mathsf{LWE}
)
.

Fix
k,n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},\theta\in\Theta
and let
\chi_{\theta}
be a
(f,g)
-combination-stable distribution over
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
(Def.
8.2
). For any even integer
k^{\prime}\leq 2k
, there exists an average-case reduction
33
33

33

Average-case reductions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
are formally defined in Def.
C.6
; the definition for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
is analogous: reduction algorithm outputs an instance
\mathcal{Z}
such that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\right)=o_{n}(1)
, which has algorithmic and hardness implications analogous to Lemma
C.3
.
for both detection and recovery (Alg.
3
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
and parameters
m^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}
depend on
\theta
and the input number of samples
m
as follows. Denote
n_{\mathsf{eff}}=n(q-1)
and assume
t=\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m
is a fixed constant;

•

If
t\leq\min\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\prime}=f(\theta)\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4},k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\prime}=g\left(f(\theta),\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-k-k^{\prime}/2}\right)\right)\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},k^{\prime}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\prime}=f(\theta)\,;

•

If
t>\max\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},k^{\prime}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\prime}=g\left(f(\theta),\widetilde{\Theta}\left(mn_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-k^{\prime}}\right)\right)\,.

Moreover, Alg.
3
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

Similarly to the
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
in Lemma
7.1
, Lemma
8.1
splits into regimes depending on the number of available combination pairs. In the regimes where each
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
equation is observed only
O(1)
times (bullets 1 and 3), the reduction outputs these observations yielding
\theta^{\prime}=f(\theta)
. In the regimes where for each
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
equation many copies are observed (bullets 2 and 4), the reduction additionally applies the “aggregation” procedure
\mathcal{A}_{N}
from Def.
8.2
, yielding
\theta^{\prime}=g(f(\theta),N)
for an appropriate
N
.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\mathcal{A}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{N}:\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{N}\to\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right\}
,
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

Output:

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\big\{\big(c^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\big\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,
\big(c^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\kappa n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

1

//
Prune the input equations; only consider those with exactly
k^{\prime}/2
nonzero coefficients in
\left[\kappa n\right]

\mathsf{S}=\left\{j:\,j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]\text{ and }|\mathsf{supp}(c_{j})\cap\left[\kappa n\right]|=k^{\prime}/2\right\}

2

//
Denote the restriction of
c_{j}
on
\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]
as a cancellation vector
\mathcal{C}(j)

For each
j\in\mathsf{S}
,

\mathcal{C}(j)\leftarrow\left(c_{j}\right)_{\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]}

3

//
Aggregate entries with same
(k-k^{\prime}/2)
-sparse cancellation vector
\beta

For each
(k-k^{\prime}/2)
-sparse
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(1-\kappa)n}
, let
\mathcal{N}(\beta)=\left\{j\in\left[\mathsf{S}\right]:\,\mathcal{C}(j)=\beta\right\}

4

//
Form disjoint combination pairs and record them

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

5

forall

(k-k^{\prime}/2)
-sparse
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(1-\kappa)n}

do

6

while

\big|\mathcal{N}(\beta)\big|\geq 2

do

7

8

|\mathsf{supp}\left(c_{s}-c_{t}\right)|=k^{\prime}

10

11

if

\log_{n(q-1)}B_{Z}\leq k^{\prime}

then

12

Sparse case -- few observations in
\mathcal{Z}

,

//
See pf. of Lem.

for defn of

\mathcal{Z}

For each
