Algorithm 3

\mathsf{DiscrEqCombLWE}\left(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},\mathcal{A},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n\right)

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

The reduction is Alg.
3
, which is a direct analogue of the
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
reduction in Alg.
1
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
. Here we highlight the main proof steps and differences from the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

As mentioned in Remark
8.2
, Alg.
12
and
13
are average-case reductions from
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n,m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta})}
and vice versa, preserving the number of samples
m
up to constants. Therefore, it is sufficient to show that Alg.
3
is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=\kappa n,m^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
8.1
.
We present the proof for the planted case and the same argument applies to the null case. We show that for an input
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n,m,\chi_{\theta})},m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
, the output of Alg.
3

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathsf{DiscrEqCombLWE}(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n,\mathcal{A})
satisfies

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}})\approx_{d_{\mathsf{TV}}=o(1)}k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\,.

(27)

Here,
\mathcal{A}
is the “aggregation” algorithm for
\chi_{\theta}
from the definition of combination-stable distribution Def.
8.2
. In this proof, we denote
n_{\mathsf{eff}}=n(q-1)
: in
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
we have
\Theta\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k}\right)
number of possible coefficient vectors.

Step 1: Distribution of elements in
\mathcal{Z}
.

We show that
\mathcal{Z}=\big\{(c^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j})\big\}_{j\in\left[|\mathcal{Z}|\right]}
, which is the set constructed in Lines 4-8 of Alg.
3
, contains elements
(c^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\kappa n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}
distributed as i.i.d. entries of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
with noise distribution
\chi_{f(\theta)}
, where recall that we assumed
\chi
is
(f,g)
-combination-stable.

The proof is analogous to Step 1 in Lemma
7.1
. The recorded value
\mathsf{Y}_{s}-\mathsf{Y}_{t}
has noise distribution
\chi_{f(\theta)}
, which follows from the definition of combination-stable noise distribution (Def.
8.2
). Moreover, since the coefficient vectors
c_{s},c_{t}
are uniformly random
k
-sparse and satisfy
\mathsf{supp}\left(c_{s}-c_{t}\right)=\mathsf{supp}\left(\left(c_{s}-c_{t}\right)_{[1:\kappa n]}\right)=k^{\prime}
, the resulting coefficient vectors
c^{\prime}_{j}=\left(c_{s}-c_{t}\right)_{[1:\kappa n]}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\kappa n}
are uniformly random
k^{\prime}
-sparse:

(c^{\prime}_{j})_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Unif}\left[(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0)\right],&i\in\mathsf{supp}(c_{j}^{\prime})\\
0,&\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

Similarly to Lemma
7.1
, since the input equations are not reused in the combination pairs, the resulting entries
(c^{\prime}_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j})
are jointly independent.

Step 2: Bound on
|\mathcal{Z}|
.

We prove that with high probability the number of recorded equations
|\mathcal{Z}|
satisfies the lower bound

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}=\begin{cases}C_{Z}\cdot m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)},&\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m\leq k-k^{\prime}/2\\
C_{Z}\cdot m,&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

where
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
hides constants depending on
k
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors; denote the corresponding event

S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}=\left\{|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}\right\}\,,\qquad\Pr\left[S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}^{c}\right]=o(1)\,.

In particular, from Poisson splitting, for each
(k-k^{\prime}/2)
-sparse
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{(1-\kappa)n}
(the cancellation vector), we have

|\mathcal{N}\left(\beta\right)|\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(\Theta\left(mn_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)\right)\,.

In the case
\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m\leq k-k^{\prime}/2
, each cancellation vector
\beta
has
O(1)
equations in its set
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
. Then, each set produces a combination pair with probability
\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}\cdot n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-2(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)
, so

|\mathcal{Z}|\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(\binom{n(1-\kappa)}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\cdot(q-1)^{k-k^{\prime}/2},\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}\cdot n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-2(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)\right)\,,

and by Binomial concentration in Prop.
6.1
, with high probability,

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq C_{Z}\cdot m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\eqcolon B_{Z}\,,

for
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

Otherwise, by Poisson concentration in Prop.
6.2
, with high probability, simultaneously for all
\beta
,
|\mathcal{N}(\beta)|\geq\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{N}(\beta)|\right)=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(mn_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)
. In this case, each
\mathcal{N}(\beta)
yields
\Theta\left(|\mathcal{N}(\beta)|\right)
combination pairs, so

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq C_{Z}\cdot m\eqcolon B_{Z}\,,

for
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
.

Step 3: Distribution of the output set
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
.

Here we conclude the proof by showing

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\right)=o_{n}(1)\,,

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
is the output of the Alg.
1
. This is handled differently in the
sparse regime
, when the number of observed
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
equations
B_{Z}
is smaller than the number of all
k^{\prime}
-sparse coefficient vectors
\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)
, and the
dense regime
, when
B_{Z}
is much larger than
\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)
and the aggregation step is required.

Case 1:
\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}B_{Z}\leq{k^{\prime}}
(sparse).
The remaining step of Alg.
3
in Lines 11-13 chooses a random
\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(B_{Z}/2)
subset of
\mathcal{Z}
if
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}
. Given
S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}
, i.e.,
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}
, this happens with high probability by Poisson concentration (Prop.
6.2
). Denoting this event
S_{\mathsf{Pois}}
, we observe that

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{\mathsf{Pois}}\cap S_{|\mathcal{Z}|})=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\,,

for
\theta^{\prime}=f(\theta)
and
m^{\prime}=B_{Z}/2
. By conditioning on the high probability event in total variation (Fact
6.1
), we obtain

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}},k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\right)\leq 1-\Pr\left[S_{\mathsf{Pois}}\right]=o(1)\,.

The condition
\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}B_{Z}\leq{k^{\prime}}
corresponds to bullets 1 and 3 in the Lemma
8.1
statement.

Case 2:
\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}B_{Z}>{k^{\prime}}
(dense).
We introduce a slight modification to the argument in Lemma
7.1
by skipping the intermediate reduction to
k^{\prime}\text{-}\mathsf{tPCA}
and map directly to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}
. Let
C_{\mathsf{Pois}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
be such that

\Pr[\mathsf{Pois}(1)\leq C_{\mathsf{Pois}}]\geq 1-n^{-K}\,,

for some large fixed constant
K
. The remaining steps of Alg.
3
in Lines 17-22 produce exactly
m_{c^{\prime}}
output equations with
k^{\prime}
-sparse coefficient vector
c^{\prime}
, and by above, simultaneously with high probability for all
c^{\prime}
,
m_{c^{\prime}}\leq C_{\mathsf{Pois}}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{Z}
contains uniformly random
k^{\prime}
-sparse equations, given
S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}
,
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}
and for all
k^{\prime}
-sparse
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\kappa n}
, simultaneously with high probability,

\big|\mathsf{Z}_{c^{\prime}}^{obs}\big|\geq\widetilde{\Theta}\left(B_{Z}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-k^{\prime}}\right)=C_{Pois}\cdot C_{obs}\cdot B_{Z}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-k^{\prime}}\eqcolon C_{Pois}\cdot B_{obs}

(28)

for
C_{obs}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
hiding constants depending on
k
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors. Denote the event above as

S_{obs}=\left\{\forall k^{\prime}\text{-sparse }c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{\kappa n}\,,\big|\mathsf{Z}_{c^{\prime}}^{obs}\big|\geq C_{Pois}\cdot B_{obs}\right\}\,.

Given
S_{|\mathcal{Z}|},S_{obs}
, the output
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}
contains
m_{c^{\prime}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(1)
equations for every
k^{\prime}
-sparse coefficient vector
c^{\prime}
with a noise distribution
\chi_{\theta^{\prime}}
, where

\theta^{\prime}=g\left(f(\theta),B_{obs}\right)\,.

Then,

\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}|S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\cap S_{obs})=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=\kappa n
and
m^{\prime}=\binom{\kappa n}{k^{\prime}}(q-1)^{k^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)
. By conditioning on the high probability event in total variation (Fact
6.1
), we obtain

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}},k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{Pois}}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\theta^{\prime}})}\right)\leq 1-\Pr\left[S_{|\mathcal{Z}|}\cap S_{obs}\right]=o(1)\,.

The condition
\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}B_{Z}>{k^{\prime}}
corresponds to bullets 2 and 4 in Lemma
8.1
.
∎

8.2
Collision-Based Detection Algorithm for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m\right]
,
\big(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}\times\mathbb{Z}_{q}
,
T\in\mathbb{R}
.

Output:

\mathsf{ANS}\in\left\{\mathsf{PLANTED},\mathsf{NULL}\right\}
.

1

//
Aggregate entries with the same index set and coefficient vector

For all
k
-sparse
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
,

\mathcal{N}\left(c\right)\leftarrow\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\,c=c_{j}\right\}

2

//
Examine the Collisions

C_{1},C_{0}\leftarrow 0

3

forall

k
-sparse
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}

do

4

while

|\mathcal{N}\left(c\right)|\geq 2

do

5

select
s\neq t
from
\mathcal{N}\left(c\right)

6

a\leftarrow\mathds{1}\left\{\mathsf{Y}_{s}=\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right\}

7

C_{a}\leftarrow C_{a}+1

8

\mathcal{N}\left(c\right)\leftarrow\mathcal{N}\left(c\right)\setminus\left\{s,t\right\}

9

Return
\mathsf{PLANTED}

if

\frac{C_{1}}{C_{0}+C_{1}}\geq T

else

\mathsf{NULL}

10

Algorithm 4

\mathsf{CollisionDetection}\left(\mathcal{Y},T\right)

The collision-based detection algorithm (Alg.
4
) accepts
m
equations
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
and finds disjoint “collisions”, i.e., pairs
s,t\in\left[m\right]
satisfying

c_{s}=c_{t}\,.

The algorithm then computes the portion of such pairs
s,t
for which
\mathsf{Y}_{s}=\mathsf{Y}_{t}
(as it would have been in a noiseless setting) and returns
\mathsf{PLANTED}
if the portion is sufficiently large and
\mathsf{NULL}
otherwise.

Lemma 8.2

(
\mathsf{CollisionDetection}
Guarantees)
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}
be drawn from one of the two distributions:

\displaystyle H_{1}:\quad\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi)}

\displaystyle H_{0}:\quad\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}^{\mathsf{NULL}}{(n,m)}\,.

Then there exists a threshold
T=T(k,n,m,\chi)
such that
\mathsf{ANS}=\mathsf{CollisionDetection}\left(\mathcal{Y},T\right)
(Alg.
4
) can be computed in
O(\mathrm{poly}(n,q))
time and satisfies

\Pr_{\mathcal{Y}\sim H_{0}}\left[\mathsf{ANS}=\mathsf{PLANTED}\right]+\Pr_{\mathcal{Y}\sim H_{1}}\left[\mathsf{ANS}=\mathsf{NULL}\right]\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty

if the following conditions are met:

•

Uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise
\chi_{\delta}
for
\delta=O(1/q)
:

n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k-1}
and

m\delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2-1}\right)\,;

•

Discrete Gaussian Noise
\chi_{s}
for
s\leq qn^{-\varepsilon},\varepsilon>0
:

n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\cdot\max\left\{\sqrt{s},1\right\}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k}\,;

•

Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise:
for
\chi_{l}
supported on
\left[-l,l\right]
,
n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\left(\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}-1/q\right)^{-1/2}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k}
, and

m\left(\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}-1/q\right)=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{(k-1)/2}\right)\,,

where
\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{e\in\left[-l,l\right]}\chi_{l}(e)^{2}
.

In the above
\widetilde{\Omega}
hides constants in
k
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors.

We now provide proof sketches for the correctness of Alg.
4
in the three noise settings.

Uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise.

In the uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
noise
e\sim\chi_{\delta}
if

e=\begin{cases}0,&\text{w. prob. }\delta\\
\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right),&\text{w. prob. }1-\delta\,,\end{cases}

where
\delta=O(1/q)
.
Denote
\mu_{c}
to be the law of the observed equation (

⁢
k
-
LWE

) with a
k
-sparse coefficient vector
c
. Then, multiplying
\mathsf{Y}_{j}\sim\mu_{c_{j}}
by a nonzero
s\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
preserves the noise distribution and yields

\displaystyle s\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\langle sc_{j},x\rangle+se_{j}\sim\mu_{sc_{j}}\,.

Then, under the uniform noise model, we can assume that for all
j\in\left[m\right]
we have
\left(c_{j}\right)_{\min\mathsf{supp}\left(c_{j}\right)}=1
(i.e., we can rescale
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
by
\left(c_{j}\right)_{\min\mathsf{supp}\left(c_{j}\right)}^{-1}
). Then, each of the
m
equations has a coefficient vector drawn uniformly out of
\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k}(q-1)^{k-1}\right)
options. A direct calculation then gives that, if
m=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{k}(q-1)^{k-1}\right)
, with high probability,

C_{1}+C_{0}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n^{k}(q-1)^{k-1}}\right)\eqcolon N\,.

For our algorithm to succeed, we require
N/q\gg 1
, translating into
m=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\right)
.
Under the two hypothesis distributions we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{PLANTED}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,\delta^{2}(1-1/q)+1/q\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{NULL}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,1/q\right)\,.

From the binomial concentration (Prop.
6.1
), if we set the threshold
T=1/q+\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{1/(Nq)}\right)
, Alg.
4
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathsf{PLANTED}
vs
\mathsf{NULL}
with high probability whenever
N\delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\sqrt{N/q}\right)
, or, equivalently,

m\delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2-1}\right)\,.

(29)

For
q=2
, which is the case of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, and any
q=O\left(\mathrm{poly}\log n\right)
, Eq. (
29
) matches the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
computational threshold (
Comp. Thr.
) up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors.

Discrete Gaussian Noise.

In the discrete Gaussian noise,

\chi_{s}(e)\propto\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi(e+iq)^{2}/s^{2}}\,,\quad e\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}\,,

where
1\leq s\leq qn^{-\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
.
Since there are
\binom{n}{k}
potential values for index sets
\alpha_{j}=\mathsf{supp}\left(c_{j}\right)
and
(q-1)^{k}
values for the nonzero entries of
c_{j}
, if
m=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{k}(q-1)^{k}\right)
, with high probability,

C_{1}+C_{0}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n^{k}(q-1)^{k}}\right)\eqcolon N\,.

A direct calculation gives that under the two hypothesis distributions we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{PLANTED}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,\Theta(1/s)\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{NULL}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,1/q\right)\,.

From the binomial concentration (Prop.
6.1
), if we set the threshold
T=1/q+\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{1/(Nq)}\right)
, Alg.
4
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathsf{PLANTED}
vs
\mathsf{NULL}
with high probability whenever
N=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\max\left\{1,s\right\}\right)
, where
\widetilde{\Omega}(1)
hides
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors and constants, since
s\ll q
. Equivalently,

n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\cdot\max\left\{\sqrt{s},1\right\}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k}\,.

Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise.

The bounded noise
\chi_{l}
is supported on
\left[-l,l\right]
, so, denoting
\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}=\sum_{e\in\left[-l,l\right]}\chi_{l}(e)^{2}
, we have the probability that two samples from
\chi_{l}
collide equal to
\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}
. By the argument analogous to above, in Alg.
4
, if
m=\widetilde{O}\left(n^{k}(q-1)^{k}\right)
, with high probability,

C_{1}+C_{0}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\frac{m^{2}}{n^{k}(q-1)^{k}}\right)\eqcolon N\,,

and therefore,

\displaystyle\mathsf{PLANTED}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\mathsf{NULL}:\quad

\displaystyle C_{1}\sim\mathsf{Bin}\left(N,1/q\right)\,.

From the binomial concentration (Prop.
6.1
), if we set the threshold
T=1/q+\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{1/(Nq)}\right)
, Alg.
4
succeeds at distinguishing
\mathsf{PLANTED}
vs
\mathsf{NULL}
with high probability whenever
N\left(\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}-1/q\right)=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\max\left\{1,\sqrt{N/q}\right\}\right)
, or, equivalently,

m\left(\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}-1/q\right)=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{(k-1)/2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2}\left(\|\chi_{l}\|^{2}_{2}-1/q\right)^{-1/2}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k}(q-1)^{k}\,.

8.3
Equation Combination Reduction for Uniform
\mathsf{LWE}
Noise

In this section we give an improved equation combination reduction for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
with uniform noise in the case of prime
q
(Lemma
8.3
). Compared to the general result in Lemma
8.1
, it gains
(q-1)
factors for the number of equations in the output instance. This is achieved by observing that, similarly to the detection algorithm in
§​​ 8.2
, we can assume that in the input instance
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(c_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, the coefficient vectors
c_{j}
are defined up to a multiplicative factor in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}\setminus 0
.

Proposition 8.1

(Uniform
\mathsf{LWE}
Noise is combination-stable)
.

Let
\chi_{\delta}
be the uniform
\mathsf{LWE}
noise, i.e.,
e\sim\chi_{\delta}
if for
\delta=O(1/q)
,

e=\begin{cases}0,&\text{w. prob. }\delta\\
\mathsf{Unif}\left(\mathbb{Z}_{q}\right),&\text{w. prob. }1-\delta\,.\end{cases}

Then,
\chi_{\delta}
is
(f,g)
-combination-stable, where

f(\delta)=\delta^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad g(\delta,N)=\begin{cases}\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{Nq}\delta\right)\,,&\widetilde{\Omega}(1)\leq Nq\leq\delta^{-2}\\
\delta\,,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\widetilde{\Theta}
hides constants and
\mathrm{poly}\log\left(Nq\right)
factors.

Proof.

f(\delta)=\delta^{2}
can be verified via a direct calculation;
g(\delta,N)
is achieved by a majority vote whenever the number of samples is sufficiently large.
∎

Lemma 8.3

(Discrete Equation Combination for
\mathsf{LWE}
with Uniform Noise)
.

Fix
k,n,m,q\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},\delta=O(1/q)\in(0,1)
and let
\chi_{\delta}
be uniform
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
noise.

For any even integer
k^{\prime}<2k
, there exists an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (Alg.
3
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{\delta})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{\delta^{\prime}})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
and parameters
m^{\prime},\theta^{\prime}
depend on
\theta
and the input number of samples
m
as follows. Denote
\mathcal{Q}=\log_{n}(q-1)
and assume
t=\log_{n}m
is a fixed constant;

•

If
t\leq\min\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}+\left(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}-1\right)\mathcal{Q},\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}+\left(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}-1\right)\mathcal{Q}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}n^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}(q-1)^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2-1)}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}+\left(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}-1\right)\mathcal{Q},k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}+\left(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}-1\right)\mathcal{Q}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}}(q-1)^{k^{\prime}-1}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\delta^{2}mn^{-(k/2+k^{\prime}/4)}(q-1)^{-(k/2+k^{\prime}/4-3/2)}\right)\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}+\left(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}-1\right)\mathcal{Q},k^{\prime}+(k^{\prime}-1)\mathcal{Q}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}\,;

•

If
t>\max\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}+\left(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}-1\right)\mathcal{Q},k^{\prime}+(k^{\prime}-1)\mathcal{Q}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}}(q-1)^{k^{\prime}-1}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\delta^{2}m^{1/2}n^{-k^{\prime}/2}(q-1)^{-(k^{\prime}/2-1)}\right)\,.

Moreover, Alg.
3
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

Combined with results in Lemma
8.2
, our average-case reductions (specifically, second bullet point) yield new algorithms for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
with uniform noise that improve upon Lemma
8.2
in a specific parameter regime by a factor of
\sqrt{q-1}
.

Corollary 8.1

(Detection Algorithms for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
with Uniform Noise)
.

There exists a
O(\mathrm{poly}(n,q))
-time algorithm solving the detection task for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
with uniform noise
\chi_{\delta}
if
k\geq 6
and for some even integer
k^{\prime}\geq 2
,
n^{k/2+k^{\prime}/4}(q-1)^{k/2+k^{\prime}/4-1}\lesssim m\lesssim n^{k-k^{\prime}/2}(q-1)^{k-k^{\prime}/2-1}
and

m\delta^{2}=\widetilde{\Omega}\left(n^{k/2}(q-1)^{k/2-3/2}\right)\,.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

The reduction is Alg.
3
with one modification. For each
j\in\mathsf{S}
, we first modify
c_{j}\leftarrow c_{j}\cdot\left(c_{j}\right)_{\min\left\{\mathsf{supp}c_{j}\cap\left[\kappa n+1:n\right]\right\}}^{-1}
, i.e., ensure that the smallest nonzero coordinate in the cancellation vector of
c_{j}
is equal to
1
. This decreases the space of all cancellation vectors to have size
\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}(q-1)^{k-k^{\prime}/2-1}
compared to
\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}(q-1)^{k-k^{\prime}/2}
in Lemma
8.1
, which in turn increases the number of combination pairs in the reduction. The proof follows the proof of Lemma
8.1
with one key modification: the bound on
|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}
. An argument analogous to that in Lemma
8.1
shows that, with high probability,

|\mathcal{Z}|\geq B_{Z}=\begin{cases}C_{Z}\cdot m^{2}n^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}(q-1)^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2-1)},&\text{if }\log_{n}m\leq k-k^{\prime}/2+(k-k^{\prime}/2-1)\mathcal{Q}\\
C_{Z}\cdot m,&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

where
C_{Z}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
. The sparse and dense cases of Lemma
8.1
correspond to
\log_{n}B_{Z}\leq k^{\prime}+(k^{\prime}-1)\mathcal{Q}
and
\log_{n}B_{Z}>k^{\prime}+(k^{\prime}-1)\mathcal{Q}
. With the rest of the proof unchanged, this yields the bounds in Lemma
8.3
.
∎

8.4
Equation Combination Reduction for Discrete Gaussian Noise

Proposition 8.2

(Discrete Gaussian is combination-stable)
.

Let
\chi_{s}
be discrete Gaussian distribution in
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for odd
q>2
, i.e.,
\forall e\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
,

\chi_{s}(e)\propto\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-\pi(e+iq)^{2}/s^{2}}\,,

where
s\leq qn^{-\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon>0
.
Then,
\chi_{s}
is
(f,g)
-combination-stable, where

f(s)=\sqrt{2}s\quad\text{and}\quad g(s,N)=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(s/\sqrt{N}\right)\,.

Proof.

f(s)=\sqrt{2}s
can be verified via a direct calculation;
g(s,N)=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(s/\sqrt{N}\right)
can be achieved as follows. The key identity is that given
s_{1},s_{2}\sim\chi_{s}
independent,

\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{s_{1}+s_{2}}{2}\,\Big|\,s_{1}\equiv s_{2}\mod{2}\right)=\chi_{s/\sqrt{2}}\,,

(30)

proved in
[
2
,
76
]
. Iterative application of (
30
) achieves the desired
g(s,N)
.
∎

Plugging the parameters in Prop.
8.2
into the general Lemma
8.1
, we obtain:

Lemma 8.4

(Discrete Equation Combination for
\mathsf{LWE}
with Discrete Gaussian Noise)
.

Fix
k,n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},0<s<qn^{-\varepsilon}
and let
\chi_{s}
be be discrete Gaussian noise. For any even integer
k^{\prime}\leq 2k
, there exists an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (Alg.
3
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{s})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{s^{\prime}})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
and parameters
m^{\prime},s^{\prime}
depend on
s
and the input number of samples
m
as follows. Denote
n_{\mathsf{eff}}=n(q-1)
and assume
t=\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m
is a fixed constant;

•

If
t\leq\min\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}s\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4},k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(s\cdot m^{-1}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k/2+k^{\prime}/4})\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},k^{\prime}\Big]
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m\right)\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}=\sqrt{2}s\,;

•

If
t>\max\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},k^{\prime}\right\}
,

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad s^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(s\cdot m^{-1/2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}/2})\,.

Moreover, Alg.
3
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

Combined with results in Lemma
8.2
, our average-case reductions (specifically, third bullet point) yield new algorithms for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
with discrete Gaussian noise.

Corollary 8.2

(Detection Algorithms for
k
-sparse
\mathsf{LWE}
with Discrete Gaussian Noise)
.

There exists a
O(\mathrm{poly}(n,q))
-time algorithm solving the detection task for
k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}
with discrete Gaussian noise
\chi_{s}
in the following parameter regime. Denoting
k^{\prime}\leq k
be the smallest even integer satisfying
k^{\prime}\geq\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m
and
k^{\prime}>2(k-\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m)
, it suffices to have

n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{k^{\prime}/2}\cdot\max\left\{\sqrt{s},1\right\}\lesssim m\,.

8.5
Equation Combination Reduction for Bounded
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
Noise

While the most general version of combination reduction for bounded
\mathsf{LWE}
noise is stated in Lemma
8.1
, we illustrate a particular example. Let
\chi_{1}
be
binary
\mathsf{LWE}
noise
, i.e.,
\chi_{1}
is only supported on
\left\{0,\pm 1\right\}
and assume
q\geq 5
. Denote
\chi_{2}
, supported on
\left\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2\right\}
to be the distribution of the difference of two independent samples from
\chi_{1}
:

\chi_{2}(e)=\sum_{e_{1},e_{2}\in\left\{0,\pm 1\right\}:e_{1}-e_{2}=e}\chi_{1}(e_{1})\chi_{1}(e_{2})\,.

In this case, our equation combination reduction (Alg.
3
) can map an instance with noise distribution
\chi_{1}
to noise distribution
\chi_{2}
. We state the results for regimes of Lemma
8.1
where the output noise is exactly
\chi_{2}
; other regimes (bullet points two and four) also apply, but require explicit aggregation procedure (e.g., a majority vote) for the bounded noise.

Corollary 8.3

(of Lemma
8.1
)
.

Fix
k,n,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.
For any even integer
k^{\prime}\leq 2k
, there exists an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (Alg.
3
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n,m,\chi_{1})}\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}LWE}{(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\chi_{2})}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
and
m^{\prime}
depends on the input number of samples
m
as follows. Denote
n_{\mathsf{eff}}=n(q-1)
and assume
t=\log_{n_{\mathsf{eff}}}m
is a fixed constant;

•

If
t\leq\min\left\{k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},\frac{k}{2}+\frac{k^{\prime}}{4}\right\}
, then

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m^{2}n_{\mathsf{eff}}^{-(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)\,;

•

If
t\in\Big(k-\frac{k^{\prime}}{2},k^{\prime}\Big]
, then

m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(m\right)\,.

Moreover, Alg.
3
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

9
Gaussian Equation Combination

The discrete equation combination reduction (
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
) in
§​​ 7
combines pairs of entries in the input and achieves entrywise noise independence by not reusing them – this works best for sparser instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, i.e. small density
t
. We introduce a new reduction in Lemma
9.1
,
Gaussian equation combination
(
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
), that improves upon parameter tradeoffs in
§​​ 7
for inputs with larger density
t
.

9.1
Gaussian Equation Combination Reduction Idea and Lemma Statement

We prove a reduction for the Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models in Lemma
9.1
and analogous result for discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
follows by the discrete-Gaussian equivalence in Prop.
5.1
.

In
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
(equivalently
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) with a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, we observe a collection
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
for
\alpha_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
and

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta\cdot x_{\alpha_{j}}+\mathsf{G}_{j}\,,

where
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and we recall that
x_{\alpha}=x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{k}}
for an index set
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
. The product of two
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
entries
\mathsf{Y}_{s},\mathsf{Y}_{t}
for distinct
s,t\in\left[m\right]
with
k^{\prime}=|\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}|
has a mean matching the
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
entry with signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}
corresponding to an index set
\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}
:

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}\right]=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}}x_{\alpha_{t}}=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}\,.

(31)

Contrary to
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
in
§​​ 7
, where the
distribution
of
\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}
matched that of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
,

\mathsf{Y}_{s}\mathsf{Y}_{t}=\delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}+{\delta x_{\alpha_{s}}\mathsf{G}_{t}+\delta x_{\alpha_{t}}\mathsf{G}_{s}}+{\mathsf{G}_{s}\mathsf{G}_{t}}\ \ \not\approx_{d_{\mathsf{TV}}}\ \ \delta^{2}\cdot x_{\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,,

(32)

i.e., multiplying two
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
entries does not give a
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
entry. Our insight and the main technical contribution is that in some parameter regimes, aggregating many pairs
s,t\in\left[m\right]
with the same
\gamma=\alpha_{s}\triangle\alpha_{t}\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
does yield the correct
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
distribution, even when entries are reused in Eq. (
32
).

For the reduction below, we consider
t\in(1/3,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
and assume

-kt/2<\eta\leq(k-2)/2\,,

which in particular ensures the entrywise signal
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
is
o(1)
and that the input instance is in the conjectured hard regime (see
§​​ 1.2
).

Lemma 9.1

(Gaussian Equation Combination: Reduction to Tensor PCA)
.

Let
t\in(1/3,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R},k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
parameters satisfying
-kt/2<\eta\leq(k-2)/2
. If
k^{\prime}
is even and satisfies

k^{\prime}<\min\left\{kt+2\eta,\frac{k\left(3t-1\right)}{2},\frac{k\left(1+t\right)}{4}\right\}\,,

(33)

then there exists a poly-time average-case reduction
34
34

34

As defined in Def.
C.6
for both detection and recovery (Alg.
5
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,

for
n^{\prime}=n\left(1-o(1)\right)
. Moreover, Alg.
5
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
.
35
35

35

Here
x^{\prime}
can be any subset of
n^{\prime}
coordinates of
x
by permutation invariance of the model. See Sec.
C.4
for why this is sufficient to transfer weak/strong/exact recovery.

Remark 9.1
.

In the case of
\eta\to 0
, i.e. at the
computational threshold
, the parameter regime above can be rewritten as follows: all even
k^{\prime}
satisfying:

1.

t\in(1/3,3/5)
:
k^{\prime}<\frac{k\left(3t-1\right)}{2}
;

2.

t\in(3/5,1]
:
k^{\prime}<\frac{k(1+t)}{4}
.

Remark 9.2

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
Sampling Procedure for Alg.
5
and Discrete Input)
.

While Algorithm
5
, as stated, is a reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})
, we sometimes use it as a subroutine for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models with the standard sampling procedure, i.e., as a reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})
. It is implicitly assumed that first a Poissonization (Alg.
12
) reduction is applied, then Alg.
5
, and then the reduction back to standard sampling model (Alg.
13
).

Similarly if Alg.
5
is applied as a discrete model reduction for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
: this implicitly assumes the insertion of the appropriate Gaussianization/Discretization (
§​​ A.1.1
,
§​​ A.1.2
) and/or Resampling (
§​​ A.2
) reductions.

We present a brief proof sketch here and provide the full proof of Lemma
9.1
in
§​​ 9.2
. For this proof we adopt a
tensor view
of the input instance
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
. Let
\mathbf{Y}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
be a symmetric tensor satisfying for all
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Y}_{j^{\star}},&\text{ if }\ j^{\star}=\min\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right\}\\
0,&\text{ if }\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right\}=\emptyset\,.\end{cases}

Here, since
\mathbf{Y}
is symmetric up to index permutations, we index its entries by the multisets of indices in
\left[n\right]
. The idea behind the
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
is to consider a tensor flattening
Y=\mathsf{mat}_{k-k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{n}{k^{\prime}/2}}
– a matrix whose rows are indexed by elements of
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
and columns by
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
.
36
36

36

See
§​​ 6.1
for notation.
We consider the tensorization (i.e., the inverse procedure to
\mathsf{mat}_{k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\left(\cdot\right)
) of a Gram matrix

\mathbf{Z}=\mathsf{tens}{\left(Y^{\top}Y\right)}\quad\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k^{\prime}}\,.

Note that for all
\gamma\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}
, the resulting entry
\mathbf{Z}_{\gamma}
is a sum of products in Eq. (
32
) and we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{Z}_{\gamma}\right]\propto x_{\gamma}\,.

To ensure
independence of the entry-wise noise
and correct output distribution, our reduction works with a Gram-like matrix similar to
Y^{\top}Y
. Our main technical contribution is a distributional result for such Wishart-like matrices in Lemma
9.2
, which is a generalization of known results for Wishart matrices from
[
12
]
. Lemma
9.2
is proved in
§​​ D.2
.

Lemma 9.2

(Generalization of Theorem 2.6 of
[
12
]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\in[0,1]^{d\times n},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\in[0,1]^{d\times m}
have i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}({p_{R}})
and
\mathsf{Ber}({p_{L}})
entries respectively. Let
\delta_{R},\delta_{L}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters such that
\delta_{R},\delta_{L}\ll 1
and let
M_{R}^{\mathsf{full}}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{d\times n},M_{L}^{\mathsf{full}}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{d\times m}
be any fixed matrices. Let
\left(X^{G}_{R}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{d\times n}\right)
and
\left(X^{G}_{L}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{d\times m}\right)
.

Define for
a\in\left\{R,L\right\}
:

M_{a}=\delta_{a}M_{a}^{\mathsf{full}}\odot\mathsf{E}_{a}\quad\,,X^{G}_{a}=\left(X^{G}_{a}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\odot\mathsf{E}_{a}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad X_{a}=M_{a}+X^{G}_{a}\,.

If the following assumptions on parameters
{p_{R}},{p_{L}},\delta_{R},\delta_{L},d,n,m
are satisfied:

\displaystyle{p_{R}},{p_{L}}

\displaystyle\in\left\{1\right\}\cup(0,1-c)\text{ for some }c>0

(a)

\displaystyle\delta_{R}

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{1,\left({p_{R}}n\right)^{-1/2}\right\}\text{ and }\delta_{L}\ll\min\left\{1,\left({p_{L}}m\right)^{-1/2}\right\}

(b)

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\ll{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}d\text{ and }m\ll{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}d

(c)

\displaystyle mn

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{{p_{R}}{p_{L}}d,\delta_{R}^{-4},\delta_{L}^{-4}\right\}

(d)

\displaystyle n^{2}m

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{{p_{L}}d,{p_{R}}^{-2}\delta_{R}^{-4}\right\},\text{ and }nm^{2}\ll\min\left\{{p_{R}}d,{p_{L}}^{-2}\delta_{L}^{-4}\right\}\,,

(e)

then

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(N\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}M_{R}^{T}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}X_{L}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad d\to\infty\,.

9.2
Proof of Lemma
9.1

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\mathbb{R}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,
t\in\left[0,1\right]
,
\kappa\in(0,1)
.

Output:

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,
\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}
.

1

\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\leftarrow\mathsf{kSplit}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

2

Denote
\mathcal{Y}^{(A)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{(A)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(A)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(A)}\right]}
for
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}

3

//
Construct the tensor view of the input

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}^{(A)}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{Y}_{j^{\star}}^{(A)},&\text{ if }\ j^{\star}=\min\left\{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(A)}\right]:\alpha_{j}^{(A)}=\alpha\right\}\\
0,&\text{ if }\left\{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(A)}\right]:\alpha_{j}^{(A)}=\alpha\right\}=\emptyset\end{cases}\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}\text{ and }\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}

4

//
Flatten the tensors

\hat{Y}^{(A)}\leftarrow\mathsf{mat}_{k-k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\left(\mathbf{Y}^{(A)}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}}
for
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}

5

//
Only keep part of the entries

For
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}
let
Y^{(A)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{\kappa n}{k^{\prime}/2}}
:

\forall\alpha\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1,\dots,n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2},\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}\,,\qquad Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{(A)}\leftarrow\hat{Y}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(A)}

6

//
Compute Revealed Entry Probability

p\leftarrow C\cdot n^{k(t-1)/2}
, where
C
is defined in the proof of Lem.
9.1
and satisfies
\Pr[\mathsf{Y}^{(A)}\neq 0]=p

7

//
Construct the Gram matrix

Z\leftarrow p^{-1}\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}^{-1/2}\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{\top}Y^{(2)}

8

//
Arrange the entries of
\mathsf{tens}\left(Z\right)
into an instance of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}

\mathbf{Z}=\mathsf{tens}\left(Z\right)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathbf{Z}_{\gamma}\right)\right\}_{\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}}

9

Return
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\big)
.
//
Alg.

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{GaussEqComb}\left(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},t,\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n\right)

We prove that
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}\left(\cdot\right)
(Alg.
5
) is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery, as defined in Def.
C.6
,

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})\,,

where parameters
k,k^{\prime},t,\eta,n^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
9.1
. This immediately yields an average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})
, since by Prop.
5.2
there exist average-case reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
both ways.

To prove the reduction, since it is sufficient to map parameters up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors by Prop.
C.3
, we show that in the planted case
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
, the output
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{GaussEqComb}\left(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},t\right)
satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)=o(1)\,,\quad\text{where }m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}}\right)\text{ and }\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta}\right)\,,

and in the null case
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois,NULL}}_{t}(n)
, the output
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{GaussEqComb}\left(\mathcal{Y},k^{\prime},t\right)
satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois,NULL}}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime})\right)=o(1)\,,\quad\text{where }m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k^{\prime}}\right)\,.

Denote
m=n^{k(1+t)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
and assume the input instance contains
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(m\right)
equations.

Step 1: tensor view of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
.

In Lines 1-6 of Alg.
5
, we reshape the input instance into two matrices
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}
, which we hereby describe. We show the calculation for the planted case and the argument in the null case is analogous.
From the guarantees of the splitting procedure (Lemma
5.2
),
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n,\tilde{m},\delta)
,
\tilde{m}=\Theta(m)
, are independent and share the input signal vector
x
. Then, we can express for all
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}^{(A)}=\left(\delta\cdot x_{\alpha}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}^{(A)}\right)\cdot\mathsf{E}_{\alpha}^{(A)}\,,\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}\,,

where
\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}^{(A)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is the entry-wise noise and
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha}^{(A)}\sim\mathsf{Ber}\left(p\right)
for

p=\Pr\left[\exists j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(A)}\right]:\,\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right]=\Pr\left[\mathsf{Pois}\left(\tilde{m}\binom{n}{k}^{-1}\right)\right]=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k(t-1)/2}\right)\eqcolon C\cdot n^{k(t-1)/2}\,,

for a known
C=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
, where we used
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(A)}\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(\tilde{m}\right)
and Poisson splitting.
The flattenings
\mathsf{mat}_{k-k^{\prime}/2\times k^{\prime}/2}\mathbf{Y}^{(A)}
are matrices of dimensions
\binom{n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}\times\binom{n}{k^{\prime}/2}
with rows indexed by elements in
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
and columns by
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
. Denote the pruned sets as
N=\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}/2}
and
M=\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{k-k^{\prime}/2}
. Then in Line 5 of Alg.
5
we set for all
(\alpha,\beta)\in M\times N

Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{(A)}=\left(\delta\cdot x_{\alpha\cup\beta}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha\cup\beta}^{(A)}\right)\cdot\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\cup\beta}^{(A)}\,,\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}\,.

Let
x^{(k-k^{\prime}/2)}=\mathsf{vec}\left(\left\{x_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in M}\right)
,
x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}=\mathsf{vec}\left(\left\{x_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta\in N}\right)
, so we can rewrite

Y^{(A)}=\left(\delta x^{(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}+\mathsf{G}^{(A)}\right)\odot\mathsf{E}^{(A)}\,,\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}\,,

where
\mathsf{G}^{(A)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{|M|\times|N|})
are independent noise matrices and
\odot
denotes the entry-wise multiplication.

Step 2: Gram matrix: a reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}
(see Fig.
6
and
§​​ 5.1.4
).

In Line 7 Alg.
5
computes the Gram matrix

Z=p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{\top}Y^{(2)}\,.

(34)

The goal of this step is to show that in Lines 1-8, Alg.
5
makes an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime}=\kappa n)
(defines in
§​​ 5.1.4
). For this it is sufficient to demonstrate that in the planted case

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(Z\right),\delta^{\prime}x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}+\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{|N|,|N|}\right)\right)=o(1)\,,

(35)

where
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta}\right)
and in the null case

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(Z\right),\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{|N|,|N|}\right)\right)=o(1)\,.

(36)

Note that, since
\mathsf{Y}^{(1)},\mathsf{Y}^{(1)}
are independent,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}Z

\displaystyle=p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{(1)}\right]^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle=p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\delta x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}\right)\odot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{E}^{(1)}\right]^{\top}\left(\delta x^{(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}\right)\odot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathsf{E}^{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle=p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\delta^{2}p^{2}|M|x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}=\underbrace{\delta^{2}p|M|^{1/2}}_{\delta^{\prime}}x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}\,,

where we have
\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{2}p|M|^{1/2}=\Theta\left(n^{-kt/2-\eta+k(t-1)/2+(k-k^{\prime}/2)/2}\right)=\Theta\left(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta}\right)
. It remains to show that the entries of
Z
are indeed independent Gaussian entries with unit variance. We achieve this by applying Lemma
9.2
proved in
§​​ D.2
.

Matrix
Z
in Eq. (
34
) fits the setting of Lemma
9.2
. Indeed, matrices
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|M|\times|N|}
satisfy the Lemma
9.2
conditions with
\tilde{p_{R}}=\tilde{p_{L}}=p
,
\tilde{\delta}_{R}=\tilde{\delta}_{L}=\delta
, and
\tilde{d}=|M|,\tilde{n}=\tilde{m}=|N|
, where recall
p=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k(t-1)/2}\right),\delta=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}\right)
and
|M|=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{k-k^{\prime}/2}\big),|N|=\widetilde{\Theta}\big(n^{k^{\prime}/2}\big)
. It is left to verify the asymptotic assumptions on parameters
\tilde{p_{R}},\tilde{p_{L}},\tilde{\delta}_{R},\tilde{\delta}_{L},\tilde{d},\tilde{n},\tilde{m}
: condition (
a
) is trivially satisfied and

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:cond_b}:-kt/4-\eta/2<0\quad\text{and}\quad k/2-\eta<k-k^{\prime}/2

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:cond_c}:k(5-3t)/4<k-k^{\prime}/2

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:cond_d}:k(3-t)/3<k-k^{\prime}/2\quad\text{and}\quad k(2-t)/2-\eta<k-k^{\prime}/2

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:cond_e}:k(7-t)/8<k-k^{\prime}/2\quad\text{and}\quad 2k/3-2\eta/3<k-k^{\prime}/2\,,

all of which are satisfied in the regime of our reduction:

k-k^{\prime}/2>\max\left\{k\left(1-t/2\right)-\eta,k(5-3t)/4,k(7-t)/8\right\}\text{ and }-kt/2<\eta\leq(k-2)/2\,.

Denoting

\mu^{(A)}=\delta x^{(k-k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}\odot\mathsf{E}^{(A)}\,,\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}

to be the means of the matrices
\mathsf{Y}^{(1)},\mathsf{Y}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{|M|\times|N|}
, we conclude from Lemma
9.2
,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)},I_{N\times N}\right),p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{T}Y^{(2)}\right)\to 0\,,\quad n\to\infty\,.

By
d_{\mathsf{TV}}
triangle inequality (Fact
6.1
), to conclude the theorem statement it is now sufficient to show

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)},I_{|N|\times|N|}\right),\mathcal{N}\left(\delta^{2}p|M|^{1/2}x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top},I_{|N|\times|N|}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad n\to\infty\,.

By Lemma
6.1
, Eq. (
18
), the square of the total variation above is upper bounded by

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(1)},\mu^{(2)}}\Big\|p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}-\delta^{2}p|M|^{1/2}x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\left(x^{(k^{\prime}/2)}\right)^{\top}\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{(1)},\mu^{(2)}}\Big\|p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}\Big\|^{2}\,.

Now recall that for all
i,j\in[|N|]
,

\left(\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}\right)_{ij}\sim\delta^{2}\mathsf{Bin}\left(|M|,p^{2}\right)\,,

and therefore,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}\right)_{ij}-\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}\right)_{ij}\right]^{2}=\delta^{4}|M|p^{2}(1-p^{2})\leq\delta^{4}|M|p^{2}\,.

We conclude that

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}p^{-2}|M|^{-1}\cdot|N|^{2}\delta^{4}|M|p^{2}=\frac{1}{4}|N|^{2}\delta^{4}=\Theta\left(n^{k^{\prime}-kt-2\eta}\right)=o(1)\,,

since
k^{\prime}-kt-2\eta<0
, which shows Eq. (
35
) and concludes the proof for the planted case.

For the null case (Def.
C.1
), the analysis is analogous and Lemma
9.2
is similarly applicable. We conclude that the output is close in total variation distance to
N\left(p^{-1}|M|^{-1/2}\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)},I_{|N|\times|N|}\right)
, where every entry of
\mu^{(1)},\mu^{(2)}
is i.i.d.
\sim\begin{cases}0,&\text{w.p. }1-p\\
+\delta,&\text{w.p. }p/2\\
-\delta,&\text{w.p. }p/2\,.\end{cases}
. Notice that with high probability on the Bernoulli mask,

\displaystyle p^{-2}|M|^{-1}\mathbb{E}\big\|\left(\mu^{(1)}\right)^{T}\mu^{(2)}\big\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq p^{-2}|M|^{-1}O\left(|N|^{2}|M|p^{2}\delta^{4}\right)=O\left(\delta^{4}N^{2}\right)=O\left(n^{k^{\prime}-kt-2\eta}\right)=o(1)\,,

since
k-k^{\prime}/2>k\left(1-t/2\right)-\eta
and we only consider the off-diagonal entries.
By properties of total variation (Fact
6.1
) and Lemma
6.1
, we conclude that the output is close in total variation to
\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{|N|\times|N|}\right)
, proving Eq. (
36
).

Step 3: from
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
.

Since Lines 1-8 are an average-case reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime}=\kappa n)
, the final step is an average-case reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n^{\prime})
by the guarantees for
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
in Prop.
5.6
(and implicit Poissonization step,
§​​ 5.1.2
). This concludes the proof of Lemma
9.1
.

9.3
Improved Gaussian Equation Combination for
t=1
via Sparse PCA

In Lemma
9.1
we proved an average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})
, where
t\in(1/3,1]
. For
t=1
, i.e., when the input instance is also Tensor PCA, the reduction in Lemma
9.1
maps from Tensor PCA of order
k
to Tensor PCA of even order
k^{\prime}<k/2
. In this section we prove Lemma
9.3
: it extends
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
to accept as an input two
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
instances of different orders
k_{1},k_{2}
that share the same secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
:

k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{1}}(n)\ ``+"\ k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{2}}(n)\ \to\ k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}(n^{\prime})

for admissible range of parameters
k^{\prime}<\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{3}
and
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
. Our main insight is a connection to recent results for Spiked Covariance and Spiked Wigner Models in
[
20
]
(
§​​ 9.3.1
). Consequently, we improve the reduction of Lemma
9.1
to map from order
k
to all even orders
k^{\prime}<2k/3
. The proof of Lemma
9.3
is in
§​​ 9.3.2
.

We present the reduction for the Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
and the same result holds for discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
by the discrete-Gaussian equivalence in Prop.
5.1
.

Lemma 9.3

(Extended Gaussian Equation Combination)
.

Given independent instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\sim k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{1}}(n)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{2}}(n)
that share a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
and have
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\geq 0
, Alg.
6
outputs

\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{GaussEqCombTwo}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)},k^{\prime}\right)
, where

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{Z},k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\,,

where
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{k^{\prime}})
and
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})/2})
, if
(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2
is an integer and
k^{\prime}
satisfies

k^{\prime}\in\left[|k_{1}-k_{2}|,\min\left\{k_{1},k_{2}\right\}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad k^{\prime}<\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}}{3}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
6
maps two instances with the same secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}
for
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
.

Remark
9.2
similarly applies.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}^{(A)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{(A)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(A)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[M^{(A)}\right]}
for
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}
, where
\forall j\in\left[M^{(A)}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j}^{(A)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(A)}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{A}}\times\mathbb{R}
,
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
,

.

Output:

\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[M^{\prime}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[M^{\prime}\right]
,
\big(\gamma_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}
.

1

//
Construct tensor views of the two inputs

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}^{(A)}=\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(A)},\text{ where }\ j\in\left[M^{(A)}\right]:\alpha_{j}^{(A)}=\alpha\quad\text{for }A\in\left\{1,2\right\}\text{ and }\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{A}}

2

Denote
a\coloneq(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2
(assumed integer)

3

//
Flatten the tensors

\hat{Y}^{(A)}\leftarrow\mathsf{mat}_{a\times k_{A}-a}\left(\mathbf{Y}^{(A)}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{\left[n\right]}{a}\times\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{A}-a}}
for
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}

4

//
Only keep part of the entries

For
A\in\left\{1,2\right\}
let
Y^{(A)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{a}\times\binom{\kappa n}{k_{A}-a}}
:

\forall\alpha\in\binom{\left[\kappa n+1,\dots,n\right]}{a},\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{A}-a}\,,\qquad Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{(A)}\leftarrow\hat{Y}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(A)}

5

//
Construct the Gram matrix

Z\leftarrow\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{a}^{-1/2}\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{\top}Y^{(2)}

6

Arrange the entries of
\mathsf{tens}\left(Z\right)
into an instance of

,
\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}=\left\{\left(\gamma,\mathbf{Z}_{\gamma}\right)\right\}_{\gamma\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k^{\prime}}}

Return
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{Z}}\big)

Algorithm 6

\mathsf{GaussEqCombTwo}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)},k^{\prime},\kappa=1-\log^{-1}n\right)

9.3.1
Connection to Spiked Covariance and Spiked Wigner Models

To obtain this generalized
\mathsf{EqComb}
two-to-one reduction,
we draw connections between
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
and the two models of high-dimensional statistics: Spiked Covariance Model and Spiked Wigner Model. We will use the average-case reductions between them developed in
[
20
]
. While this reduction is similar to the one in
§​​ 11
, we directly reduce to the result in
[
20
]
to obtain sharper
k
tradeoffs for a specific case of
t=1
.

For
t=1
we proved in Prop.
5.6
that
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
is computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
, i.e., there exist average-case reductions between the models both ways. In
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
, we observe
m=\binom{n}{k}

k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
equations, exactly one for each index set
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
. Then, in a tensor view, a
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
instance
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
can be reshaped into a symmetric tensor
\mathbf{Y}\in\left(\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)^{\otimes k}
satisfying for all
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}

\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}=\mathsf{Y}_{j},\text{ where }\ j\in\left[m\right]:\alpha_{j}=\alpha\,.

Here, since
\mathbf{Y}
is symmetric up to index permutations, we index its entries by the sets of indices in
\left[n\right]
. A flattening
Y=\mathsf{mat}_{a\times k-a}\mathbf{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{n}{a}\times\binom{n}{k-a}}
for some
a
is an
M=\binom{n}{a}
by
N=\binom{n}{k-a}
matrix whose rows are indexed by elements of
\binom{\left[n\right]}{a}
and columns by
\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-a}
.

Definition 9.1

(Spiked Covariance and Spiked Wigner Models)
.

Under the
Spiked Covariance Model
of Johnstone and Lu
[
51
]
, one observes
M
i.i.d. samples from
\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{N}+\theta uu^{\top}\right)
for a unit vector
u\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and an SNR parameter
\theta
. Arranging the samples in the rows of matrix
Z\in\mathbb{R}^{M\times N}
, the model data has a convenient representation

Z=X+\sqrt{\theta}gu^{\top},\qquad\text{where }X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{M\times N})\text{ and }g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{M})\text{ are independent}\,.

Under the
Spiked Wigner Model
, one observes a matrix

Y=\lambda uu^{\top}+W,\qquad W\sim\mathsf{GOE}(N)\,,

where
u
is the unit signal vector and
\lambda
is the SNR.

With these definitions in mind, note that
Y=\mathsf{mat}_{a\times k-a}\left(\mathbf{Y}\right)
can be represented as

Y^{\top}=X+\sqrt{\theta}x_{L}x_{R}^{\top},\qquad X\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{M\times N}\right)\,,

where
x_{L}=x^{\widetilde{\otimes}a}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{M},x_{R}=N^{-1/2}x^{\widetilde{\otimes}k-a}\in\left\{\pm 1/\sqrt{N}\right\}^{N}
and
\theta=\delta^{2}N
. With the exception of the distribution of
x_{L}
(which corresponds to
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{M})
), this resembles the distribution of the Spiked Covariance Model with
M
samples in dimension
N
and the signal unit vector
x_{R}=N^{-1/2}x^{\widetilde{\otimes}k-a}
. Moreover, the Wigner Model with the same signal vector,

Y=\lambda x_{R}x_{R}^{\top}+W

resembles the flattening of the
(2k-2a)
-order tensor with the secret vector
x
. This connection is the inspiration for this reduction that closely mirrors the reduction of
[
20
]
from Spiked Covariance to Spiked Wigner Model.

9.3.2
Proof of Lemma
9.3

We show that Alg.
6
is an average-case reduction from
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\sim k_{1}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta_{1}}(n)
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k_{2}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta_{2}}(n)
, that share a secret vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}(n)
and the Lemma
9.3
follows from the sampling equivalence in Prop.
5.6
. In particular, under the conditions of the lemma, we prove that the output
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{GaussEqCombTwo}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)},k^{\prime}\right)
satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{Z},k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n^{\prime},m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\,,

(37)

where
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{k^{\prime}})
and
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(n^{-k^{\prime}/4-\eta_{1}/2-\eta_{2}/2})
, which shows the desired average-case reduction by Prop.
C.3
.

The proof of Eq. (
37
) closely follows that of Theorem 8.2 in
[
20
]
.
From the definition of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}
in
§​​ 5.1.4
, we can express

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{1}}:

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}^{(1)}=\delta^{(1)}\cdot x_{\alpha}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}^{(1)}\,,

\displaystyle\forall\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k_{2}}:

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{Y}_{\alpha}^{(2)}=\delta^{(2)}\cdot x_{\alpha}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha}^{(2)}\,,

where
\delta^{(1)}=\Theta\left(n^{-k_{1}/4-\eta_{1}/2}\right)
and
\delta^{(2)}=\Theta\left(n^{-k_{2}/4-\eta_{2}/2}\right)
. The flattenings
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}
in Lines 3-4 of Alg.
6
then satisfy for all
(\alpha,\beta)\in\binom{\left[(1-\kappa)n\right]}{a}\times\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{1}-a}
,

Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{(1)}=\delta^{(1)}\cdot x_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(1)}

and for all
(\alpha,\beta)\in\binom{\left[(1-\kappa)n\right]}{a}\times\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{2}-a}
,

Y_{\alpha,\beta}^{(2)}=\delta^{(2)}\cdot x_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathsf{G}_{\alpha,\beta}^{(2)}\,,

where we index the rows and columns of these matrices by the corresponding index sets. Denote
N_{1}=\binom{\kappa n}{k_{1}-a},N_{2}=\binom{\kappa n}{k_{2}-a},M=\binom{(1-\kappa)n}{a}
and
g=\mathsf{vec}\left(\left\{x_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in\binom{\left[(1-\kappa)n\right]}{a}}\right)
,
u^{(1)}=N_{1}^{-1/2}\mathsf{vec}\left(\left\{x_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{1}-a}}\right)
, and
u^{(2)}=N_{2}^{-1/2}\mathsf{vec}\left(\left\{x_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{2}-a}}\right)
, we can express

Y^{(1)}=\sqrt{\theta^{(1)}}g\left(u^{(1)}\right)^{\top}+X^{(1)},\qquad\text{and}\qquad Y^{(2)}=\sqrt{\theta^{(2)}}g\left(u^{(2)}\right)^{\top}+X^{(2)}\,,

where
X^{(1)},X^{(2)}
are independent i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
matrices and
\theta^{(1)}=\tilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k_{1}/2-a-\eta_{1}}\right)=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{N_{1}/M}n^{-\eta_{1}}\right)
,
\theta^{(2)}=\tilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k_{2}/2-a-\eta_{2}}\right)=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\sqrt{N_{2}/M}n^{-\eta_{2}}\right)
. This brings us into the setting of the Spiked Covariance Model (Def.
9.1
), so we apply the tools developed for Theorem 8.2 of
[
20
]
directly here in the following claim.

Claim 9.1
.

In the setup above, if
M\gg N_{1}N_{2}
, for
W\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{1}\times N_{2}}
with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(M^{-1/2}\left(Y^{(1)}\right)^{\top}Y^{(2)},\lambda u^{(1)}\left(u^{(2)}\right)^{\top}+W\right)\to 0\qquad\text{as }N,M\to\infty\,,

where
\lambda=\sqrt{\theta^{(1)}\theta^{(2)}M}
.

Proof.

The proof (for both planted and null distributions) follows that of Theorem 8.2 in
[
20
]
with the following modifications:

1.

We skip the symmetrization step of the original Theorem 8.2.

2.

The key proposition of
[
12
]
that shows Claim
9.1
in the case of
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}
having i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries is stated only for the case of
N_{1}=N_{2}
. As proved in the source paper
[
12
]
(Corollary 4.3), the same result holds for any dimensions
N_{1},N_{2},M
satisfying
M\gg N_{1}N_{2}
.

3.

The standard in the field argument for removing random cross-terms will succeed whenever both
\left(\theta^{(1)}\right)^{2}N_{2}
and
\left(\theta^{(2)}\right)^{2}N_{1}
are
o(1)
which again holds for
M\gg N_{1}N_{2}
and
\theta^{(1)},\theta^{(2)}
as in Lemma
9.3
.

4.

Again we allow one of the matrices to be a vector. The proof is not altered.

∎

Since
a=(k_{1}+k_{2}-k^{\prime})/2>(k_{1}+k_{2})/3
, the condition
M\gg N_{1}N_{2}
holds, and therefore, Claim
9.1
shows that
Z\sim\lambda u^{(1)}\left(u^{(2)}\right)^{\top}+W
for
\lambda=\sqrt{\theta^{(1)}\theta^{(2)}M}
. Therefore
\mathbf{Z}=\mathsf{tens}\left(Z\right)
satisfies for all
\alpha\in\binom{\left[\kappa n\right]}{k_{1}+k_{2}-2a}
,

\mathsf{Z}_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\theta^{(1)}\theta^{(2)}M}N_{1}^{-1/2}N_{2}^{-1/2}\cdot x_{\alpha}+W_{\alpha}\,,

where
W_{\alpha}
are i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Finally note that
\sqrt{\theta^{(1)}\theta^{(2)}}MN_{1}^{-1/2}N_{2}^{-1/2}=\Theta\left(n^{-(k_{1}+k_{2}-2a)/4}-(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2})/2\right)
, which is the appropriate signal level for
(k_{1}+k_{2}-2a)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}}(\kappa n)
. This concludes the proof of Lemma
9.3
.

10
Densifying Reduction: Growing
t

10.1
Result Overview

In this section we build average-case reductions that relate instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
with different values of parameter
t
, i.e. different density of revealed (nonzero) entries and different entry-wise signal level (SNR). Our average-case reductions are in Theorem
10.1
and their immediate implications for hardness conjectures (Conj.
1.1
) are in Corollary
10.1
. Analogous results hold for the Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models by discrete-Gaussian equivalence in Prop.
5.1
.

Theorem 10.1

(Densifying Reduction: Growing
t
)
.

Let
k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t,t^{\prime}\in(0,1]
, and
\eta\geq 0
be constants, s.t.

t^{\prime}>2t/(1-t)\quad\text{or}\quad t<t^{\prime}=1\,.

There exists sufficiently large
k=k(t,t^{\prime},\eta,k^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and an average-case reduction
37
37

37

As defined in Def.
C.6
.
for both detection and recovery (Alg.
7
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\,,

where
\eta^{\prime}\leq 3\eta
and
n^{\prime}=\tilde{n}^{A}
for a known constant
A=A\left(t,t^{\prime},\eta,k^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
\tilde{n}=n(1-o(1))
. Moreover, Alg.
7
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a secret vector
x^{\prime}=\left(x_{\left[1:\tilde{n}\right]}\right)^{\otimes A}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right)
38
38

38

See Sec.
C.4
on why this transfers weak/strong/exact recovery algorithms.
.

Corollary 10.1

(Densifying Reduction (Growing
t
), Corollary of Theorem
10.1
)
.

For any
t,t^{\prime}\in(0,1]
satisfying
t^{\prime}>2t/(1-t)
or
t<t^{\prime}=1

and every
k^{\prime}\geq 2
, there exists some
k\geq k^{\prime}
such that

\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)\,.

In particular, for
t,t^{\prime}
as above,

\mathsf{CONJ}_{t}\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}_{t^{\prime}}\,.

Theorem
10.1
proof overview.

The key reduction subroutine of Alg.

\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}

Lemma
7.1
.

(Discrete Equation Combination Reduction)

Fix
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. For any even integer
k^{\prime}
, s.t.

k^{\prime}\leq k\left(1-t\right)\,,

there exists an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (Alg.
1
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,

where
t^{\prime}=\min\left\{\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t,1\right\}
. Moreover, Alg.
1
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with the signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}
for
n^{\prime}=n(1-o(1))
.
39
39

39

The algorithm can set
x^{\prime}
to have any
n^{\prime}
-coordinate subset of
x
; the algorithm succeeds for any
n^{\prime}=n\left(1-(\mathrm{poly}\log n)^{-1}\right)
.

For the case of
k^{\prime}\geq 2kt
, the reduction of Lemma
7.1
shows a map from an instance with parameter tuple
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
to an instance with parameter tuple
\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}=\frac{2k}{k^{\prime}}t,2\eta\right)
for an appropriate range of even
k^{\prime}
. In particular, given an instance of a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
, one could map to an instance with

t^{\prime}\in\left\{\frac{2kt}{k_{\min}},\frac{2kt}{k_{\min}+2},\dots,\frac{2kt}{k_{\max}}\right\}\subset\Big(\frac{2t}{1-t},1\Big]\,,

where

k_{\min}=\min_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\{k^{\prime}\text{ even}:k^{\prime}\geq 2kt\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad k_{\max}=\max_{k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\{k^{\prime}\text{ even}:k^{\prime}<k(1-t)\right\}\,.

The main technical challenge of Theorem
10.1
is to apply the reduction of Lemma
7.1
with an appropriate parameter
k
, and map to
any

.

10.2
Proof of Theorem
10.1

For a given target order
k^{\prime}
, densities
t,t^{\prime}\in\left[0,1\right]
, and deficiency
\eta>0
, the reduction algorithm for Theorem
10.1
is Algorithm
7
with carefully chosen parameters

and
k
. Alg.
7
is a sequential composition of several average-case reductions and in this proof we verify that every reduction call is (1) valid and (2) maps the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
parameters appropriately.

10.2.1
Preliminary Lemmas

In addition to Lemma

, which shows a guarantee on the

The guarantee in Cor.

for
\mathsf{Densify}

\mathsf{Sparsify}
and Cor.

and

Corollary
5.2
.

(Guarantees for
\mathsf{Densify}
(Alg.

) and
\mathsf{Sparsify}
(Alg.

))

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
parameters. There is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (
\mathsf{Densify}
if
t\leq t^{\prime}
and
\mathsf{Sparsify}
otherwise)

\displaystyle\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\,,\quad\text{where }\eta^{\prime}=\eta+{k|t-t^{\prime}|/2}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
21
and Alg.
22
map an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with the same signal vector
x
.

Corollary
5.3
.

(of Lemma
5.4
: Guarantees of
\mathsf{ReduceK}
)

Let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
parameters. For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},a|k
there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{a}\,,

for all

.
Moreover, the reduction maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right)
. From the discrete-Gaussian equivalence (Prop.
5.1
), the analogous results hold for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models.

Prop.
5.3
relating
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}

.

(Reduction from a Collection of

\mathsf{Compose}

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z},t\in\left[0,1\right],\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. There exists a poly-time average-case reduction
40
40

40

As defined in def.

(Alg.

)

\text{from}\quad\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{r},\eta}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\qquad\text{with }t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}\,,

where the input instances share the same signal vector
