, which is preserved exactly in the reduction.

10.2.2
Special Case:
0<t<t^{\prime}=1

For any
k^{\prime},\eta
and
0<t<t^{\prime}=1
, it is sufficient to choose any
k>\max\left\{\frac{k^{\prime}}{t},\frac{2k^{\prime}}{1-t}\right\}
and
a=k-k^{\prime}
as inputs to Alg.
7
. In particular, let
k,a
be as above and
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
be the input instance to Alg.
7
of order
k
. We show that the map

\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{SparseToDense}\left(\mathcal{Y},a,k^{\prime},t,t^{\prime}\right)

is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
, where parameters
n^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
satisfy the requirements of Theorem
10.1
.

By the guarantees for
\mathsf{kSplit}
in Lemma
5.2
, guarantees for
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
in Lemma
7.1
, and guarantees for
\mathsf{Compose}
in Prop.
5.3
, Lines 1-5 of Alg.
7
make an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad 2k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t},2\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\,,

where
\tilde{n}=n(1-o(1))
and
\hat{t}=\min\left\{\frac{2kt}{2k^{\prime}},1\right\}=1
. In this case, Lines 6-9 of Alg.
7
keep the instance
\mathcal{Y}^{\hat{t}}
unchanged. By guarantees for
\mathsf{ReduceK}
in Lem.
5.4
, the final step of the algorithm is an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad 2k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t}=1,2\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t}=1,\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n}^{2})\,,

which concludes the proof.

10.2.3
Proof for
0<t<t^{\prime}<1

For any fixed parameters
k^{\prime},t,t^{\prime},\eta
, we identify parameters
k,a
such that Algorithm
7
is an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\,,

where
n^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
satisfy the conditions of Theorem
10.1
. The key technical challenge of the following proof is the appropriate selection of the input parameters
k,a
.

Step 1: mapping
t\to\hat{t}
in Lines 1-5.

By the guarantees for
\mathsf{kSplit}
in Lemma
5.2
, guarantees for
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
in Lemma
7.1
, and guarantees for
\mathsf{Compose}
in Prop.
5.3
, Lines 1-5 of Alg.
7
make an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad 2(k-a)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t},2\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\,,

where
\tilde{n}=n(1-o(1))
and
\hat{t}=\min\left\{\frac{2kt}{2(k-a)},1\right\}
if the conditions of Lemma
7.1
are satisfied. Namely, the reduction succeeds for any
k
and
a\in\big[\frac{k(1+t)}{2},k\big)
an integer. Set

a\coloneq\lfloor k(1-t/t^{\prime})\rceil\,.

(38)

where recall that
\lfloor\cdot\rceil
denotes rounding to the nearest integer and
k
is a constant to be chosen later.
Note that, since
t^{\prime}>\frac{2t}{1-t}
, for sufficiently large constant
k
,
k>a\geq k\left(1-\frac{t}{t^{\prime}}\right)-1\geq\frac{k(1+t)}{2}
, i.e., the choice of
a
in Eq. (
38
) satisfies the conditions of Lemma
7.1
. Denote

\psi\coloneq a-k(1-t/t^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\left\{kt/t^{\prime}\right\},&\left\{kt/t^{\prime}\right\}\leq 1/2\\
\left\{kt/t^{\prime}\right\}-1,&\left\{kt/t^{\prime}\right\}>1/2\end{cases}\,,

where
\lfloor\cdot\rfloor,\left\{\cdot\right\}
are the integer and fractional parts of a number. Then, for sufficiently large
k\geq\frac{t^{\prime}}{t(1-t^{\prime})}
,
\frac{kt}{k-a}\leq 1
, so

\displaystyle\hat{t}=\frac{kt}{k-a}=\frac{kt}{k-k(1-t/t^{\prime})-\psi}=\frac{kt}{kt/t^{\prime}-\psi}=\frac{ktt^{\prime}}{kt-\psi t^{\prime}}=t^{\prime}+\frac{\psi(t^{\prime})^{2}}{kt-\psi t^{\prime}}=:t^{\prime}+\Delta\,.

Our goal is to map to an instance with the sparsity parameter
t^{\prime}
, so we want
\hat{t}\in\left[t^{\prime}\pm\xi\right]
, i.e.
\Delta\in[\pm\xi]
, for a small parameter
\xi>0
to be chosen later. In order for
|\Delta|\leq\xi
, it is sufficient to take
k
such that

\frac{k}{|\psi|}>\max\left\{\frac{t^{\prime}}{t},\frac{t^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\xi)}{t\xi}\right\}=\frac{t^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\xi)}{t\xi}\,.

(39)

The following Claim shows existence of
k
satisfying (
39
) and additional properties to be used later. It is proved in
§​​ D.1
.

Claim 10.1
.

Fix
c,r\in(0,1),k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There exists a constant
A(c,r,k^{\prime})
, such that for every
A\geq A(c,r,k^{\prime})
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that
k\leq cA
,
\lfloor kr\rceil
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
, and

\frac{k}{\|kr\|}>A\,,

(40)

where
\|x\|=\big|x-\lfloor x\rceil\big|
.

We now use Claim
10.1
to find a good
k
that satisfies (
39
). Let
c=\left(2t^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\eta
be a constant and apply Claim
10.1
for
c,r=t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime}
. This demonstrates that for
t^{\prime}<1
, there exists a constant
A\left(c,t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime}\right)
such that for any
A>A\left(c,t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime}\right)
the result of the claim holds.
In particular, there exists
k\leq cA
such that
\lfloor kt/t^{\prime}\rceil
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
and

\frac{k}{\|kt/t^{\prime}\|}>A=\frac{t^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\xi)}{t\xi}\,,

for
\xi=\xi(A)
chosen appropriately. Notice that
\|kt/t^{\prime}\|=|kt/t^{\prime}-\lfloor kt/t^{\prime}\rceil|=|\psi|
. As described above, this implies that
\hat{t}\in[t^{\prime}\pm\xi(A)]
for this choice of
k=k(A)
.

Denote
\xi(c,t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime})=\xi\left(A(c,t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime})\right)
, and notice that as
A\to\infty
,
\xi(A)\to 0
. Then, for any constant
c
and any
\xi\leq\xi(c,t/t^{\prime},k^{\prime})
, we have found
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that:

1.

k\leq cA=c\cdot\frac{t^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\xi)}{t\xi}
;

2.

\lfloor kt/t^{\prime}\rceil=k-\lfloor k(1-t/t^{\prime})\rceil=k-a
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
;

3.

lines 1-5 of Alg.
7
make an average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
2(k-a)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t},2\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})
for
\hat{t}\in\left[t^{\prime}\pm\xi\right]
and
a=\lfloor k(1-t/t^{\prime})\rceil
.

Step 2: mapping
\hat{t}\to t^{\prime}
in Lines 6-9.

By the guarantees for
\mathsf{Densify},\mathsf{Sparsify}
in Cor.
5.2
, Lines 6-9 make an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad 2(k-a)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\hat{t},2\eta}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\quad\text{to}\quad 2(k-a)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\,,

where

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}=2\eta+(2k-2a)|\hat{t}-t^{\prime}|/2

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta+{(k-a)\xi}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta+\left(kt/t^{\prime}+1\right)\xi

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta+\xi+c\cdot\frac{t^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+\xi)}{t\xi}\cdot\frac{t}{t^{\prime}}\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=2\eta+{c(t^{\prime}+\xi)+\xi}

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta+{2ct^{\prime}}\leq 3\eta\,,

for sufficiently small
\xi
and
c=\left(2t^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\eta
.

Step 3: mapping
(2k-2a)\to k^{\prime}
in Line 10.

The final step is the
\mathsf{ReduceK}
reduction that, by guarantees in Lem.
5.4
, is an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad 2(k-a)\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{FULL}}(\tilde{n})\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime\prime}}(\left(\tilde{n}\right)^{2(k-a)/k^{\prime}})\,,

where
\eta^{\prime\prime}=\eta^{\prime}k^{\prime}/(k-a)/2\leq\eta^{\prime}
. This is a valid reduction, since Claim
10.1
guarantees that
(k-a)
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
10.1
.
∎

11
Reductions to Tensor PCA

In this section we survey reductions that map a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
instance with an arbitrary density
t\in(0,1]
to a Tensor PCA instance, i.e. an instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
with
t=1
. We compare two approaches: Discrete Equation Combination (
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
,
§​​ 7
) and Gaussian Equation Combination (
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
,
§​​ 9
). Both methods apply to both discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
settings, possibly after a preprocessing step that switches between distributions (
§​​ A.1.1
,
§​​ A.1.2
). For example, to apply
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
to a
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
instance, one can first discretize the input (Alg.
11
) the input, then apply the
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
reduction, and finally gaussianize the output (Alg.
10
) to obtain an instance of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
. For simplicity, we state results for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
here; the analogous results for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
follow similarly.

Tensor PCA model variants.

In our Tensor PCA model
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
(Def.
1.1
) we generate
m=n^{k}
noisy equations of the form

\mathsf{Y}=\delta x_{\alpha}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)

for independent
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
.

A closely related Tensor PCA model is
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
(see
§​​ 5.1
), which is computationally equivalent to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
(Prop.
5.6
), and therefore inherits all results below. The difference is purely in sampling procedure:
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
reveals exactly
M=\binom{n}{k}
observations – one for each index subset
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
, so it contains all distinct-index entries of

\delta\cdot x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}\,,

(TensorPCA)

where
\mathsf{G}
has i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
entries.

Finally,
§​​ 1
defined
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA}(n,\delta)
model (equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA_{\eta}}(n)
), which reveals
all
entries in Eq. (
TensorPCA
), including those with repeated indices. In
§​​ 5.1.3
we show that
k\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA_{\eta}}(n)
is computationally equivalent to a collection of independent
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
instances of orders
k,k-2,k-4,\dots
(Prop.
5.3
), and our reductions can be adapted to output this collection, see
§​​ 2.3
for intuition.

Consequently, any reduction stated below to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
immediately yields analogous reduction to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{wor}}_{t^{\prime}=1,\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
or
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}TensorPCA_{\eta^{\prime}}}(n^{\prime})
, without changing the parameters.

Reductions to Tensor PCA.

The
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
reduction in
§​​ 7
as a special case builds a reduction to Tensor PCA in the following regime. (Lemma
7.1
in
§​​ 7
is stated for the discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, we restate here the analogous result for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
, which holds by discrete-Gaussian equivalence in Prop.
5.1
).

Corollary 11.1

(of Lemma
7.1
: Discrete Equation Combination Reduction)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
t\in(0,1]
, and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
parameters. If
k^{\prime}
is even and satisfies

k^{\prime}\leq\min\left\{k\left(1-t\right),2kt\right\}\,,

(41)

then there exists a poly-time average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n)\,.

Consequently,
41
41

41

Since the reduction in Lemma
7.1
only maps to even
k^{\prime}
, an additional step of
k^{\prime}\to k^{\prime}/2
reduction in
§​​ 5.4
is required; it is applicable since reduction is Lemma
7.1
can be modified to map to a full
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
instance (see
§​​ 5.1
). Formally, this can be achieved by first splitting the input into
k^{\prime}/2
independent instances via
\mathsf{kSplit}
and then applying the reduction in Lemma
7.1
to every copy, mapping to instances of orders
k^{\prime},k^{\prime}-2,k^{\prime}-4,\dots
. It remains to apply
\mathsf{Compose}
(Alg.
15
) to obtain a full
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
instance.
for any
t\in\left(0,1\right)
,

\mathsf{CONJ}_{t}\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}_{1}\,.

The
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
reduction combines pairs of entries in the input and achieves noise independence by not reusing them – this works best for sparser instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
, i.e. small density
t
. Our
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
reduction in
§​​ 9
beats parameter tradeoffs in Cor.
11.1
in the case of denser input (larger
t
).

Lemma
9.1
.

(Gaussian Equation Combination: Reduction to Tensor PCA)

Let
t\in(1/3,1],\eta,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be the
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
parameters satisfying
-kt/2<\eta\leq(k-2)/2
. If
k^{\prime}
is even and satisfies

k^{\prime}<\min\left\{kt+2\eta,\frac{k\left(3t-1\right)}{2},\frac{k\left(1+t\right)}{4}\right\}\,,

(42)

then there exists a poly-time average-case reduction
42
42

42

As defined in Def.
C.6
for both detection and recovery (Alg.
5
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,

for
n^{\prime}=n\left(1-o(1)\right)
. Moreover, Alg.
5
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
.
43
43

43

Here
x^{\prime}
can be any subset of
n^{\prime}
coordinates of
x
by permutation invariance of the model. See Sec.
C.4
for why this is sufficient to transfer weak/strong/exact recovery.

Remark 11.1
.

In the case of
\eta\to 0
, i.e. at the
computational threshold
, the parameter regime above can be rewritten as follows: all even
k^{\prime}
satisfying:

1.

t\in(1/3,3/5)
:
k^{\prime}<\frac{k\left(3t-1\right)}{2}
;

2.

t\in(3/5,1]
:
k^{\prime}<\frac{k(1+t)}{4}
.

Comparison of Cor.
11.1
and Lemma
9.1

Both Cor.
11.1
(
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
) and Lemma
9.1
(
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
) obtain a reduction from

k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n)\,,

for even
k^{\prime}<k\cdot\tau
, where
\tau=\tau\left(t,\eta\right)
:

\displaystyle\text{$\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}$ (Cor.~\ref{cor:tensor_from_resolution})}:\quad\tau=\min\left\{1-t,2t\right\}

\displaystyle\text{$\mathsf{GaussEqComb}$ (Lem.~\ref{lem:gauss_resolution})}:\quad\tau=\min\left\{t+2\tilde{\eta},\frac{3t-1}{2},\frac{1+t}{4}\right\}\,,

where
-t/2<\tilde{\eta}\coloneq\eta/k<1/2-1/k
. For the instances inside the conjectured computationally hard region (
\tilde{\eta}>0
) the following hold:

1.

t\in(0,1/3]
: only the result of Cor.
11.1
(
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
) applies;

2.

t\in(1/3,3/5)
: both reductions are applicable, but Cor.
11.1
(
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
) gives a wider range of admissible
k^{\prime}
, i.e.
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
(Lem.
9.1
) does not achieve new results in this regime;

3.

t\in[3/5,1]
: both reductions are applicable, but Lem.
9.1
(
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
) gives a wider range of admissible
k^{\prime}
and shows new reductions not achievable with the
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
. In this case we can simplify
\tau=\min\left\{t+2\tilde{\eta},\frac{1+t}{4}\right\}
.

For
\eta>0
, we summarize the admissible range of parameters
k^{\prime}<k\tau
,
k^{\prime}
even, as follows:

\tau_{t,\eta/k}=\begin{cases}2t,&t\in\left(0,1/3\right)\\
1-t,&t\in[1/3,3/5)\\
\frac{1+t}{4},&t\in\left[3/5,1\right]\,.\end{cases}

(43)

Combined with our average-case reductions for decreasing the tensor order
k
in
§​​ 5.4
,
44
44

44

Both reductions in Cor.
11.1
and in Lem.
9.1
can be modified to map to a full
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
instance, so the results in Lem.
5.4
of
§​​ 5.4
are applicable and make a
k^{\prime}\to k^{\prime}/2
average-case reduction.
we obtain the following summary of average-case reductions to Tensor PCA. The equivalent statement for discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
holds from equivalence in Prop.
5.1
.

Corollary 11.2

(of Lem.
9.1
and Lem.
7.1
)
.

Let
t\in(0,1],\eta,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be the
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
parameters satisfying
0\leq\eta\leq(k-2)/2
. For
\tau_{t,\eta/k}
defined in Eq. (
43
), if
k^{\prime}
satisfies

k^{\prime}<\begin{cases}\tau_{t,\eta/k}\cdot k,&k^{\prime}\text{ even}\\
\tau_{t,\eta/k}/2\cdot k,&k^{\prime}\text{ odd}\,,\end{cases}

there exists a poly-time average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime}=1,2\eta}(n^{\prime})\,,

with
n^{\prime}=n^{\Theta(1)}
.

12
Reductions that Reduce the Tensor Order
k

We devote this section to average-case reductions from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
, i.e. reductions that change the tensor order
k\to k^{\prime}
at the same density
t
, focusing on the canonical cases
t\in\left\{0,1\right\}
. Fully analogous results for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
follow from the discrete-Gaussian equivalence (Prop.
5.1
). Our reductions are in Theorem
12.1
with the corresponding hardness implications in Corollary
12.1
.

Theorem 12.1

(Reductions that Reduce Tensor Order
k
)
.

There exists a poly-time average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (Alg.
8
,
9
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})

with
n^{\prime}=n^{\Theta(1)},\eta^{\prime}=O(\eta)
for the following parameter regimes:

1.

Canonical
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
,
t=0,\eta\geq 0
(Alg.
8
):

(a)

even
k^{\prime}
:
all
k>k^{\prime}
;

(b)

odd
k^{\prime}
:
all odd
k>k^{\prime}
and even
k\geq 2k^{\prime}
.

2.

Tensor PCA,
t=1,\eta\geq 0
(Alg.
9
):

(a)

even
k^{\prime}
:
all
k>3k^{\prime}/2
;

(b)

odd
k^{\prime}
:
all
k>3k^{\prime}
.

Moreover, the reductions map an instance of dimension
n
and signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one of dimension
n^{\prime}
and signal
x^{\prime}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
as follows.
45
45

45

See
§​​ C.4
for why this transfers weak/strong/exact recovery algorithms.
Denote
\left(\tilde{n},\tilde{x}\right)=\left(n(1-o(1)),x_{\left[1:\tilde{n}\right]}\right)
.

1.

If
k^{\prime}
is odd and either (i)
t=1
or (ii)
t=0
with

even:

n^{\prime}=\tilde{n}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad x^{\prime}=\tilde{x}^{\otimes 2}\odot y\,,

for a known

;

2.

Otherwise:

\left(n^{\prime},x^{\prime}\right)=\left(\tilde{n},\tilde{x}\right)\,.

Corollary 12.1
.

For parameters
t,k
, and
k^{\prime}
as above,

\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}\left(k^{\prime},t\right)\,.

(44)

In particular, for any
t\in\left\{0,1\right\}
, the relation Eq. (
44
) holds for any
k^{\prime}
and sufficiently large
k
.

The reductions in Theorem
12.1
map from an instance with larger parameter
k
to an instance with a smaller one. We conjecture (Conj.
1.1
) that such a reduction is indeed possible for any pair of parameters
k>k^{\prime}
, i.e. the missing cases of Theorem

are merely a limitation of our technique.

The results for
even

k^{\prime}
are special cases of the corresponding
\mathsf{EqComb}
reductions:
\mathsf{DiscrEqComb}
in Lemma
7.1
for

and
\mathsf{GaussEqComb}
,

,

for

.

To reach
odd

k^{\prime}

. The reduction in Lemma

and Lemmas

,

can be modified to map to an instance of the full

k^{\prime}

and then applying the discrete or Gaussian equation combination reduction to every copy, mapping to instances of orders

. It remains to apply
\mathsf{Compose}
(Alg.

For
t=0
and
k

) that combines two

.

. Let

\mathcal{Y}

.

Appendix A
Reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
Variants

For convenience, we restate our notation for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
model variants. We parametrize a
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
-like model by four choices. By default (i.e., model with no superscript) we use the first option in choices (2)-(4); superscripts indicate deviations from these defaults:

(1) Noise model

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}:\ \mathsf{Y}\sim x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}:\ \mathsf{Y}\sim\delta x_{\alpha}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)

(2) Number of samples

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:\ m\text{ samples}

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{Pois}}:\ m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)\text{ samples}

(3) Index space for
\alpha

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:\ \alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
(
k
-sets of distinct indices)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{FULL}}:\ \alpha\in[n]^{k}
(
k
-multisets, index repetitions allowed, Def.
A.1
)

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{asymm}}:\ \alpha\in[n]^{\underline{k}}
(ordered
k
-tuples of distinct indices)

(4) Sampling of
\alpha

[\checkmark]

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}:
with replacement

[
✓
]
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{wor}}:
without replacement

Figure 7:
Choices in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variants: the defaults are checked and correspond to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)
.

We use the
(t,\eta)
reparametrization for these models analogously to standard
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(see Def.
1.3
).

For example,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}}(n)
(equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) observes
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
independent entries
\left\{(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j})\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
for
\alpha_{j}\in\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
sampled without replacement and
\mathsf{Y}_{j}\sim\delta x_{\alpha_{j}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

A.1
Reductions between Discrete (
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
) and Gaussian (
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
) Models

In this section we recall known scalar reductions between Gaussian and Bernoulli variables that preserve signal level
\delta
up to log factors. Applying these to the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
models yields the following general equivalence, which will allow us to work with whichever model is more convenient.

Proposition
5.1
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (2)-(4) in Fig.
7
. The corresponding discrete
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
model and its Gaussian analog
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{Gaussianize}/\mathsf{Discretize}
, Alg.
10
,
11
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

As a consequence, proving a
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reduction from one of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
to one of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}^{\star}(n^{\prime})
or
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}^{\star}(n^{\prime})
gives the remaining three reductions by composition.

A.1.1
Gaussianization

First, we describe the guarantees for a subroutine that maps a pair of Bernoulli random variables with different means to a pair of Gaussian random variables with means differing by almost the same amount. Such entry-wise transformations of measure were devised in
[
64
]
,
[
48
]
, and later generalized in
[
11
]
. The following is a special case of Lemma 5.4 in
[
11
]
, applied to
\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2-p\right),\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2+p\right)
random variables:

Lemma A.1

(Gaussianization of
\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2-p\right),\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2+p\right)
)
.

Let
n
be a parameter and suppose that
p=p(n)
satisfies
n^{-O(1)}\leq p<c<1/2
for some constant
c
. Then there exists a constant
C(c)
such that for
\mu=\mu(n)\in(0,1)
defined as

\mu=\frac{Cp}{2\sqrt{6\log n+2\log(2p)^{-1}}}=\widetilde{\Theta}(p)

the following holds. There exists a map
\mathtt{RK}_{p}
that can be computed in
O(\mathrm{poly}(n))
-time and satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathtt{RK}_{p}(\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2+p\right)),\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)\big)=O_{n}(n^{-K}),\text{ and }d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathtt{RK}_{p}(\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2-p\right)),\mathcal{N}(0,1)\big)=O_{n}(n^{-K})\,,

for any constant
K
.

Remark A.1

(Gaussianization of Rademachers)
.

Using
-1
in place of
0
yields the same mapping guarantees for
\mathsf{Rad}(\pm p)
in place of
\mathsf{Ber}\left(1/2\pm p\right)
. We denote the corresponding map by
\mathtt{RK}_{p}^{\mathsf{Rad}}
.

The following Lemma applies
\mathtt{RK}_{p}^{\mathsf{Rad}}
entrywise to map from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}
and proves one direction of Prop.
5.1
.

Lemma A.2

(Gaussianization of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}
)
.

For any constant
k,t,\eta
, there exists an
O\left(\mathrm{poly}(n)\right)
-time average-case reduction (Alg.
10
) for both detection and recovery from

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)\,.

Moreover, given an input instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, Alg.
10
outputs an instance with the same signal vector
x
and the same nonzero entry locations.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
;
\delta>0
.

Output:

\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

1

\forall j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\leftarrow\mathtt{RK}_{\delta}^{\mathsf{Rad}}\left(\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\right)

2

Return
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 10

\mathsf{Gaussianize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}},\delta\right)

Proof.

Alg.
10
applies the Gaussianization kernel
\mathtt{RK}_{\delta}^{\mathsf{Rad}}
on every revealed entry of the input instance. In the planted case,
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
(where
m=n^{k(t+1)/2}
,
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) and by entry-wise total variation guarantees of Lemma
A.1
with an appropriate choice of constant
K=K(k)
, the output
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}=\mathsf{Gaussianize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}},\delta\right)
satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}(n,m,\delta^{\prime})\right)\leq o_{n}\left(1\right)\,,

where
\delta^{\prime}=\tilde{\Theta}(\delta)
.
In the null case, the input
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n)
has i.i.d.
\mathsf{Rad}\left(0\right)
entries. By Lemma
A.1
, for any choice of constant
K
,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathtt{RK}_{\delta}^{\mathsf{Rad}}\left(\mathsf{Rad}(0)\right),\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}\left(\delta^{\prime},1\right)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}\left(-\delta^{\prime},1\right)\right)\leq O_{n}\left(n^{-K}\right)\,,

for some
\delta^{\prime}=\tilde{\Theta}\left(\delta\right)
, and therefore, the output
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}=\mathsf{Gaussianize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}},\delta\right)
satisfies satisfies

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathsf{Gaussianize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}},\delta\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n)\right)\leq o_{n}(1)

for sufficiently large
K
(see Def.
C.1
for the null model definitions).

As the problem parameters are preserved up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors, this yields (Prop.
C.3
) the desired average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
for both detection and recovery.

∎

A.1.2
Discretization

In this section we describe a “reverse” procedure –
discretization
– that maps Gaussian random variables to Rademacher (or Bernoulli) random variables preserving the means up to polylog factors. This is achieved by a simple thresholding (
\mathrm{sign}
) operation; we let
\mathrm{sign}(0)\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right)
.

Lemma A.3

(Discretization of
\mathcal{N}(\mu,1),\mathcal{N}(0,1),\mathcal{N}(-\mu,1)
)
.

Let
n
be a parameter and suppose that
\mu=\mu(n)
satisfies
0<\mu<1/2
. Then there exists a sequence
p=p(n)\in(0,1)
, such that

p=\tilde{\Theta}(\mu)\,,

and a map
\mathsf{Discr}:\mathbb{R}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}
that can be computed in
O(1)
time that satisfies

\mathsf{Discr}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-\mu,1)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathsf{Rad}(0)\,,

\mathsf{Discr}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathsf{Rad}(p)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Discr}\left(\mathcal{N}(-\mu,1)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathsf{Rad}(-p)\,.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{Discr}:\mathbb{R}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}
be simply
\mathsf{Discr}(x)=\mathrm{sign}(x)
, where recall
\mathrm{sign}(0)\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right)
.
Then,

\Pr_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)}\left[\mathsf{Discr}(x)=1\right]=\Pr_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(-\mu,1)}\left[\mathsf{Discr}(x)=-1\right]=1/2+\Theta(\mu)

and

\Pr_{x\sim\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(-\mu,1)+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)}\left[\mathsf{Discr}(x)=1\right]=1/2\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

The following Lemma maps from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}
and proves the remaining direction of Prop.
5.1
.

Lemma A.4

(Discretization of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}
)
.

For any constant
k,t,\eta
, there exists an
O\left(n^{k}\right)
-time average-case reduction (Alg.
11
) for both detection and recovery from

k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)\,.

Moreover, given an input instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, Alg.
11
outputs an instance with the same signal vector
x
and the same nonzero entry locations.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\mathbb{R}\,\forall j\in\left[m\right]
.

Output:

\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}\,\forall j\in\left[m\right]
.

1

\forall j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Discr}\left(\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}_{j}\right)

2

Return
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 11

\mathsf{Discretize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}\right)

Proof.

Alg.
11
applies the thresholding operation
\mathsf{Discr}
from Lemma
A.3
to every revealed entry of the input instance. From LEmma
A.3
,
\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}=\mathsf{Discretize}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}\right)
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}\right)=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}(n,m,\delta^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if }\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{xor}}\right)=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n,m)\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\text{if }\mathcal{Y}^{\mathsf{tens}}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n,m,\delta)\,,

where
\delta^{\prime}=\Theta(\delta)
. As the problem parameters are preserved up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors, this yields (Prop.
C.3
) an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\star}(n)\,.

∎

A.2
Poisson Entry Sampling

In this section we formally prove standard reductions between
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
with exactly
m
entries and the
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
in which there are
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
entries; this is a widely used equivalence, see, e.g.,
[
24
]
. The following proposition shows the equivalence of the two settings in a variety of models from Fig.
7
(see
§​​ 5.1
for overview of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
variants).

Proposition
5.2
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be fixed model choices (1,3,4) in Fig.
7
. Then the fixed-sample
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and its Poissonized variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ResamplePois}/\mathsf{ResampleM}
, Alg.
12
,
13
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
.

1

//
Sample the number of observed equations
m_{\mathsf{p}}
in the output instance

\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(m/2\right)
,
m_{\mathsf{p}}\leftarrow\min\left(m,\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\right)

2

//
Return exactly
m_{\mathsf{p}}
equations

Return
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 12

\mathsf{ResamplePois}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
.

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m^{\prime}\right]}
.

1

//
Compute the number of observed equations
m^{\prime}
in the output instance

\widetilde{m}\leftarrow\lfloor m/2\rfloor
,
m^{\prime}\leftarrow\min\left(\widetilde{m},m_{\mathsf{p}}\right)

2

//
Return exactly
m^{\prime}
equations

Return
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m^{\prime}\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 13

\mathsf{ResampleM}\left(\mathcal{Y},m\right)

Proof of Prop.
5.2
.

The analysis for the planted and null cases is the same for both
\mathsf{ResampleM}
and
\mathsf{ResamplePois}
.

Correctness of
\mathsf{ResamplePois}
(Alg.
12
).
By the concentration of Poisson random variable (Prop.
6.2
), for any
c>0
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}\leq\frac{1}{2}m+C\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}m}\log n\leq m\,.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
, in Alg.
12
,
m_{\mathsf{p}}=\widetilde{m}_{\mathsf{p}}
, and thus,
\mathcal{Z}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}(n,m/2,\delta)
if
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
. By the properties of total variation distance (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}(n,m/2,\delta)\right)\leq n^{-c}\,,

yielding an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}_{t,\eta}(n)
(since it is sufficient to map parameters up to constant factors by Prop.
C.3
).

Correctness of
\mathsf{ResampleM}
(Alg.
13
).
Since
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
, by the concentration of Poisson random variable (Prop.
6.2
), for any
c>0
, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

m_{\mathsf{p}}\geq m-C\sqrt{m}\log n\geq\frac{1}{2}m\geq\widetilde{m}\,.

Therefore, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
, in Alg.
13
,
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{m}=\lfloor m/2\rfloor
, and thus,
\mathcal{Z}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,{m^{\prime}},\delta)
if
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}(n,{m},\delta)
. By the properties of total variation distance (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,{m^{\prime}},\delta)\right)\leq n^{-c}\,,

yielding an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{Pois}}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
(since it is sufficient to map parameters up to constant factors by Prop.
C.3
).

∎

A.3
Repeated indices in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}

While
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(Def.
1.1
) consists of entries
(\alpha,\mathsf{Y})
with
k
-sets of distinct indices
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
, the Tensor PCA literature often considers models that allow index repetitions. For example, the standard
\delta x^{\otimes k}+\mathsf{G}
model, where
\mathsf{G}
is a symmetric tensor of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, includes the entries with repeated indices. We show that this “full” variant (
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{FULL}}
) that allows index repetitions is computationally equivalent to a collection of independent instances of the models with no index repetitions of orders
k,k-2,k-4,\dots
:

Proposition
5.3
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}
and a Collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
)

Fix
k\in\mathbb{Z},t\in\left[0,1\right],\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and any choices (1,2,4)
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
in Fig.
7
. Then,

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}_{t,\eta}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\text{and}\quad\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t_{r},\eta}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}\qquad\text{with }t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}

are computationally equivalent: there exist poly-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{Decompose}/\mathsf{Compose}
, Alg.
14
,
15
) both ways.
46
46

46

Here the second model is a collection of independent
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
instances.
These reductions preserve the input signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
exactly.

We formally prove Prop.
5.3
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
model (see
§​​ A.2
). In
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
one observes
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
independent samples
(\alpha,\mathsf{Y})
with

\mathsf{Y}\sim\delta x_{\alpha}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)

(
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
) and i.i.d. index sets
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
. The only difference in
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
is that
\alpha\in\left\{n\right\}^{k}
are
k
-multisets sampled from a natural measure
\mu
from Def.
A.1
.

The analogous results for the
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}
variants with discrete entries and/or fixed-
m
sampling follow by the computational equivalence of these models (see
§​​ 5.1.1
,
§​​ 5.1.2
and
§​​ A.1
,
§​​ A.2
) and the proof similarly holds for
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\mathsf{wor}}
models, which sample index sets without replacement.

Definition A.1

(
k
-Multiset of
\left[n\right]
)
.

A
k
-multiset
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
is an unordered collection of (not necessarily distinct)
k
elements in
\left[n\right]
. We denote the set of all
k
-multisets of
\left[n\right]
as
\left\{n\right\}^{k}
and define a measure
\mu
on
\left\{n\right\}^{k}
as:

\mu\left(\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\right)\propto\big|\left(j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\right)\in\left[n\right]^{k}:\,\left\{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\right\}=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\big|\qquad\text{for all }\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}\in\left\{n\right\}^{k}\,.

A.3.1
Proof Sketch for Prop.
5.3

The goal is to show that
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
is equivalent to a collection of
(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t_{r},\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
instances for all
r=0,1,\dots,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor
sharing the same signal vector
x
:

\displaystyle k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t_{r},\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}\,,\qquad\text{where }t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}\,.

From
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
to a collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
.

For a target order
k^{\prime}=k-2r
for some
r\in\left[0,\frac{k-1}{2}\right]
, the reduction finds in the input instance
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
equations
\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)
such that

\alpha_{j}=\beta_{j}\cup\left\{i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{r},i_{r}\right\}\text{ for }\beta\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k^{\prime}}\text{ and }i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in\left[n\right]\,.

(45)

For
\alpha_{j}
as above,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta\cdot x_{\beta_{j}}x_{i_{1}}x_{i_{1}}\dots x_{i_{r}}x_{i_{r}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)=\delta\cdot x_{\beta_{j}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,,

where
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
.
Then the equation
\left(\beta_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)
is distributed as an entry of
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
with signal level
\delta
. The expected number of
\alpha_{j}
satisfying Eq. (
45
) is
\approx m\cdot n^{-r}=n^{k(1+t)/2-r}
. The reduction constructs the output
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
out of such equations
\left(\beta_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)
differently, depending on whether there are few (
k(1+t)/2-r\leq k^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\frac{kt}{k-2r}\leq 1
) or many (
k(1+t)/2-r>k^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\frac{kt}{k-2r}>1
) of them available.

•

Sparse case: few equations (
\frac{kt}{k-2r}\leq 1
).
The reduction returns
m^{\prime}
equations with signal level
\delta^{\prime}=\delta
, where

m^{\prime}\approx n^{k(1+t)/2-r}=n^{k^{\prime}(1+t_{r})/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}=n^{-k^{\prime}t_{r}-\eta/2}\,,

where
t_{r}=\frac{kt}{k-2r}
.

•

Dense case: many equations (
\frac{kt}{k-2r}>1
).
Since there are more than
n^{k^{\prime}}
equations available, the reduction aggregates the ones with the same index sets be averaging the corresponding values
\mathsf{Y}_{\alpha_{j}}
. As we will have
\approx n^{k(1+t)/2-r}n^{-k^{\prime}}=n^{k(-1+t)/2+r}
equations for every index support, the resulting parameters are
m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}
:

m^{\prime}\approx n^{k^{\prime}}=n^{k^{\prime}(1+t_{r})/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}\approx\delta\cdot n^{k(-1+t)/4+r/2}=n^{-k^{\prime}t_{r}/4-\eta/2}\,,

where
t_{r}=1
.

From a collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
.

The reverse reduction combines the entries of the collections of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
instances in one
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
, where the reverse procedure to the aggregation in Case 2 above is the cloning procedure (
§​​ B.1
).

Algorithm 14

\mathsf{Decompose}\left(\mathcal{Y},t\right)

From
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
to a collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
: Guarantees for
\mathsf{Decompose}(\mathcal{Y},t)
.

We show that Alg.
14
is an average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
, with
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
equations to a collection
\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t_{r},\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}
with
t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}
. We show the proof for the planted case and the same argument holds for the null case.

Denote the input
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
and let
m=n^{k(1+t)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
, so that
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
.
\mathsf{Decompose}\left(\mathcal{Y},t\right)
returns a collection of instances
\left\{\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}\right\}_{r}
, which are independent, since the entries of
\mathcal{Y}
are not reused for different
r
. Moreover, for every
r
in the loop, the collection
\mathcal{Z}
contains independent pairs
\left(\beta_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\right)
for
\beta_{j}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left(\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-2r}\right)
and

\mathsf{Z}_{j}=\delta\cdot x_{\beta_{j}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,.

Note that by Poisson splitting,
\mathcal{Y}
contains
\mathsf{Pois}\left(m\mu(\alpha_{j})\right)
equations with index multiset
\alpha_{j}\in\left\{n\right\}^{k}
, where
\mu
is the measure defined in Def.
A.1
. Then,
\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}=\mathcal{Z}
contains
\mathsf{Pois}\left(\Theta\left(mn^{-r}n^{-(k-2r)}\right)\right)
entries for each
\beta\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-2r}
.

Case 1: few entries in
\mathcal{Z}
(
\frac{kt}{k-2r}\leq 1
).

In this case, by the above,

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}\right)=(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,,

where

m^{\prime}=\Theta(mn^{-r})=\Theta\left(n^{k^{\prime}(1+t_{r})/2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\delta^{\prime}=\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}=n^{-(k-2r)t_{r}/4-\eta/2}\,,

and
t_{r}=\frac{kt}{k-2r}
. Since it is sufficient to map parameters up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors (Prop
C.3
), this concludes the proof of the average-case reduction.

Case 2: many entries in
\mathcal{Z}
(
\frac{kt}{k-2r}>1
).

By Poisson concentration in Prop.
6.2
, since
|\mathcal{N}_{\beta}=\left\{j\in\left[|\mathcal{Z}|\right]:\,\beta_{j}=\beta\right\}|\sim\mathsf{Pois}\left(\Theta\left(mn^{-r}n^{-(k-2r)}\right)\right)
, with high probability simultaneously for all
\beta\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k-2r}
,

|\mathcal{N}_{\beta}|\geq\widetilde{\Theta}(1)\cdot mn^{-r}n^{-(k-2r)}=C\cdot n^{k(-1+t)/2+r}\eqcolon N_{r}\,,

where
C=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
hides factors constant in
k,r
and
\mathrm{poly}\log n
. Conditioned on this event, which we denote,
S_{N}
, every entry of
\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}
is an average of
N_{r}
observations of the form
\delta\cdot x_{\beta}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Then,

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}|S_{N}\right)=(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}tPCA}^{\mathsf{Gauss}}(n,\delta^{\prime})\,,

where

\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(\delta\cdot n^{k(-1+t)/4+r/2}\right)=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-(k-2r)t_{r}/4-\eta/2}\right)\,,

where
t_{r}=1
. Therefore, by conditioning in total variation (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}\right),(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}tPCA}^{\mathsf{Gauss}}(n,\delta^{\prime})\right)\leq\Pr\left[S_{N}^{c}\right]=o_{n}(1)\,.

Since it is sufficient to map parameters up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors (Prop
C.3
), the average-case reduction follows from the guarantees for the
\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
algorithm in Prop.
5.6
.

From a collection of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
: Guarantees for
\mathsf{Compose}(\left\{\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}\right\}_{r},t)
.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{(k-2r)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(k-2r)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(k-2r)}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(k-2r)}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j}^{(k-2r)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(k-2r)}\big)\in[n]^{k-2r}\times\mathbb{R}
for
0\leq r\leq\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor
,
t\in\left[0,1\right]

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\beta_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\beta_{j},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\mathbb{R}
.

1

for

r\in\left[0,\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor\right]

do

2

3

if

\frac{kt}{k-2r}>1

then

4

5

\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(k-2r)}\leftarrow\mathsf{ToWORSampling}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}\right)

6

forall

j\in\binom{n}{k-2r}

do

7

8

//
Here
\mathsf{GaussClone}(\cdot,A)
is the
\mathsf{GaussClone}(\cdot)
procedure repeated
\lceil\log A\rceil
times to obtain
A
clones.

\mathsf{Y}^{1},\dots,\mathsf{Y}^{A_{r}}\leftarrow\mathsf{GaussClone}(\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(k-2r)},A_{r})

//
See pf. of Prop.
5.3
for def. of
A_{r}

10

\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}\leftarrow\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\left(\mathcal{Z}^{(k-2r)}\right)

12

else

13

\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(k-2r)}\leftarrow\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}

14

//
Create a random output
k
-sparse equation out of each input one

15

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

16

forall

j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(k-2r)}\right]

do

17

\beta\leftarrow\mu_{\alpha_{j}^{(k-2r)}}
, where
\mu_{\alpha},\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
is law of
\mu
conditioned on the output equal to
\alpha\cup\left\{i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{r},i_{r}\right\},\text{ where }i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in\left[n\right]\alpha

18

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\left\{\left(\beta,\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(k-2r)}\right)\right\}

Algorithm 15

\mathsf{Compose}\left(\left\{\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}\right\}_{r},t\right)

We show that Alg.
15
is an average-case reduction from a collection
\Big\{(k-2r)\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t_{r},\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)\Big\}_{0\leq r\leq\frac{k-1}{2}}
with
t_{r}=\min\left\{\frac{kt}{k-2r},1\right\}
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
. We show the proof for the planted case and the same argument holds for the null case.

Let the inputs
\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t_{r},\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
be
\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{(k-2r)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(k-2r)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{(k-2r)}\right]}
and denote
m_{r}=n^{(k-2r)(1+t_{r})/2},\delta_{r}=n^{-(k-2r)t_{r}/4-\eta/2}
.
\mathsf{Compose}\left(\left\{\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}\right\}_{r},t\right)
returns an instance
\mathcal{Z}
and we will show that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)=o_{n}(1)\,,

where
m^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{k(1+t)/2}\right),\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}\left(n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}\right)
match the parameters of
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n)
up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors and constants. By Prop.
C.3
, this proves the desired average-case reduction.

Consider any index set
\beta\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
and assume
\beta=\alpha\cup\left\{i_{1},i_{1},\dots,i_{r},i_{r}\right\},\text{ where }i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in\left[n\right]
for some
r\in\left[0,\frac{k-1}{2}\right]
, i.e.,
\alpha
is the set of distinct indices that are included an odd number of times in
\beta
.

Case 1:
\frac{kt}{k-2r}\leq 1
.

In this case, by Poisson splitting, since the input instance
\mathcal{Y}^{(k-2r)}
contained
\mathsf{Pois}\left(m\cdot\binom{n}{k-2r}^{-1}\right)
equations with the index set
\alpha
, the output instance has
\mathsf{Pois}\left(m_{r}\cdot\binom{n}{k-2r}^{-1}\cdot\Theta(n^{-r})\right)
equations with the index set
\beta
, where

m_{r}\cdot\binom{n}{k-2r}^{-1}\cdot\Theta(n^{-r})=\Theta\left(n^{(k-2r)(1+t_{r})/2-(k-2r)-r}\right)=\Theta\left(n^{k(t-1)/2}\right)\,.

Case 2:
\frac{kt}{k-2r}>1
.

In this case we again want
\mathsf{Pois}\left(\Theta\left(n^{k(t-1)/2}\right)\right)
equations with index set
\beta
in the output index and we set
A_{r}/2=\Theta\left(n^{k(t-1)/2}\right)\cdot\Theta\left(n^{r}\right)
for the appropriate choice of constant. Then, with high probability, the output will contain
\mathsf{Pois}\left(A_{r}/2\cdot\Theta(n^{-r})\right)
equations with this index set, each independent of the form
\delta^{\prime}\cdot x_{\beta}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, where

\delta^{\prime}=\delta\cdot\Theta\left(A_{r}^{-1/2}n^{r/2}\right)=\Theta\left(n^{-(k-2r)/4-\eta/2-k(t-1)/4}\right)=\Theta\left(n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}\right)\,.

Denote this high probability event
S_{r}
and their union
S=S_{1}\cap\dots\cap S_{\lfloor\frac{k-1}{2}\rfloor}
.

The two cases above show that

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}|S\right)=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})

for the appropriate
m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}
up to
\mathrm{poly}\log n
factors. By conditioning on event in total variation (Fact
6.1
), we conclude that

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right),k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\right)=\Pr\left[S^{c}\right]=o_{n}(1)\,.

Remark A.2

(Discrete Inputs to Alg.
14
,
15
)
.

While Algorithms
14
and
15
, as stated, are reductions for the Gaussian
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}}
models, we sometimes use them as subroutines for Discrete models and/or models with the fixed number of samples. It is implicitly assumed that first a Gaussianization (
§​​ A.1.1
) and/or Poissonization (
§​​ A.2
) reduction is applied, then the appropriate algorithm, and then the discretization (
§​​ A.1.2
) and/or Fixed-Sample (
§​​ A.2
) reduction.

A.4
Symmetrization and Symmetry Breaking

While the standard option for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
sampling has index sets
\alpha\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}
, the “asymmetric” variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{asymm}}
instead indexes equations by
ordered tuples of distinct indices

\alpha\in\left[n\right]^{\underline{k}}
. We show that in the case when
\alpha
are sampled with replacement, the two models are computationally equivalent.

Proposition
5.4
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{asymm}}
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be any fixed choices (1,2) in Fig.
7
and choice (4) set to sampling with replacement. Then,
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and the corresponding “asymmetric” variant
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{asymm}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{DeSymm}/\mathsf{Symm}
, Alg.
16
,
17
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

We prove Prop.
5.4
for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}
model. Recall that in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
(equiv.
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n,m,\delta)
with
m=n^{k(1+t)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4}
) there are
m_{\mathsf{p}}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m)
independent entries
(\alpha,\mathsf{Y})
with
\mathsf{Y}\sim x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
and
\alpha\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
. The only difference in
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{asymm}}(n)
is that
\alpha
are sampled i.i.d. from
\left[n\right]^{\underline{k}}
– the set of ordered
k
-tuples of distinct indices.

The analogous results for the variants with Gaussian entries and/or fixed-
m
sampling follow by the computational equivalence of these models (see
§​​ 5.1.1
,
§​​ 5.1.2
).

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\mathbb{R}

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in[n]^{\underline{k}}\times\mathbb{R}
.

1

\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\sigma_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\mathcal{S}_{k}\right]
and set

\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\sigma_{j}(\alpha_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Z}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Y}_{j}

2

Return
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 16

\mathsf{DeSymm}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\in[n]^{\underline{k}}\times\mathbb{R}

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
, where
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
,
\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\in\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\times\mathbb{R}
.

1

\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]
, set

\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathsf{set}(\alpha_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Z}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Y}_{j}

2

Return
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
.

3

Algorithm 17

\mathsf{Symm}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

Proof of Prop.
5.4
.

Given an input
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois}}(n)
, the reduction
\mathsf{DeSymm}
(Alg.
16
) randomly orders the index sets
\left\{\alpha_{j}\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}
and preserves the equation values
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
intact. Given an input
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{asymm}}(n)
, the reduction
\mathsf{Symm}
(Alg.
17
) “removes” the index order from the observations, preserving the equation values
\mathsf{Y}_{j}
intact.

By Poisson splitting, the above are the desired average-case reductions in both planted and null cases.
∎

A.5
Entry Sampling without Replacement

In this section we show:

Proposition
5.5
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
for Recovery)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
7
. The two models
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent for recovery: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ToWORSampling}/\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
, Alg.
18
,
19
) for recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

Additionally, we prove the complete computational equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
(for both detection and recovery) for
t=1
:

Proposition
5.6
.

(Computational Equivalence of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}
for
t=1
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
7
and let
\eta>0
. The two models
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
are computationally equivalent: there are
\mathrm{poly}(n)
-time average-case reductions (
\mathsf{ToWORSampling}/\mathsf{ToWRSampling}
, Alg.
18
,
19
) for both detection and recovery in both directions. Moreover, the reductions preserve the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

Both propositions are proved via two simple transformations. In the direction “with replacement
\to
without replacement”, we delete duplicates by keeping one noisy observation per distinct index set (Alg.
18
). In the reverse direction, we synthetically introduce duplicates using the cloning procedure from
§​​ B.1.1
(Alg.
19
).

1

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
,
t\in\left[0,1\right]
;

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m^{\prime}\right]}
.

2

\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\left\{\alpha_{j}\right\}_{j\in m}

//
\mathcal{I}
is the set of distinct index sets appearing in
\mathcal{Y}

3

\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\text{random }\min\left\{n^{k(1+t)/2}/4,|\mathcal{I}|\right\}\text{-subset of }\mathcal{I}

4

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

5

//
Pick a random input noisy observation for each index set

forall

\alpha\in\mathcal{I}

do

6

\mathsf{Z}_{\alpha}\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\mathsf{Y}_{j}:\,\alpha_{j}=\alpha\right\}\right]

7

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\left\{(\alpha,\mathsf{Z}_{\alpha})\right\}

8

Return
\mathcal{Z}
.

9

Algorithm 18

\mathsf{ToWORSampling}\left(\mathcal{Y},t\right)

1

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
;

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m^{\prime}\right]}
.

2

Sample
m
elements
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}
uniformly from
\left[|\mathsf{A}|\right]
with replacement

3

Sample
s=|\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i\in\left[m\right]}|
elements
\alpha^{(1)},\dots,\alpha^{(s)}
from
\left\{\alpha_{j}\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
at random without replacement

4

Let
b_{1},\dots,b_{s}
be a random enumeration of distinct elements
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i\in\left[m\right]}

5

//
Compute maximum multiplicity of sampling with replacement

For all
i\in\left[s\right]
:
m_{i}=|\left\{j\in\left[m\right]:\,a_{j}=b_{i}\right\}|

6

m_{c}\leftarrow\max_{i\in\left[s\right]}m_{i}

7

\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{Y}^{(m_{c})}\leftarrow\mathsf{kXORClone}\left(\mathcal{Y},m_{c}\right)

//
\mathsf{kGaussClone}
if
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
; see Alg.
20
, Rmk.
5.2

8

\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset

forall

i\in\left[s\right]

do

9

Let
j
be the unique index s.t.
\alpha_{j}=\alpha^{(i)}

10

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(t)}\right)\right\}_{t=1}^{m_{i}}

11

Return
\mathcal{Z}
.

12

Algorithm 19

\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\left(\mathcal{Y},\mathsf{A}=\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right)

Proof.

of Prop.
5.5
and Prop.
5.6

Alg.
18
: from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t,\eta}(n)
.
Let
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
be drawn from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
(or the corresponding null distribution), where
\alpha_{j}
are sampled uniformly from
\mathsf{A}
with replacement and
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
). Alg.
18
forms the set
\mathcal{I}=\left\{\alpha_{j}\right\}_{j\in[m]}
of distinct index sets appearing in
\mathcal{Y}
, then selects

m^{\prime}=\min\left\{n^{k(1+t)/2}/4,|\mathcal{I}|\right\}

distinct indices from
\mathcal{I}
, and for each chosen
\alpha
outputs one uniformly random associated observation. Hence the output
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{ToWORSampling}\left(\mathcal{Y},t\right)
satisfies

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)=k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}(n,m^{\prime},\delta)\,.

It remains to show that
m^{\prime}=n^{k(1+t)/2}/4
with high probability. The map
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m})\to|\mathcal{I}|
is 1-Lipschitz, so, combined with the standard bounds on
\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{I}|
, we have, with high probability,

|\mathcal{I}|\geq m/2\,.

In the Poissonized case (
\mathsf{Pois}\in\star
) we have

m\sim\mathsf{Pois}(n^{k(1+t)/2})
, so by Poisson concentration (Prop.
6.2
),
m\geq n^{k(1+t)/2}/2
. Therefore, for all models, with high probability,
m^{\prime}=n^{k(1+t)/2}/4
. By conditioning on this high probability event in
d_{\mathsf{TV}}
(Fact
6.1
), we conclude

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Z}),k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}(n,m^{\prime}=n^{k(1+t)/2}/4,\delta)\right)=o_{n}(1)\,,

which yields an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery, since it is sufficient to map parameters
m,\delta
up to constant factors (Prop.
C.3
).

Alg.
19
: from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)
.
We first consider the planted case. Let
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
be drawn from
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{wor}}_{t,\eta}(n)
, where
\alpha_{j}
are sampled from a set
\mathsf{A}
without replacement and denote
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
,
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
).
Alg.
19
first samples i.i.d. labels
a_{1},\dots,a_{m}\in\left[|\mathsf{A}|\right]
(with replacement), computes their multiplicities
\left\{m_{i}\right\}
and the maximum
m_{c}=\max_{i}m_{i}
, then produces
m_{c}
independent clones of the input instance and uses them to realize the desired multiplicity pattern. By Lemma
5.1
, the output
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{ToWRSampling}\left(\mathcal{Y},\mathsf{A}\right)
has independent entries and the correct “with replacement” index distribution; moreover the entrywise signal level is

\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta/\sqrt{m_{c}})\,.

Therefore, in the planted case,

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)=k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta^{\prime})\,.

It remains to control
m_{c}
:
in the standard “
m
balls into
|\mathsf{A}|\geq m
bins” model, with high probability, the maximum load
m_{c}=\max_{i\in\left[s\right]}m_{i}=\widetilde{\Theta}(1)
, yielding
\delta^{\prime}=\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta)
. Since it is sufficient to map parameters
m,\delta
up to polylog factors (Prop.
C.3
), this proves that Alg.
19
is an average-case reduction for recovery.

In the null case, cloning replicates the random
\pm 1
signal component (see Def.
C.1
), so the output does not exactly match the intended null distribution. When
t=1
, however, the null model is
o_{n}(1)
-close in total variation to the distribution with
\delta=0
(Prop.
C.1
); in this special case the same argument yields the reduction for detection as well, completing Prop.
5.6
.

∎

Appendix B
Simple Reductions

B.1
Instance Cloning and Splitting

As described in
§​​ 5.2.1
, cloning and splitting reductions map one input instance
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
to two output instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
sharing the same signal
x
.

B.1.1
Cloning Reduction

Cloning keeps the same index sets as the input but ensures the noise independence across the two outputs:

Lemma
5.1
.

(Instance Cloning)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
7
. There exists a poly-time algorithm (
\mathsf{kXORClone}/\mathsf{kGaussClone}
, Alg.
20
) that, given an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
, returns two instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta))
that have independent entrywise noise, the same index sets, and the same signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as the input
\mathcal{Y}
.

1

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

Output:

\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
, where
\mathcal{Y}^{(a)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(a)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

2

For each
j\in\left[m\right]
:

\left(\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(1)},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(2)}\right)\leftarrow\mathsf{GaussClone}\left(\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)

3

Return
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
, where
\mathcal{Y}^{(a)}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}^{(a)}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
for
a\in\left\{1,2\right\}
.

4

Algorithm 20

\mathsf{kGaussClone}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

The reduction proving Lemma
5.1
uses the standard Gaussian-cloning identity (e.g.
[
11
]
): given
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
, generate an independent
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and set
Y^{(1)}=\frac{Y-Z}{\sqrt{2}}
,
Y^{(2)}=\frac{Y+Z}{\sqrt{2}}
. Then
Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)}
are independent and each is distributed as
\mathcal{N}(\mu/\sqrt{2},1)
. The primitive is applied entrywise for
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
inputs (
\mathsf{kGaussClone}
Alg.
20
). For
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
inputs, we first reduce to the Gaussian analog via
§​​ 5.1.1
, apply the cloning primitive entrywise, then (if needed) map back; we denote this algorithm
\mathsf{kXORClone}
.

We sometimes apply the cloning reduction recursively to obtain more than two clones (Rmk.
5.2
): we denote the corresponding maps to
A>2
clones
\mathsf{kGaussClone}(\mathcal{Y},A)
and
\mathsf{kXORClone}(\mathcal{Y},A)
.

Proof of Lemma
5.1
.

For the
\mathsf{GaussClone}
primitive above, we have the following guarantee:

Proposition B.1

(Gaussian Cloning)
.

There exists an
O(1)
-time algorithm
\mathsf{GaussClone}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that given
Y\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,1)
as an input,
\mathsf{GaussClone}(Y)
outputs
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
that satisfy

\mathcal{L}(Y_{1},Y_{2})=\mathcal{N}(\mu/\sqrt{2},1)\times\mathcal{N}(\mu/\sqrt{2},1)\,,

i.e.,
Y_{1},Y_{2}
are sampled independently from
\mathcal{N}(\mu/\sqrt{2},1)
. Note that the algorithm works simultaneously for all values
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.

For
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
inputs,
\mathsf{kGaussClone}
(Alg.
20
) applies
\mathsf{GaussClone}
entrywise, so the Lemma statement follows from Prop.
B.1
.

For
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
inputs,
\mathsf{kXORClone}
first applies Gaussianization, then
\mathsf{kGaussClone}
, and then Discretization. The lemma follows from Prop.
B.1
and Gaussianization/Discretization guarantees in
§​​ 5.1.1
.
∎

B.1.2
Splitting Reduction

Splitting makes the two outputs fully independent instances (independent indices and independent noise), while preserving the same signal
x
.

Lemma
5.2
.

(Instance Splitting)
Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-3) in Fig.
7
and choice (4) set to sampling with replacement. There exists a poly-time algorithm (
\mathsf{kSplit}
) that, given an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
, returns two independent instances
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,\Theta(m),\delta)
that share the same signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as the input
\mathcal{Y}
.

Remarks
5.1
,
5.2
similarly apply. Lemma
5.2
follows from a simple entry-allocation argument in two cases:

•

If
\mathsf{Pois}\not\in\star
, we randomly partition
m
input samples into two groups of size
m/2
to form the outputs
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
. This achieves a reduction to two instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m/2,\delta)
.

•

If
\mathsf{Pois}\in\star
, we allocate each of
m
input entries to
\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}
or
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
independently at random. This yields a reduction to two instances of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m/2,\delta)
by Poisson splitting.

We denote the reductions above as
\mathsf{kSplit}(\mathcal{Y})
. Similarly to cloning, we write
\mathsf{kSplit}(\mathcal{Y},A)
to denote a reduction that does splitting several times to output
A
instances (Rmk.
5.2
).

B.2
Reductions that Change the Number of Revealed Equations
m

We prove simple reductions that modify the number of samples in
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
from
m
to
m^{\prime}
by either (1) injecting pure noise observations (
\mathsf{Densify}
), or (2) removing some of the samples (
\mathsf{Sparsify}
). Lemma
5.3
and Corollary
5.2
track the resulting change in signal level
\delta
(or, in
t,\eta
parametrization, the deficiency
\eta
).

Lemma
5.3
.

(Adjusting the Number of Samples
m
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
7
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
and
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be parameters. There exists a poly-time average-case reduction (
\mathsf{Densify}/\mathsf{Sparsify}
, Alg.
21
,
22
) for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})\,,\qquad\text{with }\delta^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\delta\,,&m^{\prime}\leq m\\
\widetilde{\Theta}(\delta\cdot m/m^{\prime})\,,&m^{\prime}>m\,.\end{cases}

Moreover, the reduction preserves the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

In the
t,\eta
parametrization this yields:

Corollary
5.2
.

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{FULL}/\mathsf{asymm},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1-4) in Fig.
7
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
, and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters. There exists a poly-time average-case reduction (
\mathsf{Densify}/\mathsf{Sparsify}
, Alg.
21
,
22
) for both detection and recovery

\displaystyle\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n)\,,\quad\text{where }\eta^{\prime}=\eta+{k|t-t^{\prime}|/2}\,.

Moreover, the reduction preserves the secret signal vector
x
precisely.

As stated,
\mathsf{Densify}
(Alg.

) and
\mathsf{Sparsify}
(Alg.

) accept and return discrete Poissonized
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}

k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
with
\mathsf{Pois}\in\star
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
. The existence of analogous reductions for fixed-
m

k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
follows from equivalence of options in choices (1,2) (
§​​ 5.1.1
,
§​​ 5.1.2
).

,
