Algorithm 22

\mathsf{Sparsify}\left(\mathcal{Y},m,m^{\prime}\right)

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

The reduction algorithm is either
\mathsf{Densify}
(Alg.
21
) or
\mathsf{Sparsify}
(Alg.
22
) depending on whether
m<m^{\prime}
or
m>m^{\prime}
. We show that for
\mathsf{Pois}\in\star
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(i.e. the first option in choice (1) and the second option in choice (2) of Fig.
7
) these reductions map
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
to
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}(n,m^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})
for
\delta^{\prime}
as in Lemma
5.3
; the analogous results for
\mathsf{Pois}\not\in\star
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
follow from the option equivalence in choices (1,2) (
§​​ 5.1.1
,
§​​ 5.1.2
).

First, we demonstrate that the corresponding algorithm correctly maps the planted distributions. Let
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}(n,m,\delta)
for
\mathsf{Pois}\in\star
.

•

Case 1:
m<m^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{Densify}\left(\mathcal{Y},m,m^{\prime}\right)
. In
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, index sets
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
. Moreover, the distribution of
\mathcal{Z}
is such that
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m^{\prime})
and
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,

Z_{j}\sim\begin{cases}x_{\alpha_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)&\text{w. prob. }m/m^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{Rad}(0)&\text{w. prob. }1-m/m^{\prime}\,.\end{cases}

We conclude that
\mathcal{Z}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}(n,{m^{\prime}},{\delta^{\prime}})
for
\delta^{\prime}=\delta\cdot m/m^{\prime}
.

•

Case 2:
m>m^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{Sparsify}\left(\mathcal{Y},m,m^{\prime}\right)
. In
\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]}
, index sets
\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\sim_{i.i.d.}\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
. Moreover, the distribution of
\mathcal{Z}
is such that
m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Pois}(m^{\prime})
and
\forall j\in\left[m_{\mathsf{p}}^{\prime}\right]
,

Z_{j}\sim x_{\alpha_{j}^{\prime}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)\,.

We conclude that
\mathcal{Z}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star}(n,{m^{\prime}},{\delta})
.

In the null case of
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n,m)
(Def.
C.1
), by the same analysis,
\mathcal{Z}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}^{\star,\mathsf{NULL}}(n,m^{\prime})
, which yields an average-case reduction for detection and recovery.
∎

B.3
Reduction Decreasing Order
k
to
k/a

We prove a reduction that maps
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
to
(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}
for some
a|k
in the case
\mathsf{FULL}\in\star
. The reduction uses a change of variables, modifying the signal
x
to
x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{a}}\right]
; the assumption
\mathsf{FULL}\in\star
is crucial for a correct distributional mapping.

Lemma
5.4
.

(Reducing Order
k
to
k/a
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1,2,4) in Fig.
7
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters.
For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},a|k
, there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery (
\mathsf{ReduceK}
, Alg.
23
)

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{a}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
23
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right]
.

The reduction in Lemma
5.4
, in combination with reductions in Prop.
5.3
that decompose an instance of
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}
into standard (distinct index) instances of orders
k,k-2,k-4,\dots
, yields the following order-reducing reductions. For convenience, we also refer to the reduction in Cor.
5.3
as
\mathsf{ReduceK}
(Alg.
23
).

Corollary
5.3
.

(of Lemma
5.4
)

Let
\star\subseteq\left\{\mathsf{Pois},\mathsf{wor}\right\}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}\in\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\right\}
be some fixed model choices (1,2,4) in Fig.
7
and let
k,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t\in[0,1],\eta\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters.
For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},a|k
and
k^{\prime}\in\left\{k/a,k/a-2,k/a-4,\dots\right\}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, there is an average-case reduction for both detection and recovery

\text{from}\quad k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{\prime})\,,\quad n^{\prime}=n^{a}\,.

Moreover, Alg.
23
maps an instance with a secret signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to one with a signal vector
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes a}\odot y
for a known
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right]
.

Input:

\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}:\,a|k

Output:

\mathcal{Z}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
.

1

y\leftarrow\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{a}}\right]

2

//
Enumerate elements of
\left[n\right]^{a}

Let
N:\left[n\right]^{a}\to\left[n^{a}\right]
be any bijection

3

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\emptyset

4

forall

j\in\left[m\right]

do

5

\overline{\alpha}_{j}\leftarrow\text{ random ordering of }\alpha_{j}

6

//
Group indexes of
\overline{\alpha}_{j}
into
k/a
groups of size
a

\alpha_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\left\{N\left(\left(\overline{\alpha}_{j}\right)_{ai+1},\dots,\left(\overline{\alpha}_{j}\right)_{ai+a}\right)\right\}_{i=0}^{k/a-1}

//
Here
\left\{\cdot\right\}
denotes multiset

7

\mathsf{Z}_{j}\leftarrow\mathsf{Y}_{j}\cdot\prod_{i\in\alpha_{j}^{\prime}}y_{i}

8

\mathcal{Z}\leftarrow\mathcal{Z}\cup\{(\alpha_{j}^{\prime},\mathsf{Z}_{j})\}

9

Return
\mathcal{Z}

10

Algorithm 23

\mathsf{ReduceK}\left(\mathcal{Y},a\right)

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

The reduction is
\mathsf{ReduceK}
(Alg.
23
); we show the planted case and the null case proof is the same.

Let
\mathcal{Y}=\left\{\big(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\big)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta}(n)
, where
m=n^{k(1+t)/2}
, and
\mathcal{Z}=\mathsf{ReduceK}\left(\mathcal{Y},a\right)
be the output of Algorithm
23
. For every equation
\left(\alpha,\mathsf{Y}\right)\in\mathcal{Y}
, the reduction creates one entry in
\mathcal{Z}
as follows. Given an index multiset
\alpha=\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right\}
, the coefficients are first randomly ordered into a tuple
\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right)
and then grouped into new indices
(i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(i_{a+1},\dots,i_{2a}),\dots
. Given that
\alpha\sim\mu\left(\left\{n\right\}^{k}\right)
(Def.
A.1
), the tuple
\left(i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\right)
is uniformly random on
\left[n\right]^{k}
, so the grouped tuple
\left((i_{1},\dots,i_{a}),(i_{a+1},\dots,i_{2a}),\dots\right)
is uniformly random on
\left[\left[n\right]^{a}\right]^{k/a}
. Therefore, the index multisets of
\mathcal{Z}
are drawn independently from
\mu\left(\left\{\left[n\right]^{a}\right\}^{k/a}\right)
.

Moreover, for an input equation
\left(\alpha,\mathsf{Y}\right)
and the new index multiset
\alpha^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{Z}
, if
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
, the corresponding

\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{Y}\cdot\prod_{i\in\alpha^{\prime}}y_{i}\sim\prod_{i\in\alpha^{\prime}}\underbrace{\left(x^{\otimes a}\odot y\right)_{i}}_{x^{\prime}_{i}}\cdot\mathsf{Rad}\left(\delta\right)\,,

where
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
is the noise parameter. If
k\mathsf{\text{-}model}=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
,
\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{Y}\cdot\prod_{i\in\alpha^{\prime}}y_{i}\sim\delta\prod_{i\in\alpha^{\prime}}{x^{\prime}_{i}}+\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Therefore,

\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{Z}\right)=(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}(n^{a},m,\delta)\,,

where

m=n^{k(1+t)/2}=\left(n^{a}\right)^{k/a\cdot(1+t)/2}\quad\text{and}\quad\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}=\left(n^{a}\right)^{-k/a\cdot t/4-\eta/a/2}\,.

This concludes the proof of average-case reduction to
(k/a)\mathsf{\text{-}model}^{\star,\mathsf{FULL}}_{t,\eta/a}(n^{a})
.
∎

Appendix C
Definitions of Average-Case Reductions

In this section we formally define average-case reductions. The definition varies depending on the task, so we separately define average-case reductions for detection and for recovery. Note that all the reductions we prove in this paper are simultaneously reductions for both tasks.

For a more general overview of average-case reductions (both detection and recovery) and parametrization choices for statistical models see, e.g.
[
19
,
20
]
. Here we present formal definitions specific to the tensor problems that are the focus of this paper:
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
.

C.1
Planted
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
Models

Recall that for every set of parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
, a dimensionality parameter
n
, and a signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, the (planted) distributions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
are defined as follows:

Definition
1.1
.

(Planted Distributions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)

Fix
k,t,\eta
and let
m=n^{k(t+1)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
and
x\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\right)
.

1.

For
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
we observe
m
pairs
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
are independent and for each
j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=x_{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
)

where
\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
.

2.

For
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
we observe
m
pairs
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
are independent and for each
j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta\cdot x_{\alpha}+\mathsf{G}_{j}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)

where
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

For convenience denote
\Omega_{n}^{k}
to be the space of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n),k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}(n)
instances.

For every parameter tuple
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
,
\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n}
and
\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n}
form a
sequence of planted distributions
, i.e. distributions that depend on a signal vector
x
. Both models describe
families
of such sequences, as parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
vary. Each fixed point of
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
corresponds to a specific planted problem, whose complexity might differ from the one of a different planted problem with parameters
\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
.

C.2
Statistical Tasks: Detection and Recovery

Detection Task.

Fix parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
. The
detection task
is to decide whether a given sample instance
\mathcal{Y}
was drawn from the planted distribution
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
) or a natural
null (pure noise) distribution
that contains no structured signal
x^{\otimes k}
.

We define the null distributions for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
by replacing the structured signal
x_{\alpha_{j}}
in equation
j
with a random sign
w_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\big(\left\{\pm 1\right\}\big)
. A common alternative is to set the SNR parameter
\delta
to be
0
in Eq. (

⁢
k
-
XOR

), (

⁢
k
-
Gauss

). For all discrete models
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
, this coincides exactly with our null; for canonical Tensor PCA (
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}(n)
) the two versions are statistically indistinguishable in the conjectured hard regime
\eta>0
(i.e., their total variation distance is
o(1)
, see Prop.
C.1
). For
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
with all other densities
t\in[0,1)
, the two choices of null distributions are distinct (i.e., their total variation distance is
\Omega(1)
).

The formal null-model Definition
C.1
is the same as the planted Definition
1.1
above with the structured signal
x_{\alpha_{j}}
in equation
j
replaced by a random sign
w\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}
:

Definition C.1

(Null Models for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)
.

Fix
k,t,\eta
and let
m=n^{k(t+1)/2},\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2}
.

1.

For
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
we observe
m
pairs
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
are independent and for each
j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=w_{j}\cdot\mathsf{W}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathsf{Rad}(0)\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
)

where
w_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right]
and
\mathsf{W}_{j}\sim\mathsf{Rad}(\delta)
.

2.

For
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
we observe
m
pairs
\left\{\left(\alpha_{j},\mathsf{Y}_{j}\right)\right\}_{j\in\left[m\right]}
, where
\alpha\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\binom{\left[n\right]}{k}\right]
are independent and for each
j\in\left[m\right]
,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\delta\cdot w_{j}+\mathsf{G}_{j}\,,

(
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
)

where
w_{j}\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left[\left\{\pm 1\right\}\right]
and
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

As stated before, for Tensor PCA, the above null is close in total variation to the standard “
\delta=0
” null, hence the corresponding detection tasks are equivalent.

Proposition C.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Y}\sim k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t=1,\eta}^{\mathsf{NULL}}(n)
with
\eta>0
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}
be such that for each
j\in\left[m\right]
in the Def.
C.1
above,

\mathsf{Y}_{j}=\mathsf{G}_{j}\,,

where
\mathsf{G}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are i.i.d.. Then for any
\eta>0
,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y}),\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}})\right)\to 0\quad\text{as }n\to\infty\,.

Proof.

By Pinsker’s inequality and the tensorization of
\mathsf{KL}
divergence,

\displaystyle 2d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y}),\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}})\right)^{2}\leq\mathsf{KL}\left(\mathcal{Y}\|\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}\right)=n^{k}\cdot\mathsf{KL}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)\|\mathcal{N}(\delta w_{j},1)\right)\,,

where
w_{j}\sim\mathsf{Rad}\left(0\right)
and
\delta=n^{-k/4-\eta/2}\ll 1
. By definition of
\mathsf{KL}
,

\mathsf{KL}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(0,1\right)\|\mathcal{N}(\delta w_{j},1)\right)=\delta^{2}/2-\mathbb{E}_{X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}\log\cosh\left(\delta X\right)=\Theta\left(\delta^{4}\right)\,,

so plugging in
\delta\ll n^{-k/4}
, we obtain
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{Y}),\mathcal{L}(\tilde{\mathcal{Y}})\big)\ll 1
.
∎

Fix
(k,t,\eta)
. For a fixed signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, denote the (planted) distribution of Eq. (

⁢
k
-
XOR

)
P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(resp.
P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
) and let

\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}

be a collection of all planted distributions. Denote the null distribution
P^{\mathsf{NULL}}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t}^{\mathsf{NULL}}(n)
(resp.
P^{\mathsf{NULL}}=k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}^{\mathsf{NULL}}(n)
). Then the detection task for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(corresp.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
) is defines as follows.

Definition C.2

(Solving the Detection Task)
.

Given a sample
\mathcal{Y}
from an unknown distribution
P\in P^{\mathsf{NULL}}\cup\mathcal{P}
, the detection problem is the task of distinguishing between events

H_{0}=\left\{P=P^{\mathsf{NULL}}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{1}=\left\{P\in\mathcal{P}\right\}\,.

In particular, an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\{0,1\}
solves the detection task with error
\delta_{n}
if the Type
I+II
error does not exceed
\delta_{n}
, i.e.,

\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P^{\mathsf{NULL}}}\big[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})=1\big]+\sup_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}}\big[\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})=0\big]\leq\delta_{n}\,.

Moreover, we say that an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves the corresponding detection task with Type
I+II
asymptotic error
\delta
if in the definition above we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\delta_{n}\leq\delta\,.

Recovery (Search) Task.

Fix
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
. Similarly denote the planted distribution with signal vector
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
as
P_{x}
(distribution of Eq. (

⁢
k
-
XOR

) or Eq. (

⁢
k
-
Gauss

) with parameters
k,t,\eta,n
). Let
\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}
.

The recovery task is given a sample from
P_{x}\in\mathcal{P}
to estimate the signal vector
x
(up to a global sign)
47
47

47

The global sign assumption is necessary for even tensor orders
k
. Since some of our reductions change the parity of the tensor order, we define the recovery up to global sign for all tensor orders. See Sec.
C.4
for further discussion.
under some loss function
\ell_{n}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\to[0,+\infty)
. Formally,

Definition C.3

(Recovery Task)
.

Given a sample
\mathcal{Y}\in\Omega_{n}^{k}
from an unknown distribution
P_{x}\in\mathcal{P}
, the recovery task with a loss function
\ell_{n}
and a loss threshold
\ell_{n}^{\star}
is to produce an estimate
\widehat{x}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, such that the expected loss
\ell_{n}
is minimized. In particular, an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
solves the recovery task (with expected loss at most
\delta_{n}
) if

\sup_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}}\min\big\{\ell_{n}\left(x,\widehat{x}=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})\right),\ell_{n}\left(-x,\widehat{x}=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})\right)\big\}\leq\delta_{n}\leq\ell_{n}^{\star}\,,

for some
\delta_{n}\geq 0
and a predetermined threshold
\ell_{n}^{\star}
.

Moreover, we say that an algorithm
\mathcal{A}
solves the recovery problem for
P
(with asymptotic loss
\delta
) if in the definition above we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\delta_{n}\leq\delta\leq\ell^{\star}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\ell_{n}^{\star}\,.

See Sec.
C.4
for an overview of common recovery problems for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and Tensor PCA.

C.3
Definitions of Average-Case Reductions

C.3.1
Average-Case Reductions for Detection

First, we define an average-case reduction for a fixed dimensionality parameter
n
that maps between parameter tuples:

\left(k,t,\eta\right)\quad\to\quad\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)\,.

Fix the dimensionality parameters
n
and
n^{\prime}=n^{c}
(for some constant
c>0
). Consider the detection problems associated with
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
and associated null and planted distributions:

\displaystyle P^{\mathsf{NULL}}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t}^{\mathsf{NULL}}(n)\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\text{ with a secret }x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\,,\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\,,

\displaystyle Q^{\mathsf{NULL}}=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime}}^{\mathsf{NULL}}(n^{\prime})\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad Q_{x}=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\text{ with a secret }x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\,,\mathcal{Q}=\left\{Q_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\,.

The average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
(“from
P
to
Q
”) is defined in the following Def.
C.4
. Note that the average-case reductions
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
are defined fully analogously.

Definition C.4

(Average-Case Reduction for Detection from
P
to
Q
)
.

We say that a
O(n^{r})
-time (possibly randomized) algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\text{red}}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\Omega_{n^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}
is an
average-case reduction for detection with TV-error
t_{n}
from
P
to
Q
if for all
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, there exists some
x^{\prime}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
satisfying:

\displaystyle\text{if }\mathcal{Y}\sim P^{\mathsf{NULL}},\text{ then }

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\text{red}}(\mathcal{Y}),Q^{\mathsf{NULL}}\right)\leq t_{n}\,,

\displaystyle\text{if }\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x},\text{ then }

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\text{red}}(\mathcal{Y}),Q_{x^{\prime}}\right)\leq t_{n}\,,

where
n^{\prime}=n^{c}
(for some known constant
c>0
).
48
48

48

We allow the dimension to change
polynomially
in the reduction, since our focus is on poly-time algorithms.

The following lemma states the complexity implications of such a reduction. The proof uses the properties of total variation distance and can be found, e.g. in
[
20
]
.

Lemma C.1

(Implication of Reduction for Detection from
P
to
Q
)
.

Assume there exists an
O(n^{r})
-time average-case reduction for detection from
P
to
Q
with parameter
t_{n}
(Def.
C.4
). If there is an
O(n^{t})
-time algorithm for detection for
Q
achieving Type
I+II
error at most
\delta_{n}
, there exists a
O(n^{ct}+n^{r})
-time algorithm for detection for
P
achieving Type
I+II
error at most
2t_{n}+\delta_{n^{c}}
.

C.3.2
Average-Case Reductions for Recovery

Again, we first define an average-case reduction for recovery for a fixed dimensionality parameter
n
that maps between parameter tuples:

\left(k,t,\eta\right)\quad\to\quad\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)\,.

Fix the dimensionality parameters
n
and
n^{\prime}=n^{c}
(for some constant
c>0
). Consider the recovery problems associated with
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
and
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
and denote

\displaystyle P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\text{ with a secret }x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\,,\mathcal{P}=\left\{P_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\,,

\displaystyle Q_{x}=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\text{ with a secret }x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\,,\mathcal{Q}=\left\{Q_{x}\right\}_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\,.

Let
\ell_{n}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\to[0,+\infty)
and
\ell^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\to[0,+\infty)
be the two associated loss functions and
\ell_{n}^{\star},\left(\ell^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\right)^{\star}\geq 0
the associated thresholds.

The average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(
P
) to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
(
Q
) is defined in the following Def.
C.5
. Note that the average-case reductions
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}\to k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
are defined fully analogously.

Definition C.5

(Average-Case Reduction for Recovery from
P
to
Q
)
.

We say that a
O(n^{r})
-time (possibly randomized) algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\text{red}}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\Omega_{n^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}
is an
average-case reduction for recovery with TV-error
t_{n}
from
P
to
Q
if:

1.

(Reduction Algorithm) For all
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, there exists some
x^{\prime}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
satisfying:

\displaystyle\text{if }\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x},\text{ then }

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\text{red}}(\mathcal{Y}),Q_{x^{\prime}}\right)\leq t_{n}\,.

2.

(Recovery Algorithm) There exists an
O(n^{r})
-time algorithm
\mathcal{A}^{\text{rec}}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
such that if
\mathcal{Y},x,x^{\prime}=x^{\prime}(\mathcal{Y})
are defined as above and
\ell^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}})\leq\delta_{n^{\prime}}\leq\left(\ell^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\right)^{\star}
, then

\sup_{x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}}\ell_{n}\left(x,\mathcal{A}^{\text{rec}}\left(\mathcal{Y},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right)\right)\leq\eta_{n}\leq\ell_{n}^{\star}\,,

where
\eta_{n}=f(\delta_{n^{\prime}})
and
\left(\ell^{\prime}_{n^{\prime}}\right)^{\star}
and
\ell_{n}^{\star}
are the loss thresholds for solving the corresponding recovery problems.

The following lemma states the complexity implications of such a reduction.

Lemma C.2

(Implication of Reduction for Recovery from
P
to
Q
)
.

Let
P,Q
be two recovery problems defined as above and assume there exists an
O(n^{r})
-time average-case reduction for recovery with TV-error
t_{n}
(Def.
C.5
). Then, if there is an
O(n^{t})
-time algorithm for recovery for
Q
, that with probability at least
1-\psi_{n}
solves the recovery problem for
Q
, there exists an
O\left(n^{ct}+n^{r}\right)
-time algorithm for recovery for
P
that with probability at least
1-t_{n}-\psi_{n^{c}}
achieves loss at most
\eta_{n}=f(\delta_{n^{c}})\leq\ell_{n}^{\star}
for the loss function
\ell_{n}
.

We emphasize that while the definition above is quite general, any average-case reduction that nontrivially modifies the signal vector, i.e.
x^{\prime}\neq x
requires additional assumptions on the loss function
\ell_{n}
and, potentially, the admissible recovery algorithm itself. For more details on such assumptions for our reductions, see Sec.
C.4
.

C.3.3
Average-Case Reductions as Parameter Maps

Average-case reductions for sequences of problems.

Notably, while Def.
C.4
,
C.5
of average-case reductions are specific to a particular value of the dimensionality parameter
n
, we study the complexity of the
sequences of the tasks

\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n},\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n}
. To address this, for fixed parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
and
\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
, we define an average-case reduction

\text{from}\quad\left\{P_{n}\right\}=\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n}\quad\text{to}\quad\left\{Q_{n^{\prime}}\right\}=\left\{k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\right\}_{n^{\prime}}\,.

The definition for any combination of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
is analogous.

Definition C.6

(Average-Case Reduction from
\left\{P_{n}\right\}
to
\left\{Q_{n^{\prime}}\right\}
)
.

We say that an
O(n^{r})
-time algorithm
\mathcal{A}=\{\mathcal{A}_{n}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\Omega_{n^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}\}
is an
average-case reduction for detection (resp. recovery)

\text{from}\quad\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n}\quad\text{to}\quad\left\{k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\right\}_{n^{\prime}}

if
for any sequence
\{\left(t_{i},\eta_{i}\right)\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
, such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\left(t_{i},\eta_{i}\right)=\left(t,\eta\right)
, there exist sequences
\{\left(t_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime}\right),n_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
49
49

49

Note that the definition above does not require the algorithm exist strictly for all
n
, only for a growing sequence of dimensions
n
. This is used to avoid unnecessary technical details while allowing assumptions like “
n
is even”, which do not affect the complexity phenomena we study. We emphasize that all our reductions can formally be adjusted to work for all values of
n
, e.g. by simply ignoring one of the coordinates to make
n
even.
and an index
i_{0}
, such that

1.

\lim_{i\to\infty}\left(t_{i}^{\prime},\eta_{i}^{\prime}\right)=\left(t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
and
\lim_{i\to\infty}n_{i}=\infty
;

2.

\forall\ i>i_{0}
,
\mathcal{A}_{n_{i}}
is an average-case reduction for detection (resp. recovery) from
k_{i}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t_{i},\eta_{i}}(n_{i})
to
k^{\prime}_{i}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime}_{i},\eta^{\prime}_{i}}(n^{\prime}_{i})
with the TV-error
t_{n_{i}}
, where
n^{\prime}_{i}=n_{i}^{c}
for some constant
c
;

3.

\lim_{i\to\infty}t_{n_{i}}=0
.

This definition inherits the average-case reduction implications from Lemmas
C.1
and
C.2
(for proof see analogous Lemmas A.2, B.2 of
[
20
]
). Essentially, it states that an average-case reduction from
\left\{P_{n}\right\}
to
\left\{Q_{n^{\prime}}\right\}
transfers the (detection and recovery) algorithms from
Q
to
P
if they are guaranteed to exist in a small
L_{1}
parameter neighborhood of
t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}
.

Lemma C.3

(Implication of Reduction for Sequences)
.

Consider the detection (resp. recovery) problems associated with
\left\{P_{n}\right\}=\left\{k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\right\}_{n},\left\{Q_{n^{\prime}}\right\}=\left\{k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})\right\}_{n^{\prime}}
and assume there exists an
O(n^{r})
-time average-case reduction for detection (resp. recovery) for problem sequences (Def.
C.6
) from
\left\{P\right\}_{n}
to
\left\{Q\right\}_{n}
.

Fix
\xi>0
and assume there is a
O(n^{t})
-time algorithm for detection (resp. recovery) for
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}}(n)
for all
\left(\tilde{t},\tilde{\eta}\right)\in B_{\xi}\left(t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
50
50

50

Here
B_{\xi}\left(t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
denotes an
L_{1}
-ball around a parameter tuple
\left(t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
of radius
\xi
.
achieving asymptotic Type
I+II
error
\delta
(resp. asymptotic loss
\leq\left(\ell^{\prime}\right)^{\star}
). Then there exists a
O(n^{ct}+n^{r})
-time algorithm for detection (resp. recovery) for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
achieving asymptotic Type
I+II
error at most
\delta
(resp. asymptotic loss
\leq\ell^{\star}
per Def.
C.5
).

Average-case reduction as a parameter map.

At the center of this paper is the following complexity question:

Q: for fixed parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
and growing dimension
n
, what is the computational complexity of the detection and recovery tasks for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
(resp.
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}_{t,\eta}(n)
)?

To address this question, we establish average-case reductions between parameter tuples
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
and study the complexity implications of such reductions. Recall that believed hardness of
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR},k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
can be summarized in the following conjectures:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right):\text{ There is no poly-time algorithm solving {detection} for }k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\text{ with any }\eta>0.

\displaystyle\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right):\text{ There is no poly-time algorithm solving {recovery} for }k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\text{ with any }\eta>0.

The following hardness implication for these conjectures is a direct corollary of Lemma
C.3
.

Proposition C.2
.

Fix parameters
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+},t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. If for every
\eta^{\prime}>0
there exists
\eta>0
such that there is an average-case reduction for detection (resp. recovery) (Def.
C.6
) from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
for
n^{\prime}=n^{c}
(for some constant
c>0
), then

\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{D}}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)\quad\left(\text{resp. }\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k,t\right)\Rightarrow\mathsf{CONJ}^{\mathsf{R}}\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime}\right)\right)\,.

Note that while the definitions of average-case reductions admit these strong complexity implications, they allow certain flexibility in building the actual reduction algorithms in any given dimension. For example, the following proposition will be part of almost every average-case reduction we present in this paper. Intuitively, it states that the algorithms between instances are considered average-case reductions as long as they map parameters up to
\mathrm{poly}\log(n)
factors. The proof follows directly from Def.
C.6
.

Proposition C.3
.

Fix parameters
\left(k,t,\eta\right)
and
\left(k^{\prime},t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}\right)
and denote
\delta=n^{-kt/4-\eta/2},m=n^{k(t+1)/2}
and
\delta^{\prime}=(n^{\prime})^{-k^{\prime}t^{\prime}/4-\eta^{\prime}/2},m^{\prime}=(n^{\prime})^{k^{\prime}(t^{\prime}+1)/2}
, where
n^{\prime}=n^{c}
(for some constant
c
). Assume one builds a sequence of algorithms
\left\{\mathcal{A}^{\textnormal{red}}_{n}\right\}_{n}
that for a sequence of dimensionality parameters
\left\{n_{i}\right\}_{i},\lim_{i\to\infty}n_{i}=\infty
are average-case reductions (Def.
C.4
,
C.5
) from

k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n,m,\delta)\quad\to\quad k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}(n^{\prime},\hat{m^{\prime}},\hat{\delta^{\prime}})\,,

where

\hat{m^{\prime}}=O\left(\mathrm{poly}\log n\right)\cdot m^{\prime}\quad\text{and}\quad\hat{\delta^{\prime}}=O\left(\mathrm{poly}\log n\right)\cdot\delta^{\prime}\,.

Then,
\left\{\mathcal{A}^{\textnormal{red}}_{n}\right\}_{n}
constitute an average-case reduction from
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)
to
k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})
(Def.
C.6
).

C.4
Assumptions on the Recovery Task

An average-case reduction for recovery from

P=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t,\eta}(n)\quad\text{to}\quad Q=k^{\prime}\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}_{t^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}}(n^{\prime})

maps an instance of
P
with signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
to an instance of
Q
with signal
x^{\prime}=F(x)\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
.

Some of our reductions (e.g., entry creation/erasure, Lem.
5.3
; cloning/splitting procedures, Lem.
5.1
,
5.2
; and Gaussianization/Discretization, Lem.
A.1
,
A.3
) preserve the signal exactly:
x^{\prime}=F(x)=x
. In this case, recovery transfers for
any
loss function
\ell_{n}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
: if there exists a poly-time recovery algorithm for
Q
that succeeds with probability
1-\psi_{n}
at threshold
\ell_{n}^{\star}
, there exists a poly-time recovery algorithm for
P
that succeeds with probability
1-\psi_{n}-t_{n}
for the same
(\ell_{n},\ell_{n}^{\star})
, where
t_{n}\to_{n\to\infty}0
is the TV-error of the average-case reduction (see Def.
C.5
).

Our more technical results modify the signal in a controlled way:

(1)

Tensored signal vector:
In the “Decreasing
k\to k/c
” reduction (Lem.
5.4
) and its uses (Thm.
10.1
, Thm.
12.1
), for some integer
c
we have
n^{\prime}=\binom{n}{c}
and

x^{\prime}=F(x)=x^{\otimes c}\odot y\,,

where
y\sim\mathsf{Unif}\left(\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}\right)
is known and independent from
x
.

(2)

Subsampled signal vector:
In the discrete and Gaussian equation combination reductions(Lem.
7.1
and Lem
9.1
),
n^{\prime}=n\left(1-o(1)\right)
and

x^{\prime}=F(x)=x_{[1:n^{\prime}]}\,,

i.e.
x^{\prime}
is (any) subset of
n^{\prime}
coordinates of
x
.

In the cases where
x^{\prime}\neq x
, we cannot claim recovery transfer for an arbitrary loss metric
\ell
. However, we show that our reductions do transfer the standard
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
and
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
recovery notions:
weak, strong,
and
exact recovery
.

Standard Recovery Tasks for
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
,
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
.

Recovery task (see Sec.
C.2
) is defined
up to a global sign
: algorithms produce
\hat{x}
such that
\min\big\{\ell(x,\hat{x}),\ell(-x,\hat{x})\big\}
is small. This requirement is necessary for the even-order tensors, and as our reductions often change the order parity, we impose it for all
k
.

In
exact recovery
, the algorithm is tasked with recovering the signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
exactly with sufficiently high probability.

Definition C.7

(Exact Recovery)
.

In
exact recovery
, the loss function
\ell_{n}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\to\left\{0,1\right\}
is

\ell_{n}\left(x,\hat{x}\right)=\mathsf{1}_{x\neq\hat{x}}\,,

and an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
solves exact recovery if, given as an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}
(for
P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
) with signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, it outputs an estimate
\hat{x}=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})
satisfying

\Pr_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}}\left[\min\big\{\ell_{n}(x,\hat{x}),\ell_{n}(-x,\hat{x})\big\}=0\right]\geq 1-\psi^{\star}_{n}\,.

In
weak
and
strong recovery
, the algorithm is tasked with finding an estimate
\hat{x}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
that has large coordinate-wise overlap with
x
. Formally:

Definition C.8

(Weak and Strong Recovery)
.

In both
weak
and
strong recovery
, the loss function
\ell_{n}:\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\times\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}\to\left[0,1\right]
is

\ell_{n}\left(x,\widehat{x}\right)=1-\frac{1}{n}\big|\langle x,\hat{x}\rangle\big|\,,

and an algorithm
\mathcal{A}:\Omega_{n}^{k}\to\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
solves recovery if, given as an input
\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}
for
P_{x}=k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
(or
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
) with signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
, it outputs an estimate
\hat{x}=\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{Y})
and

\Pr_{\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}}\left[\min\big\{\ell_{n}(x,\hat{x}),\ell_{n}(-x,\hat{x})\big\}\leq\ell_{n}^{\star}\right]\geq 1-\psi_{n}^{\star}\,,

for some probability threshold
1-\psi_{n}^{\star}
.

In
weak recovery
,
\ell_{n}^{\star}=1-\Omega(1)
(recover a constant fraction
>1/2
of coordinates).

In
strong recovery
,
\ell_{n}^{\star}=o_{n}(1)
(recover
1-o(1)
fraction of coordinates correctly).

We now argue that the two signal modifications above still allow transfer of weak, strong, and exact recovery.

Tensored signal vector.

We prove that given a recovery algorithm for
Q
that returns an estimate
\widehat{x^{\prime}}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n^{\prime}}
, (where
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes c}\odot y
), one can recover the original vector
x
.

Exact recovery:
if
\widehat{x^{\prime}}=x^{\prime}
, then
\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y=x^{\otimes c}
, from which
x
is trivially recovered (up to a sign flip).

Weak/strong recovery:
we have (when recovery Alg. succeeds)

\min\big\{\ell_{n}\left(x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right),\ell_{n}\left(-x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right)\big\}=\min\big\{\ell_{n}\left(x^{\otimes c},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y\right),\ell_{n}\left(-x^{\otimes c},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y\right)\big\}\leq\ell_{n}^{\star}\,.

(46)

Denote
\ell_{n}\left(x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right)=\gamma
, then the set of mismatshed coordinates in
x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}
has size
n^{\prime}\gamma/2
. Because
y
is uniform and independent of
x
, the signal
x^{\prime}=x^{\otimes c}\odot y
in
Q
is uniform and independent of
x
and the source instance. If we additionally apply a uniformly random coordinate permutation before using the
Q
-recovery algorithm, conditioning on the loss
\gamma
, the
n^{\prime}\gamma/2
error locations in the estimate
\widehat{x^{\prime}}
are chosen at random from
\binom{\left[n^{\prime}\right]}{n^{\prime}\gamma/2}
and are independent of
x
and the original tensor.

In the case of odd
c
, if
\ell_{n}\left(x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right)<\ell_{n}\left(-x^{\prime},\widehat{x^{\prime}}\right)
, then
\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y
is a noisy observation of
x^{\otimes c}
, and otherwise it is a noisy observation of
(-x)^{\otimes c}
. In both cases, recovering
x
up to a global sign from
\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y
is a noisy
k\mathsf{\text{-}XOR}
-type (
k\mathsf{\text{-}Gauss}
-type) problem with
\binom{n}{c}
observations and at least constant entrywise noise – solvable efficiently with existing algorithms (Sec.
4.2
).

In the case of an even
c
, we can efficiently and with high probability distinguish between the two cases. We can then similarly use the noisy observation of
x^{\otimes c}
(either
\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y
or
-\widehat{x^{\prime}}\odot y
) to recover the original
x
. The distinguishing is possible via a following efficiently-computable test. Given a tensor
\mathsf{Y}
that is either a noisy observation of
x^{\widetilde{\otimes}c}
or
-x^{\widetilde{\otimes}c}
, split all
n
coordinates into
\Theta(n)
sets of size
3c/2
. For every such set
i_{1},\dots,i_{3c/2}
, compute
\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{c}}\cdot\mathsf{Y}_{i_{c/2},\dots,\mathsf{Y}_{i_{3c/2}}}\cdot\mathsf{Y}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{c/2},i_{c},\dots,i_{3c/2}}
. For the case of
x^{\otimes c}
, the the number of these products equal to
1
is concentrated around
n\left(1/2+\Omega(1)\right)
and otherwise around
n\left(1/2-\Omega(1)\right)
. These cases are distinguishable with high probability. Thus, both weak and strong recovery are transferred.

Subsampled signal vector.

In this case, the recovery algorithm for
Q
returns
\widehat{x^{\prime}}
for
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
.

Weak/strong recovery:
the estimate
\widehat{x^{\prime}}
is already a sufficiently good estimate for the original signal vector
x
:

\ell\big(x,\big(\widehat{x^{\prime}},\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{n-n^{\prime}=o(1)}\big)\big)\leq\ell^{\star}+o(1)\leq\begin{cases}1-\Omega(1)\,,&\text{for weak recovery}\,,\\
o(1)\,,&\text{for strong recovery}\,.\end{cases}\,.

Exact recovery:
exact recovery of a coordinate subset
x^{\prime}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}
does not determine all
n
coordinates, but applying the reduction twice with overlapping coordinate set does (with the success probability squared).

Formally, let
\mathcal{Y}\sim P_{x}
be the input instance with signal
x\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}
and let

\mathcal{Y}^{(1)},\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}=\mathsf{kSplit}\left(\mathcal{Y}\right)

be the two independent instances sampled from
P_{x}
(see Lem.
5.2
). Moreover, let

\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(1)}=\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\odot\left(y^{(1)}\right)^{\otimes k}\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{(2)}=\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\odot\left(y^{(2)}\right)^{\otimes k}\,,

where
y^{(1)},y^{(2)}\sim\mathsf{Unif}{\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{n}}
. After such transformation,
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
is an instance of
P_{z^{(1)}=x\odot y^{(1)}}
and
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
is an independent instance of
P_{z^{(2)}=x\odot y^{(2)}}
.

Apply the average-case reduction
\mathcal{A}
independently:

\mathcal{A}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\right)
has the signal vector
w^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\right)
has the signal vector
w^{(2)}
for
51
51

51

The guarantees for reductions that have this signal modification (Alg.
1
and
5
) state that any
n^{\prime}
-subset of indices chosen for the output. This can also be achieved by simply permuting the coordinates of
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}
.

w^{(1)}=z_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}=x_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}\odot y_{\left[1:n^{\prime}\right]}^{(1)}\quad\text{and}\quad w^{(2)}=z_{\left[n^{\prime}-n,n\right]}=x_{\left[n^{\prime}-n,n\right]}\odot y_{\left[n^{\prime}-n,n\right]}^{(2)}\,.

If the exact recovery algorithm for
Q
succeeds with probability
1-\psi_{n^{\prime}}
per instance, then, since
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(1)}\right)
and
\mathcal{A}\left(\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}\right)
are fully independent, we recover both
w^{(1)}
and
w^{(2)}
with probability at least
(1-\psi_{n^{\prime}})^{2}
. We thus recover
x
exactly with probability at least
(1-\psi_{n^{\prime}})^{2}
.

Appendix D
Deferred Proofs

D.1
Proof of Claim
10.1

Claim
10.1
.

Fix
c,r\in(0,1),k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There exists a constant
A(c,r,k^{\prime})
, such that for every
A\geq A(c,r,k^{\prime})
, there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that
k\leq cA
,
\lfloor kr\rceil
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
, and

\frac{k}{\|kr\|}>A\,,

(47)

where
\|x\|=\big|x-\lfloor x\rceil\big|
.

Fact D.1

(Integer Approximation Facts
[
57
]
)
.

Given
r\in(0,1)
, we can express
r
as a simple continued fraction

r=0+\frac{1}{b_{1}+\frac{1}{b_{2}+\dots}}\,,

(48)

where
b_{i}>0
are integers. We denote expression in (
48
) as
[b_{0}=0;b_{1},b_{2},\dots]
. The
i
-th convergent
of
r
is then defined as
Q_{i}=[0;b_{1},\dots,b_{i}]=[0;b_{1},\dots,b_{i},0,0,\dots]
. The following holds:

•

(Theorem 1 in
[
57
]
) The
i
-th convergent can be expressed as
Q_{i}=A_{i}/B_{i}
, where
\mathsf{gcd}(A_{i},B_{i})=1
, and the sequences
\left\{A_{i}\right\},\left\{B_{i}\right\}
satisfy the recursive relation

X_{i}=b_{i}X_{i-1}+X_{i-2}

for any
i\geq 2
. As a consequence,
\left\{B_{i}\right\}_{i=2}^{\infty}
is a growing sequence of integers.

•

(Theorem 9 in
[
57
]
) In the above setting,

|r-Q_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{B_{i}B_{i+1}}\,,

or, equivalently,

|rB_{i}-A_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{B_{i+1}}\,.

Proof of Claim
10.1
.

Express
r
as a simple continued fraction
r=[b_{0}=0;b_{1},b_{2},\dots]
, where
b_{i}>0
are integers and consider the convergents
\left\{Q_{i}=\frac{A_{i}}{B_{i}}\right\}
for
r
defined as in Fact
D.1
. Then, from Fact
D.1
,
|rB_{i}-A_{i}|\leq\frac{1}{B_{i+1}}
and, as a consequence, for every integer
t>0
,

|r\cdot B_{i}t-A_{i}t|\leq\frac{t}{B_{i+1}}\,.

(49)

Recall that
\left\{B_{i}\right\}
is a growing sequence of integers and define
\tilde{c}=c/(2k^{\prime}+1)
and
i_{c}=\min\left\{i:B_{i}\geq 1/\tilde{c}\right\}
. Let
A(c,r,k^{\prime})=B_{i_{c}}/\tilde{c}
and choose any
A>A(c,r,k^{\prime})
. Let index
i
be such that
B_{i}\leq\tilde{c}A<B_{i+1}
. Then,
B_{i}\geq B_{i_{c}}\geq 1/\tilde{c}
. From (
49
) we then obtain

\frac{B_{i}q}{\|B_{i}qr\|}\geq B_{i}B_{i+1}\geq 1/\tilde{c}\cdot\tilde{c}A=A\,.

Thus, for any
A>A(c,r,k^{\prime})
and
q\leq(2k^{\prime}+1)
, the condition (
47
) of the Claim holds with
k=B_{i}q\leq\tilde{c}(2k^{\prime}+1)A=cA
for
q\leq 2k^{\prime}+1
.
It is now sufficient to choose
q
such that
\lfloor B_{i}qr\rceil
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
. Let
\alpha=\frac{B_{i}r}{k^{\prime}}
and let
q\leq 2k^{\prime}+1
be such that

\|q\alpha\|\leq\frac{1}{2k^{\prime}+1}\,,

where the existence of such
q
is guaranteed by the pigeonhole principle. Then,

|qB_{i}r-k^{\prime}\lfloor q\alpha\rceil|\leq\frac{k^{\prime}}{2k^{\prime}+1}<\frac{1}{2}\,,

so
\lfloor qB_{i}r\rceil=k^{\prime}\lfloor q\alpha\rceil
is a multiple of
k^{\prime}
.
∎

D.2
Proof of Lemma
9.2

Lemma
9.2
.

(Generalization of Theorem 2.6 of
[
12
]
)

Let
{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\in[0,1]^{d\times n},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\in[0,1]^{d\times m}
have i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}({p_{R}})
and
\mathsf{Ber}({p_{L}})
entries respectively. Let
\delta_{R},\delta_{L}\in\mathbb{R}
be parameters such that
\delta_{R},\delta_{L}\ll 1
and let
M_{R}^{\mathsf{full}}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{d\times n},M_{L}^{\mathsf{full}}\in\left\{\pm 1\right\}^{d\times m}
be any fixed matrices. Let
\left(X^{G}_{R}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{d\times n}\right)
and
\left(X^{G}_{L}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{d\times m}\right)
.

Define for
a\in\left\{R,L\right\}
:

M_{a}=\delta_{a}M_{a}^{\mathsf{full}}\odot\mathsf{E}_{a}\quad\,,X^{G}_{a}=\left(X^{G}_{a}\right)^{\mathsf{full}}\odot\mathsf{E}_{a}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad X_{a}=M_{a}+X^{G}_{a}\,.

If the following assumptions on parameters
{p_{R}},{p_{L}},\delta_{R},\delta_{L},d,n,m
are satisfied:

\displaystyle{p_{R}},{p_{L}}

\displaystyle\in\left\{1\right\}\cup(0,1-c)\text{ for some }c>0

(a)

\displaystyle\delta_{R}

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{1,\left({p_{R}}n\right)^{-1/2}\right\}\text{ and }\delta_{L}\ll\min\left\{1,\left({p_{L}}m\right)^{-1/2}\right\}

(b)

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle\ll{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}d\text{ and }m\ll{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}d

(c)

\displaystyle mn

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{{p_{R}}{p_{L}}d,\delta_{L}^{-4},\delta_{R}^{-4}\right\}

(d)

\displaystyle n^{2}m

\displaystyle\ll\min\left\{{p_{L}}d,{p_{R}}^{-2}\delta_{R}^{-4}\right\},\text{ and }nm^{2}\ll\min\left\{{p_{R}}d,{p_{L}}^{-2}\delta_{L}^{-4}\right\}\,,

(e)

then

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(N\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}M_{R}^{T}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}X_{L}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad d\to\infty\,.

Proof.

Recall that for
a\in\left\{R,L\right\}
,

X_{a}=M_{a}+X^{G}_{a}\odot\mathsf{E}_{a}\,,

and therefore the Wishart matrix of interest can be expressed as

\displaystyle X_{R}^{\top}X_{L}=\left(M_{R}+X^{G}_{R}\right)^{\top}\left(M_{L}+X^{G}_{L}\right)

\displaystyle=M_{R}^{\top}M_{L}+M_{R}^{\top}X^{G}_{L}+\left(X^{G}_{R}\right)^{\top}M_{L}+\left(X^{G}_{R}\right)^{\top}X^{G}_{L}

\displaystyle=\text{(mean)}+\text{(cross-term 1)}+\text{(cross-term 2)}+\text{(noise)}\,.

We will prove the Lemma statement in two steps: intuitively, bound (
TV1
) removes the first cross-term and proves that the noise terms is Gaussian; bound (
TV2
) removes the second cross-term.

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}X_{L},\mathcal{N}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad d\to\infty\,.

(TV1)

\displaystyle d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}M_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right),\mathcal{N}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad d\to\infty\,.

(TV2)

Then, applying the triangle inequality for total variation distance (Fact
6.1
), the statement of the Lemma follows.

Note that Lemma
9.2
is a generalization of a result of
[
12
]
for bipartite masks.

Proof of (
TV1
).

Denote
\psi={p_{R}}{p_{L}}d
and let

Y=\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}X_{L}\,,

\mu=\mathcal{L}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}X_{L}\right)=\mathcal{L}\left(Y\right),\quad\text{and}\quad\nu=\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)\,.

Our goal for this section is to show that under the conditions of the Lemma,
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu,\nu\right)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.

We will identify a high-probability event
S
on
X_{R}
and
\mathsf{E}_{L}
in the process of the proof. Then, denoting
\mu_{S}
to be
\mu
conditioned on the event
S
by the properties of total variation (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu,\nu\right)\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)+\Pr\left[S^{c}\right]\,,

and thus it is sufficient to show that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.
By Pinsker’s inequality,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)}\,.

Denote
\mu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right),\nu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)
to be the measures
\mu,\nu
after conditioning on the value of
X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\in S
.

Note that the above are product measures by column. For
i\in[m]
let

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)_{i}=\mathcal{L}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}\left(X_{L}\right)_{i}\right),\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)_{i}=\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{i},I_{n}\right)\right)\,.

Note that even after conditioning on
X_{R}
and
\mathsf{E}_{L}
, the columns of both matrices are independent and Gaussian with parameters computed below. Then, applying the convexity and tensorization of
\mathsf{KL}
divergence,

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\in S}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)\big\|\nu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\in S}\sum_{i\in[m]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)_{i}\big\|\nu\left(X_{R},\mathsf{E}_{L}\right)_{i}\right)\,.

(50)

Both
\mu\left(X_{R},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\right)_{i}
and
\nu\left(X_{R},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\right)_{i}
are high-dimensional Gaussians – we now compute their parameters. Denote
\mathsf{E}_{i}
to be the set of nonzero coordinates in the column
\left(X_{L}\right)_{i}
and let
A_{i}
be the vector
\left(M_{L}\right)_{i}
restricted to those coordinates. Moreover, denote
X_{|i}
to be the submatrix of
X_{R}
consisting of the rows in
\mathsf{E}_{i}
. With this notation,

\displaystyle\mu\left(X_{R},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\right)_{i}:

\displaystyle\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}\left(X_{L}\right)_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{|i}^{\top}A_{i},\psi^{-1}X_{|i}^{\top}X_{|i}\right)

\displaystyle\nu\left(X_{R},{\mathsf{E}_{L}}\right)_{i}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{|i}^{\top}A_{i},I_{n}\right)\,.

Denote
\Sigma_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}=\psi^{-1}X_{|i}^{\top}X_{|i}
to be the covariance in
\mu\left(X_{R}\right)_{i}
given
\mathsf{E}_{i}
and let

\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}=\Sigma_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}-I_{n}\quad\,.

Fix some
c\in(0,1)
and define the following event:

S_{op}=\left\{\forall i\,,\,\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|_{op}\leq c\right\}\,.

Claim D.1
.

Event
S_{op}
is high probability, i.e.

\Pr\left[S_{op}^{c}\right]\to 0\quad\text{as }d\to\infty\,.

Claim D.2
.

\forall i\in\left[m\right]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}\in S_{op}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{|i}^{\top}A_{i},\Sigma_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)\big\|\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{|i}^{\top}A_{i},I_{n}\right)\right)=o\left(m^{-1}\right)\,.

By Claim
D.1
, we can set
S=S_{op}
. Then, applying Claim
D.2
to every term of Eq. (
50
), we obtain

\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)\leq o(1)\,,

which concludes the proof of (
TV1
).

Proof of Claim
D.1
.

We will use the following standard bound on the singular values of random matrices.

Proposition D.1

(Prop. 4.6.1 in
[
77
]
)
.

Let
A
be an
m\times n
matrix, where
m\geq n
, whose rows
A_{i}
are independent, mean zero, sub-gaussian isotropic random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Denote by
s_{1}(A)
and
s_{n}(A)
the largest and smallest singular values of
A
. Then for every
t\geq 0
, we have

\sqrt{m}-CK^{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)\leq s_{n}(A)\leq s_{1}(A)\leq\sqrt{m}+CK^{2}(\sqrt{n}+t)

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-t^{2})
. Here
K=\max_{i}\|A_{i}\|_{\psi_{2}}
.

Recall that conditioned on
\mathsf{E}_{i}
,
\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}=\psi^{-1}X_{|i}^{\top}X_{|i}-I
, where we can decompose
X_{|i}=X^{G}_{|i}+\left(M_{R}\right)_{|i}
. In this proof we will denote
\delta=\delta_{R}
. First,

\|\left(M_{R}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}\leq\delta\|\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}\,,

where recall the entries of
\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}
are i.i.d.
\mathsf{Ber}({p_{R}})
. Denote
d^{\prime}\leq d
to be the number of rows in
\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}
. In case
{p_{R}}=1
,
\|\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}\leq\sqrt{nd^{\prime}}
. Otherwise, for the case
{p_{R}}\in\left(0,1-c\right)
, the rows of
\left(\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}-{p_{R}}\right)/\sqrt{{p_{R}}(1-{p_{R}})}
are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{{p_{R}}(1-{p_{R}})}}
-subgaussian isotropic. By Prop.
D.1
, for any
t\geq 0
with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-t^{2}\right)
,

\displaystyle\|\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq{p_{R}}\sqrt{nd^{\prime}}+\sqrt{{p_{R}}(1-{p_{R}})}\left(\sqrt{d^{\prime}}+\frac{C}{{p_{R}}(1-{p_{R}})}(\sqrt{n}+t)\right)

\displaystyle\leq{p_{R}}\sqrt{nd^{\prime}}+O\left(\sqrt{{p_{R}}d^{\prime}}+{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)\,,

(51)

where we set
t=O\left(\log n\right)
. Note that bound (
51
) also trivially holds for
{p_{R}}=1
. We conclude that with high probability

\displaystyle\psi^{-1/2}\|\left(M_{R}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq\psi^{-1/2}\delta\|\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}\sqrt{nd^{\prime}}+O\left(\psi^{-1/2}\delta\sqrt{{p_{R}}d^{\prime}}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)\,.

(52)

Now
X^{G}_{|i}
has entries that are
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
with probability
{p_{R}}
and
0
otherwise. Then,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{{p_{R}}}}X^{G}_{|i}
has isotropic,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{{p_{R}}}}
-subgaussian rows, and therefore, by Prop.
D.1
, for any
t\geq 0
with probability at least
1-2\exp\left(-t^{2}\right)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{{p_{R}}}}s_{i}\left(X^{G}_{|i}\right)\in\left[\sqrt{d^{\prime}}\pm C\frac{1}{{p_{R}}}\left(\sqrt{n}+t\right)\right]\,.

Setting
t=O\left(\log n\right)
, we conclude that with high probability,

\displaystyle s_{i}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X^{G}_{|i}\right)\in\left[\psi^{-1/2}\sqrt{{p_{R}}d^{\prime}}\pm O\left(\psi^{-1/2}{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)\right]\,.

(53)

Recall that
d^{\prime}\sim\mathsf{Bin}(n,{p_{L}})
. We will use the following fact about concentration of binomial random variables.

Proposition
6.1
.

(Concentration of Binomial RV)

Let
X\sim\mathsf{Bin}(n,p)
, where
p>0
and
np\geq n^{t}
for some
t>0
. By Berstein’s inequality,

\Pr\left[|X-pn|\geq t\right]\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t^{2}/2}{np(1-p)+t/3}\right)\,,

and as a consequence, with probability at least
1-n^{-c}
,

X\in\left[pn\pm C\sqrt{pn}\log n\right]=:\left[pn\pm\phi(p,n)\right]\,,

where
C=C(c)
is a universal constant.

By Prop.
6.1
, with high probability at,
d^{\prime}\in\left[d{p_{L}}\pm\tilde{O}\left(\sqrt{{p_{L}}d}\log d\right)\right]
. Substituting this result into (
53
), we obtain

\displaystyle s_{i}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X^{G}_{|i}\right)

\displaystyle\in\left[1\pm\tilde{O}\left(\psi^{-1/2}\sqrt{{p_{R}}}\right)\pm O\left(\psi^{-1/2}{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\in\left[1\pm o(1)\right]\,,

where we used
\psi^{-1/2}\sqrt{{p_{R}}}=\left({p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}\ll n^{-t}
from Cond. (
c
),
\psi^{-1/2}{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}=\sqrt{\frac{n}{{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}d}}\ll 1
from Cond. (
c
).
Substituting the concentration of
d^{\prime}
into (
52
), we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{-1/2}\|\left(M_{R}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}\sqrt{n{p_{L}}d}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta\sqrt{{p_{R}}{p_{L}}d}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\delta\sqrt{{p_{R}}n}+\delta+\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1)\,,

where we used
\delta,\delta\sqrt{{p_{R}}n}\ll 1
from Cond. (
b
),
\psi^{-1/2}\delta{p_{R}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{n}\ll\sqrt{\frac{n}{{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}d}}\ll 1
from Cond. (
b
), (
c
).
Combining the bounds on the singular values of
\psi^{-1/2}X^{G}_{|i}
and on
\psi^{-1/2}\|\left(M_{R}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|_{op}=\|\psi^{-1}X_{|i}^{\top}X_{|i}-I\|_{op}\ll 1\,,

which concludes the proof.
∎

Proof of Claim
D.2
.

We apply the formula of Lemma
6.1
for the
\mathsf{KL}
divergence between Gaussians and the divergence of Claim
D.2
can be expressed as

\displaystyle-\log\det\left(I+\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)+\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{t}}{t}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{t}\right)=\sum_{t=2}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{t}}{t}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{t}\right)\,.

Moreover,
\forall t>2
,
\big|\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{t}\right)\big|\leq\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|^{t-2}_{op}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{2}\right)\leq c^{t-2}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{2}\right)
, since under
S_{op}
we have
\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|_{op}\leq c
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}\in S_{op}}\left[\left(-\log\det\left(I+\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)+\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=O(1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}\in S_{op}}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{2}\right)\,.

Note that since
S_{op}
is a high probability event by Claim
D.1
,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}\in S_{op}}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{2}\right)\leq\Pr\left[S_{op}\right]^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\mathsf{Tr}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}^{2}\right)=O(1)\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|_{2}^{2}\,.

We now explicitly compute
\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\|_{2}^{2}
. Recall that
\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}=\psi^{-1}X_{|i}^{\top}X_{|i}-I
. In this proof, denote
\delta=\delta_{R}
. Recall that every row of
X_{R}
is included in
X_{|i}
independently with probability
{p_{L}}
and the entries
\left(X_{R}\right)_{ab}\sim\mathcal{N}(\pm\delta,1)\cdot\left({\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{ab}
.
For an off diagonal entry, i.e.
a\neq b\in[n]
:

\displaystyle\psi^{2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)_{ab}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\delta^{2}\right)^{2}d{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}+\delta^{4}d(d-1){p_{R}}^{4}{p_{L}}^{2}\,.

For a diagonal entry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\left(\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}}\right)_{aa}^{2}

\displaystyle=\psi^{-2}\left(\delta^{4}+6\delta^{2}+3\right)d{p_{R}}{p_{L}}+1+\psi^{-2}\left(1+\delta^{2}\right)^{2}d(d-1){p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}^{2}-2\psi^{-1}\left(1+\delta^{2}\right)d{p_{R}}{p_{L}}

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\delta^{2}\psi^{-1}+\delta^{4}\right)+3\psi^{-1}+1+1-2

\displaystyle=O\left(\delta^{2}\psi^{-1}+\delta^{4}+\psi^{-1}\right)\,.

Combining these results we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}^{G}}\|\Delta_{\mathsf{E}_{i},X_{R}^{G}}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(n^{2}-n\right)\left(\psi^{-2}\left(1+\delta^{2}\right)^{2}d{p_{R}}^{2}{p_{L}}+\psi^{-2}\delta^{4}d(d-1){p_{R}}^{4}{p_{L}}^{2}\right)+nO\left(\delta^{2}\psi^{-1}+\delta^{4}+\psi^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(n^{2}{p_{R}}\psi^{-1}+\delta^{4}n^{2}{p_{R}}^{2}+\delta^{2}n\psi^{-1}+n\delta^{4}+n\psi^{-1}\right)\ll m^{-1}\,,

where we have used
\delta\ll 1
(Cond. (
b
)),
n^{2}{p_{R}}\psi^{-1}=\frac{n^{2}}{{p_{L}}d}\ll m^{-1}
and
\delta^{4}n^{2}{p_{R}}^{2}\ll m^{-1}
from Cond. (
e
),
\max\left\{\delta^{2}n\psi^{-1},n\delta^{4},n\psi^{-1}\right\}\ll m^{-1}
from Cond. (
d
), (
b
), which concludes the proof.

∎

Proof of (
TV2
).

In this section we show the second step of our argument and prove

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}M_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right),\mathcal{N}\left(\left({p_{R}}{p_{L}}d\right)^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)\to 0\,,\quad d\to\infty\,,

where both measures are mixtures of Gaussians with corresponding distributions for
X_{R},M_{R},M_{L}
.
Again denote
\psi={p_{R}}{p_{L}}d
and let

\mu=\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}X_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)\,,\quad\text{and},\quad\nu=\mathcal{L}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}M_{R}^{\top}M_{L},I_{n\times m}\right)\right)

We will follow the same proof outline as (
TV1
) and identify a high-probability event
S
on
M_{L}
and
{\mathsf{E}_{R}}
in the process of the proof. Then, denoting
\mu_{S}
to be
\mu
conditioned on the event
S
by the properties of total variation (Fact
6.1
),

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu,\nu\right)\leq d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)+\Pr\left[S^{c}\right]\,,

and thus it is sufficient to show that
d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)\to 0
as
d\to\infty
.
By Pinsker’s inequality,

d_{\mathsf{TV}}\left(\mu_{S},\nu\right)\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)}\,.

Denote
\mu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right),\nu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)
to be the measures
\mu,\nu
after conditioning on the value of
M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\in S
. Note that the above are product measures by row. For
i\in[n]
let

\displaystyle\mu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}=\mathcal{L}\left(\psi^{-1/2}\left(X_{R}\right)_{i}^{\top}M_{L}+\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{m}\right)^{\top}\right),\quad

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\nu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}=\mathcal{L}\left(\psi^{-1/2}\left(M_{R}\right)_{i}^{\top}M_{L}+\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{m}\right)^{\top}\right)\,.

Note that even after conditioning on
M_{L}
and
\mathsf{E}_{R}
, the rows of both matrices are independent and Gaussian with parameters computed below. Then, applying the convexity and tensorization of
\mathsf{KL}
divergence,

\displaystyle\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\in S}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\left(M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\right)\big\|\nu\left(M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\in S}\sum_{i\in[m]}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu\left(M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\right)_{i}\big\|\nu\left(M_{L},\mathsf{E}_{R}\right)_{i}\right)\,.

(54)

Both
\mu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}
and
\nu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}
are high-dimensional Gaussians – we now compute their parameters. Denote
\mathsf{E}_{i}
to be the set of nonzero coordinates in the row
\left(X_{R}\right)_{i}
and recall that
\left(X_{R}\right)_{i}=\left(X^{G}_{R}\right)_{i}+\left(M_{R}\right)_{i}
. Let
A_{i}
be the vector
\left(M_{R}\right)_{i}
restricted to the nonzero coordinates
\mathsf{E}_{i}
. Moreover, denote
\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}
to be the submatrix of
M_{L}
consisting of the rows in
\mathsf{E}_{i}
. With this notation,

\displaystyle\mu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}:

\displaystyle\psi^{-1/2}\left(X_{R}\right)_{i}^{\top}M_{L}+\mathcal{N}\left(0,I_{m}\right)^{\top}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}A_{i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i},I_{m}+\psi^{-1}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}\right)

\displaystyle\nu\left(M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\right)_{i}:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}A_{i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i},I_{m}\right)\,.

Denote

\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}=\psi^{-1}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}=I_{m}+\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\,.

Fix some
c\in(0,1)
and define the following event:

S_{op}=\left\{\forall i\,,\,\|\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|_{op}\leq c\right\}\,.

Claim D.3
.

Event
S_{op}
is high probability, i.e.

\Pr\left[S_{op}^{c}\right]\to 0\quad\text{as }d\to\infty\,.

Claim D.4
.

\forall i\in\left[m\right]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}\in S_{op}}\mathsf{KL}\left(\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}A_{i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i},\Sigma_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\right)\big\|\mathcal{N}\left(\psi^{-1/2}A_{i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i},I_{m}\right)\right)=o\left(n^{-1}\right)\,.

By Claim
D.3
, we can set
S=S_{op}
. Then, applying Claim
D.4
to every term of Eq. (
54
), we obtain

\mathsf{KL}\left(\mu_{S}\|\nu\right)\leq o(1)\,,

which concludes the proof of (
TV2
).

Proof of Claim
D.3
.

The claim is equivalent to showing that with high probability (
\ll n^{-1}
),

\|\psi^{-1}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}\leq c\,.

Fully analogous to the proof in Claim
D.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\psi^{-1/2}\|\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}\|_{op}

\displaystyle\leq O\left(\psi^{-1/2}\delta_{L}{p_{L}}\sqrt{m{p_{R}}d}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta_{L}\sqrt{{p_{L}}{p_{R}}d}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta_{L}{p_{L}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{m}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\delta_{L}\sqrt{{p_{L}}m}+\delta_{L}+\psi^{-1/2}\delta_{L}{p_{L}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{m}\right)

\displaystyle=o(1)\,,

where we used
\delta_{L},\delta_{L}\sqrt{{p_{L}}m}\ll 1
from Cond. (
b
),
\psi^{-1/2}\delta_{L}{p_{L}}^{-1/2}\sqrt{m}\ll\sqrt{\frac{m}{{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}d}}\ll 1
from Cond. (
b
), (
c
), which concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Proof of Claim
D.4
.

By the same argument as in Claim
D.2
, it is sufficient to show that

\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|_{2}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|\psi^{-1}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}^{\top}\left(M_{L}\right)_{|i}\|_{2}^{2}\ll n^{-1}\,.

We explicitly compute the expectation. Denote here
\delta=\delta_{L}
.
\forall a\in\left[[\right]m]
,

\displaystyle\psi^{2}\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\left(\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\right)_{aa}^{2}=\delta^{4}{p_{L}}{p_{R}}d+\delta^{4}{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}^{2}d(d-1)=\delta^{4}O\left({p_{L}}{p_{R}}d+{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}^{2}d^{2}\right)\,.

\forall a\neq b\in\left[m\right]
,

\displaystyle\psi^{2}\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\left(\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\right)_{ab}^{2}\leq\delta^{4}{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}d+\delta^{4}{p_{L}}^{4}{p_{R}}^{2}d(d-1)=\delta^{4}O\left({p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}d+{p_{L}}^{4}{p_{R}}^{2}d^{2}\right)\,.

Combining the two bounds together, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|\Delta_{M_{L},{\mathsf{E}_{R}}}\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{4}\psi^{-2}\cdot O\left({p_{L}}{p_{R}}dm+{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}^{2}d^{2}m+{p_{L}}^{2}{p_{R}}dm^{2}+{p_{L}}^{4}{p_{R}}^{2}d^{2}m^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta^{4}O\left(\psi^{-1}m+m+{p_{L}}\psi^{-1}m^{2}+{p_{L}}^{2}m^{2}\right)\ll n^{-1}\,,

where we used
\delta^{4}\psi^{-1}m,\delta^{4}m\ll n^{-1}
by Cond. (
d
) and
\delta^{4}{p_{L}}\psi^{-1}m^{2}=\frac{\delta^{4}m^{2}}{{p_{R}}d}\ll n^{-1}
,
\delta^{4}{p_{L}}^{2}m^{2}\ll n^{-1}
by Cond. (
e
).
∎

∎
